Hand-coded MAN command: skips leading spaces in the arg tail, handles empty/-h as a usage message, builds "/man/NAME" in a path buffer, FRESOLVEs + FOPENs it, and streams the file byte-by-byte to stdout; prints "no manual entry for NAME" if the page is missing. It is the asm twin of os/commands/man.c.

Correct, clean, and faithful to the C twin — argument parsing, the "/man/" prefix build, the not-found reprint of path+5, and both carry branches (JC after FOPEN and FGETB) all match the documented ABI and the C reference exactly, with no inverted-carry bugs, pointer clobbers, or stack imbalance. The main weakness is efficiency: it ignores the STA (P1)+ post-increment idiom that the rest of the commands-asm tree uses everywhere, spending a separate INP1 after every store, and it loads message pointers with a 4-instruction TAP1L/TAP1H dance in places where the same file already uses the 1-instruction LDP1 #label form. Documentation is a genuine strength — the header block and inline comments are thorough and accurate.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · efficiency · m_build (lines 45-60) and mb_l (lines 71-73) — The prefix build writes '/man/' as five (STA (P1)) + (INP1) pairs, and the copy loop does STA (P1)/INP1/INP2 separately. The ISA and the rest of this codebase (cp.asm, diff.asm, dir.asm, cmp.asm all use STA (P1)+) support post-increment stores, so every explicit INP1 here is a wasted instruction. → Replace each 'STA (P1) / INP1' pair with 'STA (P1)+'. That removes the five INP1s in the prefix build and one INP1 in the copy loop (~6 instructions / bytes), and matches the established house idiom.
- _low_ · clarity · m_nf (lines 90-93) and m_usage (lines 113-116) — Message pointers are loaded with LDA #<lbl / TAP1L / LDA #>lbl / TAP1H (4 instructions), yet the same file loads the path pointer with the 1-instruction LDP1 #path (lines 45,77,96). This is an internal inconsistency and costs extra bytes on each error/usage path. → Use LDP1 #m_msg / LDP1 #m_use (keeping the following LDA #0 that SYS_PUTS expects, per the codebase convention). Saves 2 instructions per site and reads consistently with the path loads. Note: the TAP1L/H form is the wider house style for SYS_PUTS, so this is a consistency/size nit, not a bug.
- _low_ · safety · m_build / mb_l copy loop (lines 61-76) — No bounds check while copying the name into path (.fill 80). '/man/' consumes 5 bytes and the NUL 1, leaving 74 for the name; a longer argument overflows into m_arg (line 126) and beyond. Unverified in practice since command names are short, and the C twin shares the same limitation. → Optional: cap the copy at 74 chars, or document the limit. Low priority given real inputs, but worth a one-line comment noting the 80-byte cap.
- _low_ · clarity · m_save / dash handling (lines 18-43) — m_arg is saved solely so P2 can be rewound after the '-' probe destructively advances it with INP2 (line 32). This is more machinery than the logic needs — the second char could be peeked without moving P2. → Consider saving the second-char check off a temporary or a copied pointer so the primary P2 never has to be rewound, eliminating the m_arg global. Purely a readability/register-economy cleanup; current code is functionally correct.

**Docs:** Excellent. The 7-line header states purpose, the "cat with /man/ prefix" model, the CWD-independence rationale, the entry convention (P2 = arg tail), and every BIOS/OS address used. Section banners divide parse/build/stream/not-found/usage. Inline comments are accurate and verified against behavior: the "skip 5-char /man/ prefix" note matches the five INP1s, the carry-flag comments (C=1 not found / C=1 EOF) match the JC targets, and the "clobbers P1/P2" notes on FOPEN/FGETB match lib_abi.inc. Only nit: nothing documents the 80-byte path cap or that names >74 chars would overflow, and the m_arg/rewind trick could use one more word of explanation of *why* P2 was advanced first.

**Strengths:** Both carry branches are correct: JC m_nf after FOPEN (C=1 = not found) and JC mo_d after FGETB (C=1 = EOF) match lib_abi.inc and the C twin exactly — no inverted-carry bug, which is the common failure mode here.; Behavior matches os/commands/man.c precisely, including the subtle case of a leading '-' that is not -h/-H being treated as a literal name ('/man/-x'), achieved via the m_arg rewind.; Reprinting the requested name from path+5 in the not-found path is safe and verified: FRESOLVE resolves into DIRLBA/FNAME (not in place), so the path buffer is intact — the C twin relies on the same guarantee.; Stack is balanced (no pushes; every exit is a clean RTS) and P1/P2 are reloaded (LDP1 #$FC00 before FOPEN) after the FRESOLVE/FOPEN/FGETB clobbers, exactly where the ABI requires.; Documentation is thorough and accurate — a model header block plus correct inline comments.

**Top improvements:** Adopt STA (P1)+ post-increment for the prefix build and copy loop to match the rest of commands-asm and shave ~6 instructions.; Load error/usage message pointers with LDP1 #label instead of the 4-instruction TAP1L/TAP1H sequence, consistent with the path loads already in this file.; Add a one-line note (or an actual guard) about the 80-byte path buffer cap so the unbounded name copy is documented.


### `os/commands-asm/more.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded MORE command: pages a named file (or stdin) to the console 23 lines at a time, reading the paging key from CONIN, with a -h/-H usage flag. ASM counterpart of os/commands/more.c.

A genuinely solid, correct piece of hand assembly. Every multi-byte carry branch (JC/JNC after ADD/CMP) is oriented correctly, the 16-bit line counter and the >=23 page test are right, and the pointer/register discipline across SYS_PUTS/SYS_PUTC/CONIN calls holds up. The critiques are almost entirely efficiency, clarity, and one minor argument-parsing semantic — not correctness. Documentation is well above average for hand asm: an accurate file header plus per-routine and per-branch comments that match the code. Main opportunities are trimming verbose/redundant sequences and factoring the duplicated message epilogue.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · m_chk (lines 36-47) / m_open (line 50) — When the arg begins with '-' but the second char is not h/H, control falls through to m_open, which loads the filename pointer from m_arg — still pointing at the '-'. So `more -x foo` is silently treated as opening a file literally named '-x' rather than erroring or showing usage. Whether this matches more.c is unverified. → Confirm against os/commands/more.c. If unknown flags should be rejected, branch unknown '-' cases to m_usage; if they should be ignored, advance m_arg past the flag before m_open. If the current fall-through is intentional, add a comment saying so.
- _low_ · efficiency · m_sk (lines 18-34) — The space-skip loop reloads P2 from m_arg every pass and does a full 16-bit ADD/carry-propagate to advance one byte, instead of using INP2 to walk the pointer and computing m_arg once at the end. This is correct but noticeably more code/cycles than needed. → Walk with INP2 in the loop (LDA (P2) is unaffected), and after the skip write P2 back to m_arg via TPA2L/TPA2H. Removes the per-pass reload and the 16-bit increment entirely.
- _low_ · efficiency · m_page (lines 93, 97) — After JSR prompt, A already holds mkey, and per the ISA CMP preserves A, so the `LDA mkey` reloads before the 'Q' and 13 comparisons are redundant. → Drop the two `LDA mkey` reloads (keep just the CMP against each of 'q','Q',13). Purely cosmetic — behavior is already correct.
- _low_ · clarity · prompt (lines 139-141) — P1 is zeroed (LDA #0/TAP1L/TAP1H) immediately before JSR CONIN with no comment. It is unclear whether CONIN consumes P1 or this is leftover/dead setup. Unverified against the CONIN ABI. → If CONIN ignores P1, delete the three instructions; otherwise add a one-line comment stating what CONIN expects in P1.
- _low_ · clarity · m_nf (113-121) and m_usage (122-130) — The two message routines are identical except for the string pointer: load P1, LDA #0, JSR SYS_PUTS, LDA #10, JSR SYS_PUTC, RTS. Duplicated epilogue. → Factor a `putln` helper taking P1 = string, printing the string plus a newline and returning; call it from both sites. Saves bytes and centralizes the newline behavior.
- _low_ · clarity · m_lc / m_full / Enter branch (lines 84, 101, 106) — Page height is hard-coded as literals 23 (threshold) and 22 (Enter preset). They are commented but not named, so the screen-height assumption is spread across three sites. → Define an equate (e.g. PAGE = 23) and use PAGE and PAGE-1, so the terminal height lives in one place.

**Docs:** Strong. File header states purpose, usage, the stdin-vs-CONIN split, and the P2 entry contract. Branch-level comments are accurate: the ">=23 (hi!=0 or lo>=23)" note (line 79), the "preset to 22 so the 23rd line prompts" note (line 101), and the "SYS_PUTS/SYS_PUTC clobber P1/P2" note (line 112) all match the code. The erase string is correctly sized to the 8-char "--More--". Only real gap: the LDA #0 / TAP1L / TAP1H that zeroes P1 before CONIN (lines 139-141) is undocumented — unclear whether CONIN requires it or it is dead setup. Magic page-height constants (23/22) are commented but not named.

**Strengths:** All multi-byte carry handling is correct: JNC after ADD to skip hi-byte propagation (lines 30, 75) and JNC after CMP #23 for the lines<23 continue (line 86) are all oriented the right way — the common inverted-carry bug class is absent.; The 16-bit line counter and the >=23 page test (check hi!=0 first, then lo>=23) are logically complete and correctly handle the impossible-but-safe overflow case.; Register/pointer discipline is sound: mch/mkey/lines/m_arg are held in static memory across SYS_PUTS/SYS_PUTC/CONIN/nextc calls that clobber P1/P2 and A, and A is reloaded after each clobbering call before it is used.; The erase string is precisely sized (CR + 8 spaces + CR) to blank the 8-character "--More--" prompt — a real attention-to-detail touch.; Documentation quality is high and accurate for hand-written asm: file header, entry contract, and branch-level comments that actually match the code.

**Top improvements:** Verify the '-x' unknown-flag fall-through against os/commands/more.c and either reject unknown flags or advance m_arg past them (line 39-47 / 50).; Simplify the space-skip loop to walk P2 with INP2 and compute m_arg once at the end, dropping the per-pass reload and 16-bit increment (lines 18-34).; Factor the duplicated message-plus-newline epilogue in m_nf/m_usage into a shared putln helper.; Name the page-height constant (PAGE=23) instead of scattering the 23/22 literals across three branches.


### `os/commands-asm/mv.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded MV command: moves/renames a file via copy-then-delete (P8XFS has no rename), with glob support that expands a wildcard source and moves each match into a destination directory. It is the ASM counterpart of os/commands/mv.c.

This is a careful, faithful, and correct port of mv.c: I diffed it against the C reference and the control flow, 16-bit pointer carry-chaining, and flag-branch directions all match the intended semantics. Every carry/borrow branch I traced (mv_sk, m_arg+=mpw, cg_ptr, cg_base, mg_l, FOPEN/FGETB/FOPENDIR carry tests) is correct, and the stack is balanced (all JSR routines end in RTS, no PHA/PLA imbalance). The weaknesses are inherited-from-C rather than introduced: no buffer bounds checking on the fixed 80-byte path buffers, and no same-file guard on the glob path (a copy-onto-self data-loss edge). Documentation is a genuine standout — a real file header, per-routine contracts, and accurate carry/flag inline comments.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · mv_glob / mg_l loop (lines 196-262) — The glob path has no same-file guard (streq is only applied in the single-source path at lines 170-181). A command like `MV *.TMP .` resolves each match's src to /cwd/NAME and joinp builds jdst = /cwd/NAME — the identical file. move_one then FOPENs src, FWOPEN truncates the same file to empty, the copy loop reads nothing, and FDELETE removes it: silent data loss. This flaw is also present in mv.c, so it is a shared design gap, not an introduced regression. → Add a streq(m_src, m_jdst) check inside the glob loop and skip (or warn) when they are equal, mirroring the single-source guard, so a match already living in the destination directory is not destroyed.
- _medium_ · safety · mpw_l (79-104), abspath ab_copy (530-545), joinp (357-406), SYS_GETCWD path in abspath — No bounds checking on any fixed buffer. patw/m_src/m_dst/m_jdst are 80 bytes; the source-word copy, abspath output, GETCWD result, and joinp concatenation (dst up to 80 + '/' + basename up to 64) can all overrun. Since these buffers are laid out contiguously at the end of the file (patw, m_src, m_dst, m_jdst, gfiles), an overflow silently corrupts the adjacent buffer. Matches the C version's lack of checks. → Cap the copy loops against a max length (e.g. compare mpw / write index to 79 and stop or error). At minimum, joinp should guard i against the m_jdst size before writing.
- _low_ · correctness · move_one mo_cp, first FGETB (lines 332-333) — The C reference calls FGETB as bios(FGETB,0,0), i.e. with P1=0, on every iteration. The ASM zeroes P1 before FPUTB (lines 336-338, correct) but does NOT zero P1 before FGETB, so the first FGETB runs with P1 still holding the dst path pointer from FWOPEN (subsequent iterations happen to have P1=0 as a side effect of the prior FPUTB). This is harmless only if FGETB ignores P1 and reads via the file handle — which is almost certainly true, but the asymmetry with both the C source and the FPUTB path is a latent hazard. UNVERIFIED against microcode. → For parity with mv.c and FPUTB, set P1=0 before the FGETB call as well; it is one extra LDA/TAP1L/TAP1H and removes the question.
- _low_ · clarity · abspath (ap_n, lines 487-488, 542-544) — ap_n is initialized and incremented for every copied char but never read by any caller (the source-word advance uses mpw, and the dst word is the last token). It is dead computation and dead storage. → Remove ap_n and its increment, or document why it is retained (e.g. intended future use / parity with a lib signature).
- _low_ · clarity · mpw_d empty-word check (lines 105-110) — The `mpw==0 -> mv_usage2` branch is effectively unreachable: mv_sk has already skipped all leading spaces and mv_chk has already rejected NUL/CR, so the first char of the source word is always a real char and mpw>=1 by the time mpw_d is reached. It mirrors an equally-defensive check in mv.c. → Keep it if defensiveness is intended, but a one-line comment noting it is a belt-and-suspenders guard (unreachable given the preceding skip/reject) would prevent a future reader from puzzling over it.
- _low_ · efficiency · cg_ptr (lines 411-446) — mgi*64 is computed by adding 64 in a loop mgi times, making per-element indexing O(mgi) and the whole glob loop O(n^2). With n<=24 this is negligible in practice. → Either shift: load mgi, SHL six times (x64) for a constant-cost multiply, or maintain a running slot pointer in the mg_l loop that is bumped by 64 each iteration instead of recomputed from mgi.
- _low_ · efficiency · pervasive (e.g. lines 44-46, 79-81, 368-370) — Zero-tests are consistently done as `LDB #0; CMP` after a load. If the P8X LDA sets the Z flag, the LDB/CMP pair is redundant and a bare JZ/JNZ after LDA would save two instructions in many hot loops. UNVERIFIED: the author's uniform use of explicit CMP suggests loads do NOT affect flags on this ISA, in which case the current code is necessary. → Confirm against the firmware/microcode header whether LDA/LDA(Pn) update Z. If it does, drop the redundant LDB #0/CMP in the byte-scan loops; if it does not, add a one-line note in the file header so the pattern reads as deliberate.

**Docs:** Excellent. There is a full file header (invocation forms, algorithm, BIOS/OS address map, entry convention), and every non-trivial routine (move_one, joinp, cg_ptr, cg_base, abspath, streq) has a header stating inputs/outputs/clobbers. Inline comments accurately explain the subtle parts: the low->high carry propagation on pointer bumps, the "CMP sets C when mgi>=ge_cnt so JC exits" loop guard (verified correct), and the FGETB EOF-via-carry contract. Comments match the code — I found no misleading ones. Minor gaps: the move_one header lists clobbers as "P1/P2, m_ch" but doesn't mention it also depends on FRESOLVE's hidden global state persisting into FOPEN/FWOPEN/FDELETE (the C version documents this "redo FRESOLVE before each" rationale; the ASM omits it); and ap_n's purpose is undocumented (it is dead here).

**Strengths:** Verified faithful to os/commands/mv.c — parse logic, glob branch, move_one copy-then-delete ordering (FRESOLVE-before-FWOPEN, single FCLOSE to commit, FRESOLVE-again-before-FDELETE) all match the C reference exactly.; All 16-bit pointer arithmetic correctly chains the low->high carry by hand (mv_sk, m_arg+=mpw, cg_ptr, cg_base), which is exactly the bug class this ISA invites — and it is done right everywhere.; Every carry/flag branch direction is correct: FOPEN JC=not-found, FGETB JC=EOF, FOPENDIR JC=not-dir, and the mg_l `CMP; JC` loop-exit on mgi>=ge_cnt.; Outstanding documentation: file header with BIOS/OS address map and entry convention, per-routine input/output/clobber contracts, and accurate inline explanations of the non-obvious carry and flag logic.; Clean structure: small single-purpose subroutines (move_one, joinp, cg_ptr, cg_base, abspath, streq), consistent naming prefixes per routine, and a well-organized data section with commented buffer sizes that match the ge_max=24 / stride-64 assumptions.

**Top improvements:** Add a same-file guard (streq) inside the glob loop so `MV *.TMP .` (or any glob whose match already sits in the destination dir) cannot copy-onto-self and then delete, destroying the file — currently only the single-source path is protected.; Introduce bounds checks on the 80-byte path buffers (source-word copy, abspath output, GETCWD append, joinp concatenation) so a long path corrupts nothing rather than silently overrunning into the adjacent contiguous buffer.; Zero P1 before the first FGETB for parity with FPUTB and with mv.c, eliminating the one call that runs with a stale dst-path pointer in P1.; Remove the dead ap_n bookkeeping in abspath (or document its intended use).


### `os/commands-asm/pwd.asm`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded PWD /BIN command: parses the argument tail for -h/-H (prints a usage string), otherwise fetches the current working directory via SYS_GETCWD and prints it followed by a newline.

This is a small, straightforward command and it is essentially correct. Control flow is simple (only Z-based branches and JSR/RTS — no multi-byte arithmetic, so the whole inverted-carry-branch bug class the ISA is prone to simply does not apply here). The two genuinely tricky ABI points — syscalls clobbering P1/P2, and there being no pointer-to-pointer move — are both identified and handled correctly, and the documentation is unusually good. The only issues are minor: a redundant re-load in the option check and an unverifiable fixed buffer size.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · efficiency · line 42 (_chk, after the 'h' test) — After the 'h' comparison at lines 38-41, A still holds the option letter (the header notes CMP preserves A, and the intervening LDB does not touch A). Line 42 'LDA (P1)' re-reads the exact same byte before the 'H' comparison, so it is a redundant load — P1 was not advanced between the two checks. → Delete line 42; fall straight from the 'h' JZ into 'LDB #"H" / CMP / JZ _usage'. Saves a load with no behavioral change.
- _low_ · correctness · lines 74-76 (_buf .fill 52) — Fixed 52-byte destination for SYS_GETCWD. If the OS can ever return a path (including separators and NUL) longer than 52 bytes, GETCWD overruns _buf into whatever follows it in memory. _buf is the last symbol in the file, so the overflow lands in adjacent RAM. Whether 52 is actually sufficient depends on the OS's max-path guarantee, which I could not verify from this file. → Cross-check 52 against the OS's authoritative max-path constant (e.g. from the abi include) and, if one exists, size _buf from it or add a static assert/comment citing it, rather than a bare numeric claim. Unverified — flagging as a robustness risk, not a confirmed bug.
- _low_ · efficiency · _skip loop (lines 27-32) — The space-skip loop loads the current byte twice per space: 'LDA (P1)' to test, then 'LDA (P1)+' to consume/advance. This is a deliberate peek-then-consume and is correct, but costs an extra load on every skipped space. → Acceptable as-is given the need to peek before advancing; only worth restructuring if this path is ever hot (it is not for a shell command). Noting for completeness, not action.

**Docs:** Strong. A complete file header states purpose, the C counterpart, the ABI (entry point, P2 = arg tail), each syscall's number and register convention, and the exact build command. Inline comments are accurate and target the non-obvious spots: the byte-wise P2->P1 copy and *why* it's needed, the CMP flag semantics (Z on equal), and the mandatory P1 reload before each syscall because GETCWD may trash it. Magic numbers (32=space, 10='\n', 52=buffer) are all annotated. The only soft spot is the line-74 claim that 52 bytes "holds the longest path the OS can return" — asserted, not cross-referenced to an OS MAXPATH constant, so it can silently drift.

**Strengths:** Correctly handles the syscall-clobbers-P1/P2 hazard: P1 is explicitly reloaded with &_buf before SYS_PUTS (lines 55-58) rather than assuming the GETCWD setup survived, with a comment explaining why.; Byte-wise P2->P1 copy (lines 22-25) correctly works around the absence of a pointer-to-pointer move, and the comment says so.; Contains no multi-byte arithmetic and only Z-flag branches (JZ/JNZ), so it sidesteps the inverted JC/JNC carry-chain bug class entirely — the right instinct for this kind of parse-and-print command.; Exemplary header documentation: ABI, syscall register conventions, and the exact build invocation are all recorded.; Argument parsing degrades gracefully on every edge case traced (empty tail, lone '-', '-' followed by NUL or a non-h/H letter all correctly fall through to printing the CWD).

**Top improvements:** Remove the redundant 'LDA (P1)' at line 42.; Tie the _buf size (52) to an authoritative OS max-path constant instead of a hard-coded literal, to prevent silent overflow if the OS path limit grows.; Optionally verify against os/commands/pwd.c that -h/-H usage text and behavior match byte-for-byte, per the commands-asm dual-implementation convention.


### `os/commands-asm/sed.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded /BIN `sed` command: parses `s/re/new/[g] [file]`, then for each input line runs the shared regex engine to substitute the first (or, with /g, every) match span with a literal replacement and prints the rewritten line.

This is a well-structured, carefully bounded piece of hand assembly. The argument parser, per-line scan/replace loop, regex-at-position wrapper, and readline are cleanly separated, and every buffer write is guarded against overflow. I found no confirmed correctness bugs: the multi-byte carry chains, buffer caps, EOF handling, and the zero-length-match guard all check out. The main criticisms are a genuinely dead no-op block at entry, heavy repetition of an inline 16-bit base+index address idiom that could be a subroutine, and a pervasive but undocumented reliance on LDA/TAPnL preserving the carry flag.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · lines 14-21 (entry) — The entry block copies P2 into s_sav and immediately copies it straight back into P2 with no intervening use — a genuine no-op costing ~8 instructions. The comment itself admits 'no net effect'. → Delete lines 14-21 entirely and start at sed_sk. If it was meant to normalize/validate P2, either do that explicitly or drop it; keeping dead code that the comment flags as useless invites confusion on the next edit.
- _low_ · isa-misuse · address-index idiom (lines 204-209, 236-242, 254-260, 287-293, 297-303, 314-320, 375-382, and re_at/readline) — The pervasive base+index idiom does `ADD` (sets C), then `TAPnL`, then `LDA #>base`, then `JNC`. Correctness depends on both TAPnL and LDA preserving the carry flag across two instructions before the branch. The ISA header documents CMP preserving A and the shifts rotating through C, but does not state that LDA/TAPnL leave C untouched. UNVERIFIED — I could not confirm the microcode flag behavior from this file. The code is internally consistent (the anchored-^ path at 111-113 uses only STA between ADD and JNC, the common index paths use LDA), which strongly implies both preserve C and the code works; I am not asserting a bug. → Confirm in docs/p8x-system-design.md that LDA and TAPnL preserve C, and add a one-line note in the header (e.g. 'index math relies on LDA/TAPnL preserving carry from ADD'). If either instruction does touch C, every high-byte carry here is wrong and this becomes a high-severity bug.
- _low_ · correctness · sl_re / re_at (lines 226-232, 391-398) — A regex that can match the empty string (e.g. `a*` where no `a` is present) returns rend==lp, so re_at yields length 0, which sl_re treats as 'no match' (JZ sl_lit). Result: empty matches never trigger a replacement. This is the correct choice for avoiding an infinite loop in /g mode (si would never advance), but it is a real behavioral limitation, not just a corner case. → No code change needed if this matches os/commands/sed.c semantics — but verify the C version behaves identically and add a comment at re_at noting that zero-length matches are deliberately reported as no-match to prevent a non-advancing /g loop.
- _low_ · efficiency · repeated 16-bit &buf[idx] computation (10+ sites) — The same ~7-instruction sequence to compute base+index with carry into the high byte is inlined at more than ten sites, inflating code size (the memory notes flag code SIZE, not symbol count, as the next TPA ceiling for on-target builds). → Factor into a small helper, e.g. a routine taking base in T/immediate and index in A returning the pointer in P1, or at least a macro. Weigh against per-call cycle cost; for a line-oriented tool the size win likely outweighs the JSR overhead.
- _low_ · clarity · buffer caps 255 vs .fill 260 (lines 248, 283, 422, 480-481) — line/out are 260 bytes but writes are capped at index 255, silently truncating lines/output longer than 255 chars while leaving 4 unused bytes. The cap is safe, just not self-explanatory and slightly wasteful of the declared size. → Either size the buffers to 256 to match the cap, or add a comment that 255 is the hard content limit and the extra bytes are slack; keep the two caps as named constants so they can't drift apart.

**Docs:** Strong documentation. Clear file header stating syntax, shared-lib dependencies, and per-line strategy; a register/variable legend before the substitution loop; and a fully commented scratch/buffer table at the end. Inline comments are accurate against the code (e.g. the "cap at 63" caps, the 16-bit carry notes, re_at's anchoring rule, readline's EOF semantics all match). Notably honest: the entry block's comment openly admits it is a no-op round-trip. Minor gap: nothing documents the load-bearing assumption that LDA/TAPnL preserve C between ADD and JNC, which the whole address-math idiom depends on.

**Strengths:** Every buffer write is bounded before it happens (pat/rep capped at 63, out at 255, line at 255), so no overflow path exists despite raw pointer stores.; Multi-byte address arithmetic consistently chains carry into the high byte (ADD/JNC/INC), and the re_at length subtraction correctly relies on modular low-byte arithmetic since match length < 256.; EOF and CRLF handling in readline is correct: a final line without a trailing newline is still processed, and the subsequent call cleanly returns 0 with no spurious empty line.; Zero-length regex matches are handled so /g mode always makes forward progress — no infinite loop.; Documentation is unusually good for hand assembly: accurate inline comments, a variable legend, a fully annotated scratch table, and honest notes (including flagging its own no-op).

**Top improvements:** Remove the dead no-op P2 round-trip at entry (lines 14-21).; Document (and verify against the design doc) the assumption that LDA/TAPnL preserve carry, since the whole address-math idiom depends on it.; Factor the repeated 16-bit base+index address computation into a helper/macro to cut code size ahead of the TPA size ceiling.; Confirm empty-match substitution behavior matches os/commands/sed.c and note it at re_at.


### `os/commands-asm/sort.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  ASM (P8X)

Hand-coded SORT command: reads up to 128 lines x 79 chars from a file or stdin into a flat 10240-byte buffer, selection-sorts the slot contents by NUL-terminated unsigned byte compare, and prints them ascending.

This is careful, correct, and unusually well-documented assembly. I traced every loop and every carry/borrow branch and found no correctness bug: all JC/JNC decisions after CMP match the "C=1 when A>=B" semantics, 16-bit arithmetic in laddr chains carry manually and correctly, pointer (P1/P2) lifetimes are safe across nextc/openarg/laddr calls, and JSR/RTS are balanced. The weaknesses are all efficiency: laddr multiplies by repeated 16-bit addition instead of shifts, the sort swaps entire 80-byte slot contents rather than an index array, and the swap loop recomputes both addresses via laddr on every single byte. None of these are bugs, but on a TTL CPU they make the hot path dramatically slower than it needs to be. Documentation is a genuine strength.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · efficiency · laddr (274-321) — Slot base = lines + row*80 is computed by adding 80 in a loop that runs `row` times (up to 127 iterations of a 16-bit add). laddr is the hottest routine in the file — called twice per byte in the swap loop and twice per char in lless — so this repeated-addition multiply dominates total run time on the TTL CPU. → row*80 = (row<<6)+(row<<4). Compute two shifted 16-bit copies of row using SHL (which the ISA has) and add them, replacing the O(row) loop with a fixed handful of shifts/adds. This alone gives a large speedup across sort and print.
- _medium_ · efficiency · so_swap / sw_l (162-217) — The 80-byte slot swap calls laddr twice for every column (160 laddr calls per swapped pair), even though the two base addresses are constant across the k-loop and the bytes are contiguous. → Compute &slot[si][0] and &slot[smin][0] once before the loop, hold them in ai/am, and walk with INP1 (or a second cached pointer) incrementing by 1 each iteration instead of re-deriving via la_s/la_c/laddr. Combined with an index-array sort (below) this becomes negligible.
- _medium_ · efficiency · selection sort so_swap (161-221) — Sorting physically copies 80-byte slot contents on every swap. Selection sort does up to nline-1 swaps, each moving 80 bytes through the expensive laddr path — O(n^2) comparisons plus O(n*80) byte copies with per-byte address recomputation. → Sort an array of 1-byte slot indices instead: lless already takes row indices, so compare via the index array and swap only the two index bytes. Print then walks slots through the permuted index array. This removes the byte-copy swap entirely. (Note: if the C counterpart also does content swaps, keep them byte-identical per project convention, but this is the higher-value fix.)
- _low_ · efficiency · lless (343-352) — After `CMP` for the equal test (JZ ll_eq), the differ path reloads ll_a/ll_b and does a second identical CMP before JC ll_no. CMP preserves A and the carry from the first CMP already encodes a>=b, so the second compare is redundant. → Fall straight through from the first CMP: after `JZ ll_eq`, the carry is still valid, so `JC ll_no` / `LDA #1 / RTS` can follow without re-loading and re-comparing. Saves 4 instructions per mismatched byte.
- _low_ · correctness · s_chk / s_open (41-58) — A leading-'-' token that is not -h/-H (e.g. "-x") falls through to s_open with s_arg still pointing at the '-', so it is passed to openarg as a filename and reported as 'sort: not found' rather than a bad-option/usage message. Behavior is defensible but silently conflates 'unknown option' with 'file not found'. → Optional: on a leading '-' that is not h/H, branch to s_usage (or a distinct 'unknown option' message) instead of trying to open it as a file. Confirm this matches sort.c's behavior before changing, to keep the two implementations in step.
- _low_ · docs · line 11 — Header states openarg clobbers P1/P2, but lib_stdin.inc documents openarg as clobbering A/B/P2 only (it does not touch P1). Unverified whether nextc touches P1; the lumped statement is over-broad. → Tighten the clobber note to match the include (openarg: A/B/P2). Harmless as-is since it only over-warns, but accuracy aids future callers.

**Docs:** Excellent. The file header explains the buffer layout, the 128/79 caps and why 8-bit indices suffice, and per-routine clobber lists. laddr and lless have proper In/Out/clobber headers, and the BSS block documents every variable. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (e.g. line 96 "col>=79 -> drop char (leave room for NUL)", line 350 "C=1 means a >= b -> not less"). One minor inaccuracy: line 11 says "nextc/openarg... clobber P1/P2" but lib_stdin.inc documents openarg as clobbering only A/B/P2 (over-conservative, harmless). Nothing misleading.

**Strengths:** No correctness bugs found across a fairly intricate read/sort/swap/print pipeline: every JC/JNC after CMP matches the A>=B carry semantics and all loop bounds are correct.; Manual 16-bit arithmetic in laddr correctly captures and chains the low-byte carry (la_car) into the high byte for both the column add and the base-address add.; Pointer discipline is safe: P1/P2 are always reloaded from memory (s_arg, ai, am, la_*) after any call that could clobber them, so there are no stale-pointer hazards across nextc/openarg/laddr.; Edge cases handled: empty input (0 lines), single line, missing trailing newline at EOF, buffer-full partial-line discard, CR dropping, and the col>=79 cap that reserves the last byte for the NUL.; Outstanding documentation — file header, per-routine In/Out/clobber contracts, accurate inline comments, and a fully annotated BSS block.

**Top improvements:** Replace laddr's repeated-addition *80 with shift-based multiply ((row<<6)+(row<<4)) using SHL — the single biggest speedup since laddr is the hottest routine.; Sort an array of 1-byte slot indices instead of copying 80-byte slot contents on every swap; this removes the entire byte-swap path.; In the swap loop, compute the two slot base addresses once and walk with INP1 rather than calling laddr twice per byte.; Drop the redundant second CMP in lless by reusing the carry from the equality compare.


### `os/commands-asm/tail.asm`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded TAIL command: prints the last N lines (default 10, clamped 1..40) of a named file or stdin, using a 40-slot x 256-byte ring of NUL-terminated line buffers that is replayed oldest-first at EOF.

This is a genuinely solid piece of hand assembly. I traced every carry/borrow branch, the multi-byte arithmetic (mul10n, the digit accumulator, total++), the ring-advance/wrap math, and the base/count selection at replay time, and found no correctness bugs. The two constructs most likely to hide a bug — holding P1 across SYS_PUTC in the print loop, and not re-setting fromfile after openarg — are both provably safe once you check the ABI (OUTCH preserves P1/P2; openarg sets fromfile itself). The remaining issues are maintainability and micro-efficiency, not defects: the line-commit logic is duplicated between t_nl and t_feof, and there is a lot of repeated P2-reload boilerplate. Documentation is excellent and, importantly, accurate.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · clarity · t_nl (168-194) vs t_feof (197-224) — The 'close current line (write NUL) + advance slot with (slot+1)%n wrap + increment 16-bit total' sequence is duplicated almost verbatim in two places. A future change to ring-advance or the terminator must be made in both, and the two copies can silently drift. → Factor the shared tail into a subroutine, e.g. commit_line (write NUL via tbuf_addr, bump+wrap slot, 16-bit total++), and JSR it from both t_nl (then JMP t_fl) and t_feof (then fall to t_print). Saves ~25 lines and removes the drift risk.
- _low_ · clarity · t_sk/t_chk/t_dd/t_dsk/t_d1 and openarg-call sites — The 4-instruction idiom 'LDA t_arg / TAP2L / LDA t_arg+1 / TAP2H' (reload P2 from the 16-bit t_arg) is repeated ~7 times. It is correct but it dominates the argument-parsing code and obscures the actual logic; it exists only because t_ainc deliberately does not touch P2. → Add a tiny helper (t_syncp2: reload P2 from t_arg; RTS) and JSR it, or have t_ainc optionally leave P2 in sync. Reduces boilerplate and makes the parse loops easier to read.
- _low_ · efficiency · t_dl (line 68), t_fl (line 149), t_clamp (line 114) — Several LDA reloads are redundant because CMP preserves A (per the ISA). Line 68 'LDA (P2)' re-reads the char that is already in A after the CMP #48 at line 66 (the intervening JNC does not alter A). Line 149 'LDA tch' reloads a value still live in A after the CMP #10 at 147. Line 114 'LDA n' reloads n still in A after CMP #0 at 112. These waste a few bytes/cycles each. → Drop the redundant loads where A is provably unchanged since the last CMP. (Leave the reload at line 73, which IS needed because JSR mul10n clobbers A.) Minor; some may prefer to keep them for defensiveness, so this is optional.
- _low_ · correctness · t_dl / mul10n (decimal parse) — UNVERIFIED-as-defect / behavioral note: a very large -N (e.g. -70000) overflows the 16-bit n silently. Most such values still clamp to 40 because n+1 ends up nonzero, but an adversarial value that wraps to a low byte in 1..40 with a zero high byte (e.g. -65540 -> 4) would be accepted as that small number rather than clamped to 40. This mirrors the C twin's 16-bit int overflow, so it is consistent with the reference implementation and almost certainly out of scope, but it is technically reachable. → If strict behavior is ever wanted, break out of the digit loop early once n exceeds 40 (or once n+1 becomes nonzero) so the clamp is guaranteed. Otherwise leave as-is for parity with tail.c and note it is intentional.

**Docs:** Strong file header (purpose, invocation, ring layout, entry register P2, state-var glossary). Every routine has a header stating inputs/outputs/clobbers. Inline comments are non-obvious-focused and I verified they match the code, including the tricky flag-semantics notes (lines 62-63 correctly explain CMP C-semantics for the digit test; line 228 correctly justifies the total+1!=0 => >n shortcut; line 45 correctly notes INP2 peeks without touching t_arg). tbuf_addr's carry-fold rationale is well documented. Minor gap: nothing documents the per-line 255-char cap at the point of the col>=255 test (only the C twin's header mentions it), and no note that error exits intentionally do not return a status code (matches codebase convention but a one-liner would preempt the question).

**Strengths:** Every carry/borrow branch after CMP/SUB is correct - the single most common bug class in this ISA is entirely absent here (verified JNC/JC at lines 67, 71, 79, 117, 156, 180, 188, 213, 221, 236, 280, 315, 332, 368, 375).; The replay loop correctly relies on SYS_PUTC preserving P1 - confirmed against OUTCH in p8xos.asm, which saves/restores P1 around FPUTB and P2 around the capture buffer. This is exactly the kind of cross-module assumption that is usually wrong, and here it is right.; tbuf_addr's address math is both correct and efficient: it exploits the exact 256-byte slot stride (slot -> high byte) and properly folds the low-byte carry from tb_col into the high byte, so it works even though <buf is nonzero.; The ring base/count selection at EOF handles all three cases correctly (total<n: base=0,count=total; total==n: base=0,count=n because slot has wrapped to 0; total>n: base=slot,count=n), including the total>=256 fast path.; Stack is perfectly balanced - no PHA/PLA in this file, pure JSR/RTS, and P3-as-SP is never disturbed.; Documentation is accurate, not just present: the flag-semantics comments actually match the branch directions, which is rare and valuable.

**Top improvements:** Extract the duplicated line-commit sequence (write NUL + advance/wrap slot + 16-bit total++) shared by t_nl and t_feof into one subroutine to eliminate the drift risk.; Introduce a t_syncp2 helper (or make t_ainc keep P2 in sync) to collapse the ~7 repetitions of the LDA t_arg/TAP2L/LDA t_arg+1/TAP2H idiom.; Remove the handful of redundant LDA reloads that survive an intervening CMP (which preserves A), keeping only the ones after a clobbering JSR.


### `os/commands-asm/touch.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded TOUCH command: parses a whitespace-separated name list from the argument tail, makes each name CWD-absolute via an inlined abspath, and creates a zero-byte file for any name that does not already exist (existing files are left untouched, not truncated).

This is a careful, correct, and faithful hand-assembly port of touch.c. The load-bearing carry branch (FOPEN C=1 = "not found", so JNC after FOPEN means "exists, skip") is inverted-branch-safe and matches the documented ABI, and all multi-byte pointer arithmetic chains carry correctly. The abspath CWD-join logic (walk to NUL, back up, conditionally append '/') is subtle but sound. Deductions are minor: a hardcoded $FC00 magic address, two duplicated space-skip loops, and one unverified defensive edge (empty CWD). No real bugs found.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · lines 112-115 (t_ex FOPEN buffer setup) — The read buffer address $FC00 is hardcoded as two magic bytes ($00 / $FC) rather than referenced by name. The C version uses the symbolic RDBUF; per the project memory the buffer was deliberately moved to $FC00 to avoid a collision, so a future move would require editing raw bytes here and could silently desync from the C side. RDBUF is not currently defined in lib_abi.inc. → Add `RDBUF = $FC00` to lib_abi.inc and load it via `LDA #<RDBUF`/`LDA #>RDBUF` so the asm and C share one source of truth for the buffer address.
- _low_ · correctness · abspath / ab_sld (lines 175-177) — UNVERIFIED defensive edge: if SYS_GETCWD ever returned an empty string (NUL at ap_out[0]), ab_sl would hit NUL immediately and ab_sld's DEP1 would back P1 up to ap_out-1, reading/comparing a byte before the buffer. In practice CWD is always at least "/", so this cannot trigger today; flagging only as a latent assumption, not a live bug. → No change needed given the CWD>="/" invariant; optionally add a comment at ab_sld noting the routine assumes a non-empty CWD.
- _low_ · clarity · t_sk (18-34) and t_ns/t_nsk (88-104) — The leading-space skip and the inter-name space skip are two near-identical loops that each reload P2 from t_arg and do a verbose 16-bit RAM increment (LDA/LDB #1/ADD/STA + JNC/high-INC). This is repeated boilerplate that inflates the routine and is easy to edit inconsistently. → Consider factoring a small `skip_spaces` helper (advance t_arg while (P2)==32) and/or an `inc16 t_arg` macro/subroutine; keeps the two call sites in lockstep. Optional given the command's small size.
- _low_ · style · t_nsk (line 92) — The t_nsk label is never used as a jump target (the loop re-enters at t_ns, which reloads P2); it functions only as a comment anchor. Harmless but slightly misleading since it reads like a loop-top. → Drop the t_nsk label or add a note that iteration re-enters at t_ns (to reload the pointer), so readers don't assume a tight (P2)-only inner loop.

**Docs:** Excellent documentation. The file header states purpose, the abspath algorithm (P2 source / P1 dest), the create-vs-leave-untouched invariant, the exact BIOS/OS addresses, and the P2-entry convention. abspath has its own accurate routine header including the clobber list (A,B,P1,P2). Inline comments are accurate and match the code, including the non-obvious parts: the P3-is-SP rationale for using P2 as source cursor, the "overwriting the NUL is fine" note at ab_sld, and the FOPEN carry meaning. Only gap: the exists-check FOPEN is never FCLOSEd; a one-line note that the read stream is a single shared pipe (so re-opening simply re-inits it, no leak) would preempt the reviewer's natural worry — the C version has the same shape.

**Strengths:** Correct, non-inverted carry handling: JNC t_loop after FOPEN correctly treats C=0 as 'file exists -> skip', matching the documented FOPEN C=1=missing ABI and preserving the crucial 'do not truncate existing files' invariant.; All multi-byte arithmetic chains carry/borrow correctly by hand (the arg+=n add at 80-87 and every 16-bit t_arg increment use JNC then high-byte INC).; Faithful behavioral port of touch.c: same usage/-h/-H handling, same abspath-then-advance-then-skip-spaces-then-exists ordering, same double-FRESOLVE before FWOPEN.; The abspath CWD-join (walk to NUL, DEP1 to inspect last char, conditionally append exactly one '/') is a genuinely tricky sequence and is implemented correctly, with P1 landing on the right byte in both branches.; Clean register discipline: P2 consistently used as source cursor (P3 is SP), P1 reloaded before each BIOS call, JSR/RTS balanced with no stack imbalance, no pointer clobbered across a call without being reloaded.; Outstanding, accurate documentation (see doc notes).

**Top improvements:** Define RDBUF = $FC00 in lib_abi.inc and reference it symbolically at t_ex instead of hardcoding the $00/$FC bytes, keeping the asm and C in sync on the read-buffer address.; Add a one-line comment (or the RDBUF symbol) documenting that the exists-check FOPEN is intentionally left open because the read stream is a single shared pipe re-inited on next open, so there is no handle leak.; Optionally factor the two duplicated space-skip loops / 16-bit t_arg increments into a shared helper or macro to reduce boilerplate and edit risk.


### `os/commands-asm/tree.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: B**  ·  ASM

Hand-coded TREE command: depth-first indented listing of the CWD directory tree, the assembly twin of tree.c. Because P8X has only P1/P2 as GP pointers, per-level locals live in depth-indexed global arrays keyed by a global w_depth instead of a software stack.

Faithful, well-organized translation of tree.c with genuinely helpful inline commentary including carry-branch notes. The one substantive problem is unbounded recursion depth against fixed-size depth-indexed arrays (a divergence from tree.c, which is stack-bounded), which can silently corrupt memory on trees deeper than 16. A couple of ISA/BIOS-dependent details are marked low/unverified.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · tw_desc / walk recursion (lines 188-191) vs array sizes — No bound check on w_depth before INC w_depth / JSR walk. nsub/idx are .fill 16 and csub is .fill 768 (16 levels); a tree deeper than 16 levels indexes past the arrays (nsub[16] aliases idx[0], csub runs off the end) — silent memory corruption. Real divergence from tree.c, whose per-level locals are naturally stack-bounded. → After INC w_depth, CMP against MAXD (16) and skip the recursive JSR walk at the ceiling, or document TREE as undefined beyond 16 levels.
- _low_ · clarity · walk entry, lines 67-68 — 'LDA #0 / STA kk' with comment '(temp: store 0 via nsub addr)' is dead code: kk is overwritten later and the store doesn't use nsub_addr. → Delete the two lines, or fix the misleading comment.
- _low_ · correctness · tw_next FNEXT test (JC tw_desc) — tree.c detects end-of-directory via r&256; the asm uses JC after FNEXT, correct only if the BIOS maps end-of-dir to carry. Consistent-looking but unverified from this file. → Confirm FNEXT returns end-of-dir in carry; add a comment tying JC to tree.c's r&256.
- _low_ · correctness · tw_isdir de+12 and de+15/+16 LBA — Assumes de[12]==2 for dir and 16-bit start LBA at de[15..16]; tree.c uses de_isdir()/de_lba() abstractions. Magic offsets depend on SYS_DIRENTRY layout — unverified here. → Verify against the SYS_DIRENTRY struct; add a comment citing the field-layout source.

**Docs:** Inline comments are a strength: nearly every block annotated, clobber lists given, carry-branch reasoning spelled out. Two accuracy problems: header claims 'MAXD levels deep ... as tree.c' but tree.c has NO depth limit (its sub[24] lives on the native call stack), hiding that the asm added an unenforced cap; and the walk-start comment on the 'STA kk' line is wrong (that store is dead).

**Strengths:** Locals kept entirely in memory (arrays), so no P1/P2 register-clobber hazards survive across JSR SYS_PUTC/FNEXT/SYS_DIRENTRY.; Carry-sensitive branches correct and explicitly justified (nsub>=24 uses JC to skip recording; multi-byte address arithmetic chains carry via JNC+INC).; Descent termination (idx==nsub) and the nsub<24 cap faithfully match tree.c; dotfile/./.. skip and indent loop correct.; Clean, readable structure with clear label naming and banner comments.

**Top improvements:** Add a MAXD (16) depth guard before recursing so deep trees can't overflow the depth-indexed arrays — the only latent memory-corruption bug.; Fix the header's 'as tree.c' depth-cap claim (tree.c has no cap).; Remove the dead 'STA kk' at walk entry and its incorrect comment.


### `os/commands-asm/uniq.asm`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY (8-BIT TTL ISA)

Hand-coded UNIQ command: collapses adjacent duplicate lines from a named file or stdin, with a -h/-H help flag. It inlines its own readline() and streq() and borrows the shared stdin engine (openarg/nextc) via ;#use stdin.

This is a clean, careful, and correct piece of hand-assembly. The hard parts of this ISA — 16-bit pointer ripple-increments, chaining carry manually, and working around opcodes (nextc/(P2) loads) that clobber the pointer registers — are all handled correctly, and every carry/borrow branch direction I checked is right. I found no correctness bugs; the only issues are minor edge-case behaviors and a couple of small size/DRY opportunities. Documentation is genuinely excellent and, importantly, accurate.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · readline, lines 159-162 — Lines longer than 255 chars are silently truncated to 255 before comparison. Two different long lines that share the same first 255 characters will compare equal via streq and be collapsed as adjacent duplicates. This is an edge case and likely matches the uniq.c counterpart, but it is a real (if rare) semantic quirk. → Confirm the C version has identical truncation behavior so the two stay byte-compatible; if so, add a one-line comment noting the 255-char-prefix collision is intentional/accepted rather than leaving it implicit.
- _low_ · clarity · u_chk, lines 38-49 — Help detection only inspects the single character immediately after the first '-'. So '-h'/'-H' (and incidentally '-hanything') trigger usage, but '--help' is treated as a filename to open, and any other unknown flag like '-x' falls through and is opened as a file rather than rejected. Behavior is slightly surprising for a flag parser. → Either document that only a bare -h/-H is recognized and everything else is treated as a filename, or, if matching the C version, verify the C flag handling agrees. No code change strictly needed, but the asymmetry deserves a comment.
- _low_ · efficiency · u_nf (121-129) and u_usage (130-138) — The two exit routines are byte-for-byte identical except for the message pointer: both do TAP1L/TAP1H, LDA #0, JSR SYS_PUTS, LDA #10, JSR SYS_PUTC, RTS. This duplicates ~7 instructions. → Load the message pointer into P1, then JMP to a shared 'print P1 as line + RTS' tail. Saves bytes and centralizes the print-line idiom (which also appears at u_put).
- _low_ · isa-misuse · u_sk (27-34), readline (169-176) — the ADD/STA/JNC ripple sequence — The 16-bit increment relies on the carry flag from ADD surviving the intervening STA (STA u_arg / STA rlp) before the JNC test. This is almost certainly fine and is used consistently across the codebase, but the firmware header quoted to me documents flag effects for CMP/SUB/shifts, not explicitly that STA preserves C. I could not fully confirm STA's flag behavior from the file itself — flagging as UNVERIFIED, not asserting a bug. → No change expected; just worth a one-line note in docs/p8x-system-design.md (or a file comment) stating that STA preserves C/Z, since the whole ripple-carry idiom depends on it.

**Docs:** File header states purpose, invocation, entry contract (P2 = arg tail), and its dependencies. Every routine (u_sk, u_chk, u_open, u_loop, u_copy, readline, streq) has a header, and the non-obvious mechanics are explained where they occur: the ADD/JNC/INC 16-bit increment idiom (lines 16-18, 169), why cursors are spilled to memory because nextc/(P2) clobber P1/P2 (141, 206), the openarg A=2 not-found convention (51), the CRLF/length-cap logic (156-162), and the A=0/A=1 return contracts. I spot-checked comments against code and they match — e.g. "JNC skips the INC", the streq/first branch semantics, and the buffer sizing note (255 + NUL + slack) are all correct. Only small gaps: the flag-preservation assumption across STA (see finding) is never stated, and externally-supplied symbols (oa_a, openarg, nextc) aren't called out as coming from the stdin engine.

**Strengths:** All manual carry/borrow branch directions are correct: openarg A==2 not-found (JZ), buffer-full rln>=255 (JC), nextc EOF (JC), and both 16-bit ripple increments (JNC skips the high-byte INC) — no inverted-carry bugs, which is the classic failure mode here.; Correctly works around pointer-register clobbering: readline and streq spill their write/read cursors to memory words (rlp, se_c) and reload P1/P2 per access because nextc and (Pn) dereferences trash the pointers. This is the right and non-obvious solution.; Edge cases are handled cleanly: empty lines (immediate LF) still return A=1 with a NUL string, a final line with no trailing newline is emitted (rl_skip EOF -> rl_done), CRLF is normalized by dropping CR, and the buffer can never overflow (cap at 255 before the store, NUL fits within the 260-byte buffer).; The first/streq control flow is correct and readable: first line always prints, subsequent lines print only when streq reports not-equal, and prev is refreshed on every line regardless of print decision.; Documentation is thorough and, on verification, accurate — a rare combination in hand-assembly.

**Top improvements:** Merge the identical u_nf/u_usage exit paths (and ideally the u_put print) into one shared 'print P1 as a line' tail to cut duplicated bytes.; Verify byte-for-byte behavioral parity with os/commands/uniq.c on the two edge cases that aren't obvious from this file alone: >255-char line truncation/collision, and the single-char '-h/-H only' flag handling.; Add a short note (here or in the system-design doc) that STA preserves the carry flag, since the pervasive ripple-carry increment idiom silently depends on it.


### `os/commands-asm/vi.asm`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY (8-BIT TTL ISA)

A hand-coded modal VT100 screen editor (the asm counterpart of os/commands/vi.c): loads a file into a flat 110x80 line buffer, supports NORMAL/INSERT modes with motions, insert/delete/open/split lines, single-level undo, forward search with wrap, and :w/:q/:wq/:x/:q! colon commands over BIOS CONIN/CONOUT and the file syscalls.

This is a genuinely impressive, carefully structured piece of hand assembly: ~2000 lines organized into clearly-labeled sections (terminal primitives, line-buffer helpers, load/save, redraw, editing, undo, search, colon commands, data). Carry/borrow branch directions after CMP/SUB are correct essentially everywhere I checked — no inverted JC/JNC bugs, which is the dominant failure mode for this ISA. Pointer discipline is disciplined (P1 reloaded after every P1/P2-clobbering syscall in save/drawrow). The two real defects are edge-case cursor-position bugs (empty-line 'l', and clampx clamping to length rather than length-1), one of which can corrupt the buffer, plus a systemic efficiency issue: laddr recomputes i*80 by repeated addition twice per byte inside the copy/shift loops. Documentation is excellent.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · nm_l (lines 225-244), interacting with inschar — On an empty line (llen==0), 'l' computes nm_t = len-1 = 0-1 = 255 (borrow), then CMP cx(0) vs 255 clears carry, so JNC nm_lok is taken and cx is advanced to 1 — past the end of a 0-length line. This is the only path that makes cx>len (non-empty lines correctly stop at len-1). If the user then enters insert and types, inschar runs with cx=1 > len=0: its shift loop falls straight through to ic_set, which stores the char at col 1 and then writes the NUL at ic_n+1 = 1, overwriting that char and leaving col 0 uninitialized — garbage in the line that persists to redraw and save. → Guard the empty-line case in nm_l: after llen, if A==0 jump to nm_end (do nothing), matching real vi. Alternatively make nm_lok also run clampx. Add a regression test that opens a file with a blank line, presses 'l' then 'ix<ESC>', and checks the saved line.
- _medium_ · efficiency · laddr (605-652) called inside copyline (1119-1144), inschar (1028-1052), delchar (1086-1113), splitline, opendown, delline, insline — laddr builds va_i*80 by a repeated-+80 loop (up to 109 iterations) every call. copyline calls laddr TWICE per byte (src and dst) inside its per-column loop, so a single line copy is ~2*80 laddr calls, each up to ~109 adds (~17k inner adds); delline/opendown/splitline copy many lines, multiplying this. inschar/delchar shift loops likewise recompute i*80 twice per shifted byte. → Compute the row base address once (laddr with va_c=0) into a saved 16-bit pointer and 16-bit-increment it across the byte loop, exactly as drawrow already does with dptr. For copyline, keep two running pointers (src,dst) and INP1/increment rather than re-deriving both each byte. This is the single biggest cycle win and removes most laddr calls.
- _low_ · correctness · clampx (674-685) — After a vertical move onto a shorter line, clampx clamps cx to cxn==len (the JNC/JZ pair send cx<len and cx==len to cx_ok, and cx>len sets cx=len). That leaves the NORMAL-mode cursor at column len — one past the last real character, sitting on the NUL slot. Cosmetic cursor offset; 'x' there no-ops (delchar treats cx>=len as nothing to delete) and 'h' self-corrects to len-1, so no corruption, but it deviates from vi and from the routine's own comment. → If the intent is NORMAL-mode semantics, clamp to len-1 (when len>0): after llen, if A>0 decrement before comparing/storing. If clamping to len is deliberate (to share the routine with append), fix the comment to say so and note the phantom-column behavior.
- _low_ · correctness · inschar cap (1022-1025) vs load truncation (735-738) — load stores up to 79 chars/line (cols 0..78, NUL at 79 — the true max for an 80-byte slot), but inschar refuses to insert once len>=78, so insertion can only grow a line to 78 chars. A line loaded at 79 chars can't be inserted into at all, and you can never reach the 79-char maximum via typing. Off-by-one conservative — safe, but wastes one usable column and is inconsistent with load. → Change the inschar cap test to reject only len>=79 (LDB #79). Then a 78-char line can grow to 79 with the NUL landing at index 79, still inside the slot.
- _low_ · clarity · docmd (1844-1888) — ':wq', ':q!' and ':w' match on cmd[0]/cmd[1] without checking that cmd[2]==0, so 'wqxyz' behaves as 'wq' and 'q!!' as force-quit. Only ':x' is strict (dc_x requires cmd+1==0). Harmless but inconsistent, and could mask a typo. → For the two-char commands, also verify cmd[2]==0 before acting (or document that trailing chars are ignored). Keep it consistent with the strict 'x' handling.
- _low_ · clarity · saveline (1361-1367), insline (1421-1427), undo uop==1 (1471-1477) — Three byte-for-byte-identical 'copy a NUL-terminated line via STA (P1)+' loops are inlined; getpat (1636-1723) and docmd (1734-1802) are also ~60 lines of near-duplicate prompt/echo/BS input-loop logic. Maintenance hazard: a fix to one won't propagate. → Factor the usave copy into one small subroutine (set P1=dest, P2=src, JSR copyz) and consider a shared prompt-input routine parameterized by the target buffer and prompt char for getpat/docmd.
- _low_ · efficiency · matchat (1512-1552) — Each loop iteration recomputes P2 = pat + ma_m from scratch twice: once to test for the terminating NUL (1514-1524) and again to fetch the byte for comparison (1537-1547). The pat pointer could be kept and incremented. → Load pat[ma_m] once into a register/temp, branch on NUL, then reuse it for the compare; increment a running pat pointer instead of recomputing base+index each pass.
- _low_ · correctness · findfrom ff_jl (1576-1584) — On the first scanned line the column starts at ff_sx = cx+1 (set in search). If cx ever equals len (see clampx finding), ff_sx = len+1 indexes past the line's NUL into the uninitialized bytes of the 80-wide slot, which matchat may then read. In practice cx normally stays <= len-1 so cx+1 lands on the NUL and the scan advances to the next line, so I could not construct a concrete failing input — flagging as unverified. → Have search clamp ff_sx to the line length, or have findfrom stop a line's scan once it reads the NUL regardless of the start column. Low priority unless the clampx behavior is left as-is.
- _low_ · correctness · laddr high-byte fold (634-647) — vat+1 is formed as vat+1 + >line + vacar using two separate ADDs, and the carry out of the first ADD (vat+1 + >line) is discarded. Given va_i<=109 the offset is <=~0x2250 and 'line' sits low enough that the 16-bit address cannot wrap, so this is safe with the current buffer placement — but it is a latent trap if 'line' were ever relocated high in memory. Unverified as an actual bug; safe today. → Either add a comment stating the no-wrap assumption on 'line''s address, or chain the carry from the >line add into the vacar add so the high byte is exactly correct regardless of placement.
- _low_ · clarity · undo un_fin (1503-1504) and nm_x snap1 (394) — undo unconditionally sets dirty=1, so undoing back to a pristine buffer still reports it as modified (comment acknowledges this). Separately, 'x' calls snap1 (recording uop=1) even when cx>=len and delchar is a no-op, creating a useless undo entry. → Optional: track a saved-clean marker to clear dirty when the buffer matches the on-disk state; and skip snap1 in nm_x when there is nothing at cx to delete. Both are polish, not required.

**Docs:** Strong. A real file header (lines 1-8) with the buffer layout and the full BIOS address list; every routine has an accurate purpose-comment block that I was able to verify against the code (inschar's "78-char cap", drawrow's "screen row = dr_r+1", save's "sv_p2 reloaded before every byte", the undo uop table, the gotoxy '59'==';' assembler-comment trick are all correct). Magic numbers (110, 80, 79, 22/23, 38, 24) are explained where they matter. Gaps: (1) the clampx comment "pull it back to the last valid column (line length)" is misleading — line length is one PAST the last valid column index, and the code indeed leaves cx==len (see finding). (2) No comment flags that pressing Enter in insert mode (im_split sets uop=0) silently discards the undo snapshot taken by i/a/A. (3) load's "columns past 79 are truncated" is slightly loose (it stores cols 0..78, i.e. 79 chars, and drops at col>=79).

**Strengths:** Carry/borrow branch directions after CMP/SUB are correct throughout the file — the number-one bug class for this ISA is essentially absent (load truncation, line-cap checks, scroll window math, delline/opendown/splitline bounds all branch the right way).; Excellent, accurate documentation: real file header with buffer layout and BIOS map, per-routine comment blocks that match the code, and even the gotoxy '59'==';' assembler-comment trick is explained.; Correct and careful pointer discipline around clobbering syscalls: save's sv_cl reloads P1 from sv_p2 before every FPUTB, drawrow snapshots the row pointer into dptr and 16-bit-increments it, and rawkey/outc re-zero P1 for CONIN/CONOUT.; load() handles the fiddly cases right: FOPEN failure -> one empty line, empty file -> nlines=1, CR dropped / LF ends line, trailing partial line counted, final-newline not counted as an extra empty line, and the 110-line / 79-column caps prevent buffer overrun (max index line[8799] fits the 8800-byte buffer).; Clean modal structure and dispatch: the NORMAL-mode key handlers are a readable chain of self-contained nm_* clauses, and the undo design (uop code + usave scratch line) is simple and correctly wired for the in-place / open / delete cases it claims to support.

**Top improvements:** Fix the empty-line 'l' motion (nm_l): bail when llen==0 so cx can never exceed len; this closes the buffer-corruption path through inschar.; Optimize the laddr usage in copyline/inschar/delchar/split/open/delline: compute the row base once and increment a running pointer (as drawrow already does) instead of re-deriving i*80 twice per byte.; Reconcile clampx and the inschar cap with the documented 79-char width: clamp NORMAL-mode cx to len-1 (or fix the comment), and let inschar grow lines to a full 79 chars.; De-duplicate the three identical usave-copy loops and the two near-identical getpat/docmd input loops into shared subroutines to cut size and maintenance risk.


### `os/commands-asm/wc.asm`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  P8X ASSEMBLY

Hand-coded WC command: counts lines, words, and bytes for a file/glob or stdin, maintaining 24-bit counters and printing them via a byte-wise divmod-10 (the CPU has no divide). ASM counterpart of os/commands/wc.c.

This is a genuinely strong, careful piece of hand assembly. The hardest parts — 24-bit counter increments with manually chained carry, and a 24-bit-over-10 long division built from an 8-bit repeated-subtraction divmod — are all implemented correctly, including the tricky flag distinctions (JNC after ADD for carry ripple, JNZ after INC because INC only sets Z, JC after SUB for the borrow into the high byte). I found no high- or medium-severity correctness bugs. The findings are minor: a few redundant reloads that ignore CMP's A-preserving guarantee, an inefficient (but bounded and clearly-written) divmod, a slightly loose help-flag match, and one small doc omission.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · efficiency · w_wsq, lines 108/112/116 — The whitespace test reloads the character with LDA wch before each of the tab(9)/LF(10)/CR(13) comparisons, but the ISA guarantees CMP preserves A, so A still holds wch from the previous compare. These three LDA wch instructions are dead loads. → Drop the LDA wch at lines 108, 112, 116; keep only the initial LDA wch at line 104. Saves 3 loads per byte scanned on the hot path.
- _low_ · efficiency · divmod10 (dv_l/dv_sub loop, 318-345) — divmod10 divides by 10 via repeated subtraction, so it can loop up to ~255 times per byte (dv can reach 9*256+255=2559, quotient up to 255). Called for every byte of every emitted digit, this is a lot of cycles when counts are large. Not a bug — bounded and correct — but the slowest part of the command. → Optional: replace with a shift-based divmod10 or a small subtract-larger-multiples-of-10 ladder (e.g. try 2560,1280,...,10). Fine to leave as-is given clarity goals for a homebrew; noting for awareness.
- _low_ · correctness · w_chk (38-49) — The help check treats ANY argument beginning with '-h' or '-H' as the help flag (it only inspects the second character, not that the arg is exactly "-h"). So e.g. "-hello" would print usage instead of being opened as a filename. This is unverified against wc.c's exact behavior and is likely intentional parity, hence low severity. → If exact-match is desired, after matching 'h'/'H' also confirm the following byte is NUL (or a space) before branching to w_usage. Otherwise document that the flag is prefix-matched.
- _low_ · correctness · w_sk (19-35) — Leading-whitespace skipping in the arg tail only skips ASCII 32 (space); tabs (9) are not skipped. A tab-prefixed argument would be passed to openarg with the leading tab intact. Low impact since arg tails are normally space-delimited. → If desired, also skip tab (9) in the w_sk loop, or document that only spaces are trimmed.
- _low_ · docs · put24 header, line 196 — The clobber list says "clobbers A/B, num24, dg, psnd" but put24 also clobbers pstmp indirectly through pu_push, and dm10/divmod10 scratch (dmrem, dmi, dv, dvq, dvr) via the JSR dm10 calls. → Add pstmp (and note that dm10's scratch is also clobbered) to put24's clobber comment for accuracy.
- _low_ · style · line 206 (and 48-value adds) — LDB #48 is used to convert a digit to ASCII; the numeric 48 is less self-documenting than the character literal. → Use LDB #'0' to make the digit-to-ASCII intent explicit; the assembler already supports char literals (used at line 39 for '-').

**Docs:** Excellent. A real file header explains the 24-bit rationale and maps put24/dm10 back to wc.c's dm10/put24. Every routine has a header comment stating what it does AND its clobber set (e.g. "Uses P1; clobbers ..."). Inline comments are accurate and target the non-obvious spots: the carry-ripple pattern, "INC sets Z, so JNZ skips the next byte", MSB-first walk in dm10, and the ws->word transition rule. Verified several comments against the code and they match. One small omission: put24's clobber list (line 196) doesn't mention pstmp, which pu_push writes. Magic numbers are mostly softened by comments or char literals, though a couple (e.g. #48 for '0') would read better as #'0'.

**Strengths:** 24-bit counter increments correctly chain carry by hand: JNC after ADD for the low byte, then JNZ after INC on higher bytes (correctly relying on INC setting Z rather than assuming a carry the opcode may not produce).; The 24-bit/10 division (dm10 walking bytes MSB-first, folding the running remainder into divmod10's high half) is a correct long-division scheme, and dm10 safely stores only dvq's low byte because the quotient of (rem<10)*256+byte can never exceed 255 — a subtle invariant that holds.; divmod10's 16-bit subtract-10 uses the borrow flag correctly: JC after SUB skips the high-byte DEC on no-borrow, decrements it on borrow.; put24 runs the first dm10 unconditionally so a zero value still prints "0", and digits are buffered LSB-first then replayed reversed — a clean, correct approach.; Pointer discipline is sound: P2 is reloaded from the in-memory w_arg after any syscall/nextc that could clobber it, counters live in memory across calls, and divmod10 genuinely preserves P1 as its comment claims (so dm10's post-call STA (P1) is safe).; Documentation is exemplary for hand assembly: file header, per-routine headers with clobber sets, and accurate inline notes on every non-obvious flag decision.

**Top improvements:** Remove the three redundant LDA wch reloads in the whitespace test (CMP preserves A) to tighten the per-byte hot loop.; Consider a faster divmod10 (shift-based or multiples-of-10 ladder) if large-file performance matters; current repeated-subtraction is correct but can loop hundreds of times per digit-byte.; Tighten or document the help-flag match so "-hello"-style args aren't silently treated as -h, and optionally skip leading tabs alongside spaces in w_sk.; Small doc fix: add pstmp (and dm10 scratch) to put24's clobber comment, and use #'0' instead of #48 for the ASCII conversion.


## Compiler (host C)

### `compiler/p8cc.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: B**  ·  C (P8CC SMALL-C SUBSET, DUAL-COMPILABLE WITH HOST GCC)

A single-pass C-subset compiler that reads C on stdin and emits P8X assembly on stdout: lexer + //#define macros, a full precedence ladder, an lvalue/type model with pointers, arrays, strings, and struct/union, plus on-demand runtime helper routines and a software stack-frame calling convention.

This is well-crafted, internally consistent code that does a lot within a very tight language subset. The lvalue/type-tag model, on-demand emission of runtime helpers (dead-code elimination of unused ops), the backpatch-free for-loop layout, and the &0xFFFF masking that keeps 16-bit-target and 32-bit-host arithmetic identical all show real care. The main weaknesses are systemic rather than local: no fixed-size table or lexer buffer is bounds-checked (silent corruption on overflow), the per-function frame reservation is limited to 255 bytes by an 8-bit immediate, and the arithmetic/comparison model is uniformly unsigned even though the compiler admits signed values (unary minus, negative initializers) — none of which is documented. Individually most are bounded or edge cases; collectively they are the difference between a B and an A.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · emit_lt (768), emit_divmod (683), emit_shr (743) — All comparisons, division/modulo, and right-shift are unsigned (CMP/JC borrow semantics, SHR zero-fill), yet the compiler produces and stores signed values: unary minus (unary(), line 1046), negative global initializers (toplevel, line 1591). So `if (x < 0)`, `-6 / 2`, and `neg >> 1` all compute wrong results with no diagnostic. → Decide the intended semantics. If the subset is meant to be unsigned-only, document that prominently in the header (and ideally reject unary minus). If signed is required, emit a signed compare (test the sign bits) for < / >, an arithmetic shift for >>, and sign-corrected div/mod.
- _medium_ · safety · whole file: gpool/goff[64], spool/soff[64], vpool/vnoff[64], mac_names/mac_vals[64], stpool[16], mpool/[160], tname[64], tstr[256], nm/dn/tag/pn[64] — No fixed-size table or scratch buffer is ever bounds-checked. addglobal/addvar/intern/mac_define/register_struct and the string-literal path in factor() all write past their arrays once the (low) limits are exceeded — e.g. the 65th string literal, the 65th global, a 64-char identifier, or a 256-byte string literal silently overwrites adjacent globals. Overflow is undetectable and corrupts codegen rather than erroring. → At minimum, guard each append with a count/offset check and emit a `; ERROR: too many …` line (matching the existing error-emission style) so overflow is diagnosable. Several limits (64 strings, 64 globals) are also quite low for real programs and could be raised.
- _medium_ · correctness · toplevel, lines 1570-1578 (frame reservation) — The prologue reserves locals with `LDB #nl` — an 8-bit immediate — then does only a single borrow into the high byte. If a function's total locals exceed 255 bytes (e.g. `char buf[300];`), nl's high bits are dropped: emitdec(nl) prints e.g. 300 but only the low byte is subtracted from __csp, so the frame is under-reserved and locals collide with the caller's frame. No check or diagnostic. → Emit a full 16-bit subtract of nl (as addconst_ax already does for 16-bit adds: subtract low byte, then subtract (nl>>8)+borrow from the high byte), or at least emit an error when nl > 255.
- _low_ · correctness · parse_type (874), count_locals (1447), toplevel (1536) — find_struct() returns -1 for an unknown tag, and every caller computes `base = 2 + find_struct(...)`. For an undefined or misspelled struct/union tag that yields base = 1, which is indistinguishable from `char`: type_size returns 1 and member access silently misbehaves, with no error emitted. → Check the find_struct result and emit a `; ERROR: unknown struct tag` line when it is -1, rather than folding -1 into a valid-looking base.
- _low_ · docs · lines 360-362 — The type-encoding comment does not match the actual encoding in mkty() (line 537): it claims base in bit 0, lvalue in bit 1, pointer depth in bits 8+, but the real layout is base in bits 0-7, lvalue in bit 8, pointer depth in bits 9+. → Rewrite the comment to match mkty/ty_base/ty_lval/ty_ptr: `base = ty & 255, lval = (ty>>8)&1, ptr = ty>>9`.
- _low_ · correctness · deref_load, lines 829-840 (sz==1 path) — Byte loads zero-extend (LDA (P1); LDA #0 -> high byte), so `char` is effectively unsigned. Combined with the signed-literal support, `char c = -1; if (c == -1)` fails (c loads as 0x00FF, compared against 0xFFFF). Part of the same unsigned-vs-signed inconsistency as the comparison finding. → If signed char is intended, sign-extend on byte load; otherwise document that char is unsigned.
- _low_ · correctness · emit_divmod, lines 683-704 — Division/modulo by zero is unguarded; the restoring-division loop yields a 0xFFFF quotient and garbage remainder rather than trapping. Unverified as to whether the target expects any defined behavior here. → Acceptable for the subset, but a one-line comment noting div-by-zero is undefined would help; optionally emit a guard.

**Docs:** The file header (lines 1-33) is excellent: it states the dual-build contract, the exact subset rules obeyed, the frame convention, and the feature set — genuinely maintainer-grade. Most routines have accurate one-line purpose comments, and the numeric ASCII constants are mostly annotated. Two gaps drop the grade: (1) the type-encoding comment at lines 360-362 is stale/inaccurate — it says base in bit 0, lvalue in bit 1, pointer depth in bits 8+, but mkty() at line 537 actually packs base in bits 0-7, lvalue in bit 8, pointer depth in bits 9+; a maintainer trusting the comment would corrupt every type. (2) The compiler's uniformly-unsigned semantics (compare, divide, shift, char zero-extension) and the fixed table/frame limits are undocumented, so a user hitting `x < 0` or a 300-byte array gets silently wrong code with nothing in the header warning them.

**Strengths:** On-demand runtime emission: the use_* flags mean only the helper routines actually referenced are emitted (emit_runtime, lines 1366-1395), a clean form of dead-code elimination.; The backpatch-free for-loop codegen (stmt, lines 1335-1358) emits post-before-body and threads jumps so a single forward pass produces a correct loop without a second pass or fixups — genuinely elegant given the constraints.; Consistent, well-designed lvalue/type model: a bare lvalue carries its ADDRESS in __ax with an lval tag bit, dereferenced lazily by rvalue(); array decay, pointer scaling, and struct member addressing all fall out of it cleanly.; Disciplined host/target dual-compilation: masking with &0xFFFF (e.g. lea_off line 589) makes negative frame offsets emit identically under 16-bit-target and 32-bit-host int, and the subset rules from the header are actually obeyed throughout.; Strong, honest file header and mostly accurate inline comments; the count_locals pre-scan/rewind trick (lines 1428-1467) is well explained and lets the prologue reserve the whole frame in one shot.
