**Top improvements:** Add overflow guards (count/offset checks emitting a `; ERROR: too many …` line) to every fixed table and lexer buffer so overflow is diagnosable instead of silent memory corruption.; Emit a full 16-bit subtract for the frame reservation (like addconst_ax) or error when a function's locals exceed 255 bytes.; Resolve and document the signed/unsigned semantics: either commit to unsigned-only (and say so, ideally rejecting unary minus) or add signed compare / arithmetic-shift / sign-corrected div and signed char loads.; Fix the stale type-encoding comment at lines 360-362 to match mkty(), and diagnose unknown struct tags (find_struct == -1) instead of folding them into base==char.


### `compiler/p8lib.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A tiny freestanding C library for the P8X that is prepended to programs, providing string helpers (strlen/strcpy/strcmp), console I/O (getline/putdec), and file/OS wrappers (loadfile/savefile/getcwd/cwdlba) over the BIOS and OS jump tables.

This is clean, careful, well-documented code for a tight custom target. The control flow is written correctly within the no-break/no-continue subset, the fixed buffers are sized correctly for their intended inputs, and the byte-extraction in loadfile masks with &255 to handle a possibly-signed char cleanly. The main real defect is an unguarded max<=0 case in getline; everything else is minor or stylistic. Documentation is a genuine strength.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · getline (line 45, 50) — The loop guard is `n < max - 1` and the terminator write is `buf[n] = 0`. This is only safe for max >= 1. If a caller passes max == 0 (or negative), `max - 1` is -1; the loop is skipped but `buf[0] = 0` still writes one byte into a zero-length buffer. If int is unsigned in the subset, max==0 makes `max-1` wrap to 65535 and the loop will happily overrun the buffer until CR/LF/EOF. → Guard the entry: `if (max < 1) return 0;` before touching buf, so the function is safe for degenerate sizes regardless of int signedness.
- _low_ · correctness · putdec (line 59) — putdec is documented as unsigned but uses `v % 10` and `v / 10`. If the p8cc int is signed 16-bit and a caller passes a value with the high bit set (e.g. 40000, which reads as negative), signed division/modulo would produce wrong digits or a bad loop. Unverified — depends on how p8cc lowers `/` and `%`, which I cannot confirm here. → Either document that callers must keep values <= 32767, or, if the subset supports it, ensure unsigned division is used. Mark/verify against the p8cc codegen for `/` and `%`.
- _low_ · safety · strcpy (line 24), strcmp (line 30) — strcpy has no destination bound and will overrun if the source is longer than the caller's buffer; strcmp reads both strings without a length cap. This matches standard C strcpy semantics, so it is a documentation/caller-contract concern rather than a bug, but the file otherwise emphasizes bounds safety (getline, loadfile caveat) and these two are the odd ones out. → Add a one-line comment on strcpy noting the destination must be large enough (no bound check), consistent with the safety notes elsewhere in the file.
- _low_ · clarity · putdec (line 55) — `char tmp[6]` is one byte larger than needed: a 16-bit unsigned value is at most 5 digits (65535). Not a bug, just a small unexplained magic size. → Either shrink to tmp[5] with a comment, or add a comment that 6 covers all 16-bit values with a margin. Low priority.
- _low_ · correctness · savefile (line 91) — `bios(0x012D, 0, data[i])` passes a char that, if char is signed, is sign-extended to int for bytes >= 128. Whether FPUTB uses only the low 8 bits is a BIOS ABI detail I cannot confirm; if it inspects the full 16-bit argument this could mis-write high bytes. Unverified. → If in doubt, mask: `data[i] & 255`, mirroring the &255 pattern already used in loadfile, to make byte-width intent explicit and robust to char signedness.

**Docs:** Excellent for a file this size. The header block explains the prepend build model, the carry-flag error caveat, and the whole-sector loadfile destination requirement (with the ceil(len/512)*512 sizing) — all non-obvious and load-bearing. Inline comments accurately map each bios() call to its BIOS/OS entry (FNORM/FFIND/FLOADAT/etc.) and the addresses match the calls; the SYS_DIRENTRY [13..14]=length note matches the code exactly. The 65535=EOF and 48='0' magic numbers are annotated. Minor gaps: strcmp's non-standard 0/1 return is noted inline but the equally non-standard strcpy return (always 0, not dest) is undocumented; putdec's "unsigned" contract is stated but the consequence of passing a value with the high bit set is not.

**Strengths:** Thorough, accurate header documentation covering the prepend build model, the carry-flag error limitation, and the whole-sector loadfile destination sizing requirement; loadfile extracts the little-endian length with `& 255` masking, correctly handling a possibly-signed char and avoiding a hardcoded scratch peek by using the SYS_DIRENTRY snapshot; Control flow is written cleanly within the no-break/no-continue subset; getline's multi-condition guard (CR/LF/NUL/EOF/length) is complete and the terminator is always written; Inline comments precisely match the actual BIOS/OS entry addresses and calls, making the hardware interface auditable; putdec and getline size their fixed buffers correctly for their documented input ranges (tmp for 16-bit decimals, buf via the n < max-1 guard)

**Top improvements:** Add a `max < 1` guard at the top of getline so it is safe against degenerate/zero buffer sizes regardless of int signedness; Clarify putdec's unsigned contract in code — verify signed vs unsigned `/` and `%` lowering in p8cc, or document the 32767 ceiling; Consider masking byte writes/args with `& 255` in savefile (FPUTB) for consistency with loadfile and robustness to signed char; Document strcpy's lack of a destination bound and its non-standard return value, matching the safety-note style used elsewhere in the file


## Shared C libraries

### `os/commands/lib_abi.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET) — USED AS A MACRO/EQUATE MANIFEST

Defines object-like #defines that give symbolic names to BIOS jump-table entries ($01xx) and OS syscall-vector entries ($20xx) plus the shared read buffer, so commands call bios(FOPEN, RDBUF, 0) instead of raw hex. Spliced into commands via //#use abi.

This is an exceptionally clean, well-documented address manifest rather than executable code, so the classic bug categories (control flow, off-by-ones, buffer bounds, uninitialized use) simply do not apply — there is nothing to overrun or mis-index. Judged on its actual job — being an accurate, readable, single-source-of-truth for ABI numbers — it is very good: every symbol carries a concise, accurate inline comment, and the header correctly explains the compile-time-substitution rationale and the carry-in-bit-8 return convention. The only real risks are inherent to a hand-maintained address table: the values cannot be verified from this file alone and must be kept in lockstep with the asm twin and the firmware by hand, with no automated cross-check.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · whole file / lib_abi.inc twin — Address values cannot be validated from this file, and there is no automated check that they match either the firmware jump table/syscall vector or the asm twin os/commands-asm/lib_abi.inc. A stale or transposed constant here would silently misroute a call (e.g. invoke the wrong BIOS routine) with no compile error. This is unverified against the actual firmware — flagged as a maintainability risk, not a confirmed defect. → Keep as-is functionally, but consider a build-time diff/assert that the C #defines and the .inc equates agree (a small script comparing the two symbol lists), so the manual sync obligation stated in the header is enforced rather than trusted.
- _low_ · clarity · BIOS section (lines 30-41) — The jump-table addresses are non-contiguous by varying strides (0x0103->0x011E, 0x0130->0x0133->0x0139 skipping 0x0136, 0x013C->0x0145 skipping two slots). For a 3-byte-per-entry JMP table these gaps presumably mean unused/omitted entries, but nothing says so, so a maintainer cannot distinguish an intentional skip from a missing define. → Add one line to the BIOS-section preamble noting that entries are 3 bytes apart and that only command-visible routines are listed here (gaps = slots not called from /bin commands).
- _low_ · docs · line 18 vs individual SYS_ entries — The clobber-P1/P2 caveat is stated only once generally; individual entries that take a pointer in P1 (GETCWD, DIRENTRY, OPENDIR, MKDIR) don't restate register effects, so callers must remember the blanket rule. → Optional: annotate the pointer-taking entries with a short (P1=...) as already done for OPENDIR/MKDIR, for consistency across all of them.

**Docs:** Strong file header explaining purpose, the #define-vs-wrapper rationale (zero code cost, literal-address requirement), the sync obligation with lib_abi.inc, and the bios() return convention with concrete & 256 usage examples. Every define has an accurate one-line comment; the FGETB EOF example (while ((c & 256)==0)) is consistent with the FGETB "C=1 at EOF" note and with bit 8 = 256. Grouping into SYS_ ($20xx) and BIOS ($01xx) sections with per-section preamble is clear. Minor gaps: the general "clobber P1/P2" note (line 18) is not restated per-entry, so which specific syscalls preserve registers is left implicit; and nothing documents why the jump-table addresses are non-contiguous (e.g. 0x0103 -> 0x011E, 0x013C -> 0x0145), so a reader cannot tell intentional skips from omissions.

**Strengths:** Header block is genuinely excellent: it explains the design choice (textual #define satisfies bios()'s literal-address requirement at zero code cost), the single-source-of-truth renumbering workflow, and the carry-in-bit-8 return convention with correct, runnable-looking usage examples.; Every define has a concise, accurate inline comment; the documented behaviors (C=1 = not found / EOF / full, RDBUF at 0xFC00 page-aligned 512 bytes) are internally consistent and match the project's known ABI conventions.; Clean organization into SYS_ ($20xx) and BIOS ($01xx) groups with a per-group preamble, and addresses within each group listed in ascending order — easy to scan.; The macro-not-wrapper approach is the right call for this tight target and is correctly justified, avoiding per-call code-space cost.

**Top improvements:** Add a build-time consistency check between this file and os/commands-asm/lib_abi.inc (and ideally against the firmware's actual vector) so the header's manual-sync requirement is enforced, since a wrong constant here fails silently at runtime.; Note in the BIOS-section preamble that entries are 3-byte JMP slots and that non-contiguous addresses are intentional (only command-visible routines listed), removing ambiguity about the gaps.; For consistency, annotate all pointer-taking syscalls with their P1 argument the way OPENDIR/MKDIR already are.


### `os/commands/lib_apath.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A shared /BIN helper, spliced in via `//#use apath`, that builds an absolute path string from a command-line path word into a caller-supplied buffer: absolute words are copied verbatim, relative words are prefixed with the CWD (SYS_GETCWD) plus a single '/' separator. Returns the number of chars consumed from the input word.

Genuinely clean, correct, single-purpose code that is easy to follow and matches its documented contract. The root-CWD no-double-slash handling is done correctly and the delimiter set (NUL/CR/space) lets a whole argv line be passed while consuming only the first word. The only real weakness is the complete absence of any output-buffer bound — which is explicitly delegated to the caller by design and documentation, and is consistent with the no-heap fixed-buffer conventions of this target. A couple of theoretical edge cases (empty CWD, empty input word) are benign in practice. Documentation is a standout.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · safety · abspath (lines 33-47), out parameter — There is no bounds check on `out`: SYS_GETCWD writes the CWD, then the path word is appended byte-for-byte with no maximum length. A path word longer than the caller's buffer silently overruns it. This is the one genuine memory-safety hazard in the file. → This is documented as the caller's responsibility (lines 8-9, 24) and is the established no-heap convention, so it is acceptable as-is. If a defensive option is ever wanted, pass a cap (e.g. abspath(out, cap, a)) and stop copying at cap-1; otherwise leave a prominent note at each call site that `out` must be sized to CWD + '/' + longest path word + NUL.
- _low_ · correctness · abspath, lines 37-40 — Unverified/theoretical: if SYS_GETCWD ever returned an empty string (i stays 0), the '/' separator is correctly skipped, but the appended word would then have no leading slash, yielding a non-absolute result. In practice the OS CWD is always at least "/", so this cannot occur; I confirmed via os/p8xos.asm that SYS_GETCWD copies the maintained CWDPATH which is never empty. → No change needed given the invariant; worth a one-line comment noting the code assumes a non-empty CWD (guaranteed to be at least "/").
- _low_ · correctness · abspath, lines 37-47 — Edge case: an empty relative word (a[0] is NUL/CR/space) produces the CWD with a trailing '/' appended, e.g. CWD "/foo" -> "/foo/", and returns 0. Callers that pass an empty arg would get a directory path with a spurious trailing slash rather than the CWD itself. → Benign because /BIN commands check for a missing path argument before calling abspath(). If robustness is desired, skip the separator+copy when the first char is already a delimiter, or document that an empty word is not a supported input.
- _low_ · clarity · line 44 — The delimiters are written as bare magic numbers 13 and 32 (mitigated by the inline comment naming them CR and space). → If the p8cc subset supports char literals, `a[j] != '\r' && a[j] != ' '` reads more directly; otherwise the current comment is an adequate substitute. Minor.

**Docs:** Excellent. There is a thorough file header explaining the naming rationale (12-char P8XFS filename vs. function name), how it is spliced, and how it differs from lib_stdin's openarg(). The routine header documents in/out/ret precisely, and every non-obvious line has an accurate inline comment. I checked the comments against the code: the "return count of chars consumed" claim matches `return j`, the root-slash description matches the `i > 0 && out[i-1] != '/'` guard, and the delimiter list (NUL/CR 13/space 32) matches the while condition. The only small gap: the `0` third argument to `bios(SYS_GETCWD, out, 0)` is never explained — a one-word note that it is an ignored placeholder (SYS_GETCWD takes no length) would remove a reader's natural "is that a buffer size?" question.

**Strengths:** Correct and subtle root-CWD handling: the `i > 0 && out[i-1] != '/'` guard avoids a doubled slash for CWD "/" without a special case.; Outstanding documentation — file rationale, precise in/out/ret contract, and accurate inline comments that I verified line-by-line against the code.; Clean control flow within the subset constraints (decls at top, no ++/--), single length pass then single copy pass — no wasted work.; Well-chosen delimiter contract (NUL/CR/space) lets a whole argv line be passed while consuming only one word, and the returned consumed-count lets callers advance to the next word.; Consistent with sibling code (lib_stdin.c) and the asm twins (touch.asm, dir.asm), which aids maintainability.

**Top improvements:** Add a one-word note that the `0` third arg to bios(SYS_GETCWD, out, 0) is an ignored placeholder (SYS_GETCWD takes no length), removing the only ambiguity a reader hits.; Optionally offer a bounds-checked variant (pass a buffer cap and stop at cap-1) if any caller can ever receive an untrusted/very long path word; otherwise keep the documented caller-sizing contract.; Consider guarding the empty-word case so an empty relative arg yields the CWD rather than CWD + trailing '/'.


### `os/commands/lib_dirent.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A small include-spliced helper library that snapshots the current directory entry (name, flag, length, start-LBA) into a fixed 18-byte global buffer via two OS syscalls, and exposes predicate/accessor functions plus a subdirectory-open helper, decoupling commands from hardcoded BIOS scratch addresses.

This is a genuinely clean, tightly-scoped helper with an exemplary header that explains the "why" (scratch-address decoupling), the buffer layout, the ABI clobber contract, and the subset constraints. The masking-with-&255 pattern is correct and consistent for the signed-char subset, and there are no buffer-overrun or uninitialized-use hazards on the C side (the global is BSS-zeroed and the syscall owns the 18-byte copy). The two substantive weaknesses are behavioral, not memory-safety: de_isdot() tests only the first character so it also flags any leading-dot name, and the 16-bit signed accessors silently misreport lengths/LBAs above 32767. Neither is a crash, but both deserve at least a documented caveat.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · de_isdot (line 43) — Only de[0] is tested, so the predicate returns true for ANY entry whose name begins with '.', not just the "." and ".." directory entries. If the P8X filesystem permits leading-dot filenames (dotfiles), a listing loop that uses de_isdot() to skip . and .. would also silently hide those legitimate entries. The comment acknowledges the loose match but frames it as intended. → If the FS can produce leading-dot names, tighten to exact matches: return true only for de[0]=='.' && de[1]==' ' (the '.' entry, space-padded) or de[0]=='.' && de[1]=='.' && de[2]==' ' (the '..' entry). If the FS cannot produce dotfiles, leave the code but state that assumption explicitly in the comment so the shortcut is justified rather than merely noted.
- _low_ · correctness · de_len (line 44), de_lba (line 45) — Both reconstruct a full 16-bit little-endian value into a 16-bit SIGNED int, so any length or LBA in 32768..65535 is returned as a negative number. A caller doing a signed comparison (e.g. de_len() > limit, or ordering LBAs) would misbehave for large files/high blocks. Round-tripping de_lba() straight into de_opendir() is safe because only the low 16 bits are passed through, but arithmetic/comparison on the return value is not. This is inherent to the 16-bit-int subset, so unverified as an actual in-practice bug — it depends on whether files/LBAs ever exceed 32767 on this target. → Document the 0..32767 safe range in the accessor comments, and have callers treat the values as unsigned bit patterns (compare via subtraction of masked halves, or split hi/lo) when large disks are possible. No code change needed if current media stay under 32K blocks/bytes.
- _low_ · clarity · de_read (line 35), de_opendir (line 47) — Both functions ignore the bios() return value and unconditionally return 0, so callers get no success/failure signal and the constant return is dead information. de_read in particular cannot report a failed/absent snapshot. → Either return the syscall's status (return bios(...);) if the ABI provides one, so callers can detect failure, or if the subset lacks void, keep returning 0 but note in the comment that the return is meaningless and must not be checked.
- _low_ · style · global de[] (line 33) — The 2-character global name `de` is spliced into every command via //#use dirent, so it occupies a very short identifier in the command's global namespace and risks collision with a local or another helper. Accessors are prefixed de_ but the buffer itself is not obviously namespaced. → Consider a slightly more distinctive name (e.g. de_buf) if the p8cc symbol budget allows; if the short name is deliberate for the tight target, add a one-line note that `de` is a reserved global owned by this include.

**Docs:** Outstanding for a 50-line file. The header block covers rationale, the exact de[] byte layout, endianness, the &255 masking justification, the usage sequence (FNEXT then de_read then query), the descend pattern, and the critical P1/P2 clobber warning. Inline comments on each accessor are accurate and match the code (verified de_len/de_lba offsets and multipliers against the stated layout). The de_isdot comment is honest that it tests only the first char. Minor gap: the header documents the clobber/contract but does not state what de[] contains if de_read() is called without a preceding successful FNEXT (stale/garbage), and it does not mention the >32767 signed-truncation limit of de_len()/de_lba(). The line-31 abi comment lists example symbols (FOPEN, FGETB, ...) none of which this file uses, which is slightly misleading but harmless.

**Strengths:** Header comment is model-quality: explains the decoupling rationale, exact byte layout, endianness, the &255 masking justification, the FNEXT->de_read->query usage contract, and the P1/P2 clobber warning.; Consistent and correct &255 masking on every byte read, which is exactly right for a signed-char subset with no unsigned char.; No memory-safety hazards: de[] is a BSS-zeroed global sized to the documented 18 bytes and the syscall owns the copy, so no overrun, off-by-one, or uninitialized-use on the C side.; Genuinely single-purpose, side-effect-isolated helpers; predicates/accessors do no BIOS calls and are safe to call repeatedly, as the comment correctly states.; Inline accessor comments were verified against the stated layout (offsets 12/13-14/15-16, LE, *256) and are accurate.

**Top improvements:** Make de_isdot() distinguish the real "."/".." entries from arbitrary leading-dot names (or explicitly document that the FS never produces dotfiles), so directory-listing commands don't silently hide legitimate entries.; Document (and, where callers compare them, guard) the 16-bit signed truncation in de_len()/de_lba() for values above 32767.; Decide whether de_read()/de_opendir() should propagate the syscall status instead of a dead constant 0, so failures are detectable.; Add a one-line note that de[] is undefined until the first de_read() following a successful FNEXT, closing the only gap in an otherwise complete header.


### `os/commands/lib_distab.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

An auto-generated, data-only opcode table for the P8X disassembler: three parallel arrays (opcode value, operand-shape code, mnemonic string) plus a count, spliced into disasm.c via //#use distab.

This is a clean, well-formed generated data file with no real defects. I independently verified the three parallel arrays each contain exactly 88 elements matching dt_n and the declared [88] bounds, that dt_op is strictly increasing and duplicate-free (0..135), and that every dt_sh value lies within the 0..9 encoding that disasm.c actually handles. There is nothing here that can overrun or mis-index on its own; correctness rests entirely on the generator, which is appropriately disclaimed. The only notes are minor clarity/documentation polish, and they are mild given the file is machine-emitted.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · lines 5-8 (dt_n and the three [88] literals) — The element count 88 is hard-coded in four places (dt_n = 88 and three [88] dimensions). If a regeneration ever emitted mismatched values, dt_n could disagree with the true array length and disasm.c's loop bound (while i < dt_n) would either miss trailing entries or, if dt_n were too large, index past the initialised data. Within this file the four values are consistent, so this is a latent generator-contract risk, not a present bug. → Have the generator confirm/assert the three initialiser counts equal dt_n, or emit a single //#define DT_N 88 used for both dt_n and the array dimensions, so the count cannot drift. No hand-edit is expected, so severity is low.
- _low_ · docs · header comment (lines 1-4) — The dt_sh operand-shape codes are the file's least self-explanatory data, yet their meaning lives only in disasm.c. Someone auditing this table in isolation cannot tell what sh=9 (a,a) or sh=3..8 mean. → Add a one-line legend for the dt_sh encoding to this header (mirroring the disasm.c comment: 0 none, 1 #imm8, 2 addr16, 3..8 (Pn)/(Pn)+, 9 a,a), and have the generator keep both copies in sync.
- _low_ · style · line 4 — "Stays in the p8cc subset (globals + array-of-pointer initialisers only)" understates the constructs actually used (int scalars and int arrays too); minor wording imprecision. → Reword to something like "only global scalar/array/pointer-array initialisers — no code, so p8cc compiles it trivially."

**Docs:** Strong header block: names the file's role, the generator (gen_p8xdis.py from genucode.OPC), the "do not edit; regenerate" warning, and why it stays in the p8cc subset. Accurate to the code. Two small gaps: (1) the dt_sh operand-shape encoding (0=none, 1=#imm8, 2=addr16, 3..8=(Pn)/(Pn)+, 9=a,a) is documented in disasm.c but NOT here, even though this is the file that defines those numeric values — a reader opening lib_distab.c alone cannot interpret the dt_sh column; (2) the phrase "globals + array-of-pointer initialisers only" is slightly loose, since the file also uses plain int scalars and int arrays, not only array-of-pointer. Neither is misleading, just incomplete.

**Strengths:** All three parallel arrays are exactly 88 long and agree with dt_n and the declared [88] bounds — verified by counting, so no zero-fill padding or silent truncation.; dt_op is strictly monotonically increasing and free of duplicate opcodes (verified), which keeps the disasm.c linear scan unambiguous and would also permit a binary search later.; Every dt_sh value falls within the 0..9 range that the consumer disasm.c actually decodes (verified), so no operand-shape code is unhandled.; Excellent provenance documentation: clearly marked auto-generated, names the generator and the shared genucode.OPC source, and explains the //#use distab splice — this is exactly why the disassembler cannot drift from the assembler/microcode.; Pure data with zero control flow means no bounds, pointer, or uninitialised-use hazards originate in this file.

**Top improvements:** Emit the element count once (e.g. //#define DT_N 88) and use it for dt_n and all three array dimensions, removing the four-way duplicated magic number.; Add the dt_sh operand-shape legend to this file's header so the table is interpretable standalone, keeping it generator-synced with disasm.c.; Have the generator assert the three initialiser lengths all equal dt_n before writing the file, hardening the parallel-array contract.


### `os/commands/lib_glob.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A shared, recursive whole-string glob matcher (gmatch) spliced into /BIN commands via //#use glob. Matches a NUL-terminated name against a pattern supporting '*' (zero-or-more) and '?' (exactly-one) wildcards, case-insensitively, returning 1 on full match else 0.

This is a clean, correct implementation of the classic grep/Kernighan-style recursive matcher, adapted well to the p8cc subset. The control flow is sound, every branch returns explicitly, there are no buffers to overrun (pure pointer walking), and I found no correctness bug. The only real caveats are inherent to the recursive approach: potentially deep recursion and exponential backtracking on adversarial multi-star patterns — neither matters for short P8XFS filenames but both deserve a one-line acknowledgment. Documentation is genuinely strong and accurate.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · efficiency · gmatch (lines 27-32, '*' handling) — The recursive '*' strategy exhibits classic catastrophic backtracking: a pattern with several stars (e.g. "*a*a*a*b") against a long non-matching string can recurse exponentially. Also, each '*' can drive recursion depth proportional to the remaining string length, and every literal/'?' char recurses one frame deep — on a tight target with a small stack, a long name could in principle exhaust stack. Not a bug for short P8XFS filenames; flagged as unverified re: actual stack size. → Leave the algorithm as-is (it mirrors grep and is correct), but add one sentence to the header noting that recursion depth grows with name length and that multi-star patterns can backtrack, so this is intended for short filenames rather than arbitrary long inputs.
- _low_ · style · lines 39, 41 (the constant 32) — The case-fold offset 32 is a magic number, though it is well commented. Fine for ASCII, which is the only charset here. → Optional: introduce a named equate/#define (e.g. //#define CASEBIT 32) for the a-z fold, or leave as-is given the adjacent explanatory comment.
- _low_ · clarity · gmatch (mixed p[0] vs *s dereference style) — The routine alternates between p[0]/s (array-index) and *s (dereference) styles for reading the current char (e.g. p[0]=='*' but *s==0 on adjacent lines). Both are equivalent; the inconsistency is purely cosmetic. → Pick one form consistently (e.g. p[0] and s[0], or *p and *s) throughout. No behavioral impact.

**Docs:** Both a file header and a per-routine header are present. The file header explains purpose, wildcard semantics, the case-insensitivity rationale (upper-cased P8XFS names), and even justifies the design choice (self-recursive, grep-modelled, pointer+const args safe). The routine header documents both parameters, the return contract, and the important claim that it touches no globals/I/O so callers keep P1/P2 — valuable on this ABI. Inline comments accurately match the code: the '*' loop comment, the "both ended together" comment on line 33, and the ASCII-fold explanation on lines 36-41 are all correct. Only minor gap: neither header notes the recursion-depth / stack-usage or backtracking cost, which is the one non-obvious operational risk.

**Strengths:** Algorithmically correct: the branch ordering ('*' first, then end-of-pattern, then end-of-subject, then '?', then literal) is exactly right, and every path returns explicitly — no fall-through, no reliance on break/continue that the subset lacks.; No buffer-overrun surface at all: pure NUL-terminated pointer traversal with no fixed arrays, so there is nothing to bounds-check and nothing to overflow.; Handles the important edge cases correctly: '*' matching zero characters, consecutive stars, trailing star, and both strings ending together (line 33).; Documentation is accurate and matches the code precisely, including the ABI-relevant note that the routine clobbers no globals so callers preserve P1/P2.; Idiomatic for the target: no stdlib, no break/continue, self-recursion instead of mutual recursion, pointer+increment style consistent with the p8cc.c/grep model it cites.

**Top improvements:** Add a one-line note to the header about recursion depth growing with name length and multi-star backtracking cost, so future callers know it is meant for short filenames.; Optionally name the case-fold constant 32 via a #define for self-documentation.; Optionally unify the p[0] vs *s access style for readability.


### `os/commands/lib_globx.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Expands a filename glob (optionally with a directory prefix) into a list of matching FILE paths by scanning the named directory or CWD, matching each entry's trimmed name against the leaf pattern via gmatch(), and writing "<dir><name>" into fixed 64-byte output slots.

Clean, well-organized routine with an unusually thorough and accurate header, correct single-pass parsing, and correct handling of the CWD-vs-named-dir and relative-path cases. The core control flow and the name-trimming loop are careful and correct. The main weakness is that none of the three fixed-size buffers it fills (leaf[16], dir[64], and each 64-byte out slot) are bounds-checked against the source length, so a sufficiently long leaf or directory prefix silently overruns. In typical use (short 8.3-style names, short paths) these stay within bounds, but the guards are cheap and their absence is exactly the kind of latent memory-corruption bug worth closing on this target.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · line 71 (leaf copy loop) — The leaf-component copy `while (ls < i) { leaf[j] = pat[ls]; ... }` has no bound against leaf's size (leaf[16]). A pattern whose last component exceeds 15 characters overruns leaf and its NUL at leaf[j]. Nothing in glob_expand enforces the P8X 12-char name limit on the typed pattern, so a long literal-plus-wildcard leaf corrupts adjacent stack/globals. → Guard the copy: `while (ls < i && j < 15) { leaf[j] = pat[ls]; j = j + 1; ls = ls + 1; }` then `leaf[j] = 0;` (truncate rather than overrun).
- _medium_ · safety · lines 88-93 (out slot assembly) — The output path is assembled as dir prefix + name + NUL into a fixed 64-byte slot with no check that the total fits. dir may be up to 63 chars and name up to 12, so a deep named-directory prefix can push the written path past 64 bytes, corrupting the next slot (or, for the final slot, memory past `out`). Only the match COUNT is bounded (cnt < maxn), never the per-slot LENGTH. → Track the write offset relative to the slot and stop/skip at 63, e.g. cap `base` at `cnt*64 + 63` before writing each byte and always terminate; or skip (do not emit) any match whose dir+name length >= 64. Whether P8X paths can realistically reach this length is unverified, but the guard is cheap.
- _low_ · safety · lines 65-68 (dir prefix copy) — The directory-prefix copy `while (j <= slashpos) { dir[j] = pat[j]; ... }` plus `dir[j] = 0` is unbounded against dir[64]. If the last '/' sits at index >= 63 (prefix longer than 63 chars), dir overruns. Same root cause as the leaf case: pattern length is never validated. → Bound the loop with `j <= slashpos && j < 63`, then NUL-terminate. Realistic path-length limits on P8X are unverified, so severity is low, but the guard closes the gap.
- _low_ · clarity · lines 88, 90-92 (slot size 64) — The 64-byte slot size is a magic number hardcoded in `cnt * 64` and implied by the copy loops, while the header documents it as '64-byte slots'. It is not tied to a named constant, so the buffer size and the stride can drift apart silently if one is ever changed. → If the p8cc //#define support (already used elsewhere in the project) is available here, define e.g. `//#define GLOB_SLOT 64` and use it for the stride and any length guard, keeping stride and slot size in lockstep.
- _low_ · correctness · lines 64-72 (trailing-slash pattern) — If the pattern ends with '/' (slashpos == i-1), leaf becomes the empty string and gmatch("", nm) is called for every entry. Behavior then depends entirely on gmatch's handling of an empty pattern (likely no matches), which is an untested edge case rather than a confirmed bug. → Optionally short-circuit: if leaf[0]==0, return 0 (an empty leaf matches nothing meaningful). Unverified against gmatch semantics; low severity.

**Docs:** Strong documentation. The file header explains purpose, the glob_expand contract (params, return range, slot layout), the //#use ordering dependency on glob, and—valuably—the rationale for the $FA scratch page and why sharing the high TPA with the $FC00 read buffer is safe. The per-routine header restates the contract and correctly notes the P1/P2 ABI clobber constraint. Inline comments are accurate and match the code: the "stop at first pad space" note matches the 12-byte space-padded field loop, the bit-8/256 end-of-directory note matches the FNEXT test, and the dir/leaf split comments are correct. Only gap: nothing documents the (missing) buffer-size assumptions—e.g. that leaf must fit in 15 chars and that dir+name must fit in 63—which is precisely where a reader would want a cautionary note.

**Strengths:** Exceptionally thorough and accurate header documentation, including the non-obvious $FA scratch-page rationale and the //#use ordering dependency on lib_glob.; Correct single-pass scan to find the last '/' and split dir prefix from leaf; no wasted extra passes.; Name-trimming loop (lines 84-85) is correctly bounded to the 12-byte field and terminates cleanly, and nm[16] has margin.; Handles both the named-directory (FOPENDIR) and CWD (SYS_OPENCWD) cases, and deliberately keeps the empty dir prefix for CWD so relative names resolve correctly per the P8X relpath convention.; Respects the documented ABI constraint that FNEXT/SYS_DIRENTRY clobber P1/P2 by holding no pointer state live across the bios() calls, and correctly filters '.'/'..'/directories via de_isdot/de_isfile.; Match count is properly bounded by maxn (cnt < maxn), so the slot COUNT never overflows.

**Top improvements:** Bound the leaf copy loop (line 71) to 15 chars so an over-long pattern component truncates instead of overrunning leaf[16].; Bound the assembled output path (lines 90-92) to the 64-byte slot, skipping or truncating any dir+name that would exceed it, so a long directory prefix can't corrupt the next slot or memory past out.; Bound the dir prefix copy (lines 65-68) to 63 chars to close the same unchecked-length gap on dir[64].; Optionally replace the hardcoded slot size 64 with a named //#define shared by the stride and the new length guard, so buffer size and stride stay in sync.


### `os/commands/lib_rdline.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Defines readline(buf): reads bytes from the current input via nextc() until LF or EOF, stripping CR, capping the stored line at 255 chars plus a NUL terminator, and returning 1 for a line read or 0 at EOF.

This is a clean, correct, well-bounded implementation of a classic read-line loop. Buffer handling is exactly right: storage stops at n==255 and buf[255] receives the NUL, so a 256-byte buffer is never overrun while over-length lines are still consumed to stay LF-aligned. The EOF sentinel handling is careful and the partial-final-line case is handled correctly. Documentation is unusually thorough and accurate. The only notes are very minor: one unverified ISA/signedness question around the 65535 literal, and mild redundancy between the two header blocks.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · lines 28-29 (65535 comparisons) — Correctness of the EOF sentinel depends on how p8cc represents the 16-bit int literal 65535 and how nextc() returns it. If p8cc int is signed 16-bit, 65535 folds to the same bit pattern as -1; the code still works as long as nextc() and this literal agree, but that is an ISA/compiler detail I cannot fully confirm from this file alone. → Unverified — likely fine given the header's stated 16-bit-int guarantee and that both sides use the identical literal. If lib_stdin defines a named EOF constant (e.g. via //#define), use it in both places instead of the bare 65535 to make the contract explicit and avoid any signed/unsigned literal ambiguity.
- _low_ · style · line 30 (buf[n] = c) — c is an int (16-bit) and buf[n] is char (8-bit); the assignment implicitly narrows. This is safe here because the branch guarantees c is 0..255 (EOF, CR, and LF are all excluded by the surrounding conditions), but the narrowing is silent. → Acceptable as-is; optionally cast or add a one-word inline comment noting c is guaranteed a real byte at this point, so future readers don't worry about truncation.
- _low_ · clarity · whole file (headers) — The file-level comment (1-12) and the routine-level comment (13-22) both spell out buffer size, return values, and the sentinel, so the contract is stated twice. → Merge into a single header, or trim the routine block to just the in/out/ret summary and leave the sentinel/CR/truncation rationale in the file header.

**Docs:** Excellent. There is both a file header and a routine header, and both are accurate against the code: the 65535 EOF sentinel, the 16-bit-int rationale for why a real byte can't collide with it, CR-drop for CRLF sources, the "discard-but-still-consume past 255" behavior, the partial-final-line semantics, and the LF-not-stored detail are all documented and all match the implementation. The dependency on lib_stdin's nextc() and the required //#use ordering is called out. The only weakness is mild redundancy: lines 1-12 and 13-22 restate much of the same contract (buf size, return values, sentinel), so the two blocks could be merged. Nothing is misleading.

**Strengths:** Buffer bounds are exactly correct: storage is gated by n < 255 and the terminator writes buf[255] at most, so a 256-byte buffer is never overrun.; Over-length lines are still consumed (not stored) so the stream stays aligned to the next LF — a subtle correctness point handled deliberately.; EOF-first vs partial-final-line semantics are handled correctly: returns 0 only when the very first read is EOF, otherwise 1 even for a line with no trailing LF.; Single-pass, no wasted work; control flow is simple and readable within the no-break/no-continue subset.; Documentation is thorough and accurate, including the rationale for the 65535 sentinel and the CRLF-normalization behavior.

**Top improvements:** If lib_stdin exposes a named EOF constant, replace the two bare 65535 literals with it to make the sentinel contract explicit and dodge any signed-literal ambiguity.; Consolidate the two overlapping header blocks into one to remove duplicated contract text.; Optionally note at line 30 that c is guaranteed a 0..255 byte, documenting why the int-to-char store is safe.


### `os/commands/lib_regex.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A tiny shared basic-regex matcher (Kernighan "Beautiful Code" dialect) spliced into /BIN commands like grep and sed via `//#use regex`, supporting `. * + ? ^ $` and setting a global `rend` to the end of the match so callers can measure the matched span.

This is a genuinely clean, careful implementation of a classic recursive matcher, well adapted to the p8cc subset (single self-recursive function to dodge the prototype restriction, no ++/--, decls at top). I found no memory-safety bug: every `re[1]` read is guarded by a preceding `c != 0` (or explicit NUL) check, so no read runs past the terminator, and `rend` only ever points inside the caller's buffer. The two substantive concerns are behavioral rather than crashing: quantifiers are non-greedy (shortest match), which is a real surprise for sed's `s///`, and there is a char-signedness inconsistency between the masked quantifier comparison and the unmasked literal comparison. Documentation is excellent and accurate.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · matchhere (whole file); * + ? loops — Quantifiers are non-greedy: each loop tries the tail (matchhere(re+2,...)) BEFORE consuming another character, so the FIRST tail match wins and the match ends as early as possible. For grep (yes/no) this is invisible, but for sed's s/// the matched span (rend - start) is the shortest one, so e.g. s/a+/X/ on "aaa" replaces a single 'a', and s/<.*>/X/ stops at the first '>'. This contradicts the greedy semantics users expect from BRE. → Either document the shortest-match behavior explicitly at the call sites / man page with a concrete example, or make sed's caller re-scan to extend the match to the longest run. A true greedy variant would require consuming max first then backtracking, which is heavier on this target — so at minimum flag the limitation loudly rather than calling it merely 'adequate'.
- _low_ · correctness · matchhere lines 41,46,51 vs 69 — UNVERIFIED (depends on p8cc char signedness). The quantifier comparisons use the byte masked to 0..255 (`c = re[0] & 255`) against an UNMASKED `*t`, e.g. `*t != c`. The literal case (line 69) instead compares `re[0] == *t` directly (both unmasked). If p8cc's `char` is signed, a pattern byte with the high bit set (>=0x80) has c in 128..255 while `*t` sign-extends to negative, so `*t != c` is always true and a high-bit LITERAL followed by * / + / ? would never match its own character (zero-match paths still work). The `.` quantifier is unaffected because of the `c != '.'` guard. → Make the two comparisons consistent: either drop the `& 255` and compare chars directly everywhere, or mask both sides (`(*t & 255) != c`). Only matters for high-bit bytes in patterns, which are currently rare, hence low severity — but worth confirming p8cc's char signedness.
- _low_ · efficiency · matchhere (nested quantifiers) — Adjacent quantifiers such as `.*.*x` cause exponential backtracking (each `*` re-invokes matchhere on overlapping suffixes). A single quantifier is fine (the inner scan is a loop, and literal advancement recurses only ~pattern-length deep), but pathological patterns over a long line could blow the tiny P8X stack or hang. → No code change needed for normal use; optionally note in the header that adjacent quantifiers are not safe against long inputs, so command authors avoid exposing them in default patterns.
- _low_ · clarity · lines 36 and 61,69 — Minor inconsistency: line 36 derives `c` for the quantifier and NUL tests, but the terminal/literal/anchor tests (lines 61, 65, 69) re-read `re[0]` directly instead of reusing `c`. It reads correctly but forces the reader to re-confirm that `re[0]==0` and `c==0` are equivalent here. → Use `c` consistently for the `re[0]==0`, `$`, and `.`/literal comparisons (e.g. `if (c == 0)`, `c == '$'`, `c == '.' || c == *t`) so there is a single canonical value for the current pattern byte.

**Docs:** Strong file header: enumerates the supported metacharacters, states the p8cc constraints that shaped the design (single recursive function, no prototype, no ++/--), the `rend` contract, and the non-greedy caveat, and even records the deferred feature ([..] classes, \ escapes) with a BACKLOG pointer. Each routine has an accurate header comment, and the inline comments match the code (e.g. the note on why quantifier cases precede the literal case, and the `$`-only-as-final-char explanation). The one gap: the header advertises non-greedy as "adequate for sed's s///" without warning that it yields the SHORTEST match, which can silently under-replace; a one-line example would prevent misuse. Nothing misleading.

**Strengths:** No buffer-safety bugs: every re[1] access is gated by a prior `c != 0` or explicit-NUL check, so scanning never reads past the NUL terminator, and rend always points within the caller's own text buffer.; Cleanly fitted to the p8cc subset: one self-recursive matchhere() avoids the rejected forward prototype, loops use flags/while(1)+return instead of break, and declarations sit at the top — the design constraints are met deliberately, not accidentally.; Excellent, accurate documentation: metacharacter table, rend contract, ordering rationale, and the `$`-only-as-final-char subtlety are all spelled out and match the code.; Correct handling of the tricky ordering — quantifier cases are tested before the literal case so that a trailing * / + / ? correctly rebinds re[0] — and `^`/`$` anchoring is handled with proper BRE semantics (leading ^ tries only position 0).

**Top improvements:** Address the non-greedy quantifier semantics for sed: either implement longest-match or, at minimum, prominently document the shortest-match behavior with a concrete s/// example so callers aren't surprised by under-replacement.; Reconcile the masked-vs-unmasked character comparisons (quantifier uses `c = re[0] & 255`, literal uses raw `re[0]`) after confirming p8cc char signedness, so high-bit literal bytes behave consistently.; Reuse `c` for the terminal/$/literal comparisons to give the current pattern byte a single canonical name and reduce reader re-verification.; Add a short header note that adjacent quantifiers (`.*.*`) can backtrack exponentially over long input, so command authors keep default patterns bounded.


### `os/commands/lib_stdin.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A concatenation-shared helper giving /BIN commands the canonical "read an optional file argument, else stdin" input pattern, with transparent glob expansion so multiple matched files read back-to-back as one byte stream via nextc().

This is well-crafted, disciplined code for its target: it stays inside the p8cc subset, factors the file/stdin/glob logic cleanly into three small functions, and the glob-rollover in nextc() is genuinely elegant. The documentation is unusually good — the header explains the concatenation model, the $FC00 buffer choice, and the FSDIRBUF paging trick. The main real weaknesses are unchecked writes into the fixed path[80] buffer (acknowledged but not defended) and an ignored open_path() return value in nextc(); plus a couple of comments that claim behavior ("space-skipped") the code does not actually implement.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · open_path, lines 55-58 — The file word is copied into path[80] on top of the CWD prefix with no bounds check. A deep CWD plus a long name (or a long absolute argument / glob entry) writes past path[79], corrupting the adjacent globals fromfile/gfiles/gnf/gidx and beyond. The comment on lines 53-54 acknowledges the assumption but the code never enforces it, so malformed or long input silently corrupts memory rather than failing cleanly. → Guard the copy with an index limit, e.g. `while (a[j] != 0 && a[j] != 13 && a[j] != 32 && i < 79) { ... }` then always `path[i]=0`. Optionally return 2 (not found) when the name is truncated so callers see a clean failure.
- _medium_ · correctness · nextc, line 82 — open_path(p)'s return value is discarded; the comment asserts 'a match always exists -> opens'. That holds only at glob time — if a matched file is deleted or becomes unreadable between glob_expand and the roll-forward, open_path returns 2 (FOPEN failed) and the subsequent bios(FGETB) on line 83 reads from an unopened/stale stream. Behavior then depends on BIOS FGETB semantics for a failed open (unverified). → Check the result: if open_path(p) returns 2, skip to the next glob entry (continue the roll-forward loop) instead of blindly calling FGETB. This makes a mid-stream missing file behave like an empty file rather than relying on undefined FGETB state.
- _low_ · docs · open_path (line 39) and openarg (line 88) — Both headers describe the argument as 'space-skipped', implying leading spaces are consumed, but no code skips leading whitespace. If a caller ever passes a word with a leading space, the copy/scan loops stop immediately at that space and the open fails (empty name). The comment is inaccurate for the code as written. → Either drop the '(space-skipped)' phrasing, or, if leading spaces are actually possible from callers, add an explicit skip loop (`while (*a == 32) a = a + 1;`) at the top of openarg.
- _low_ · correctness · nextc, lines 77-84 (glob roll-forward with empty files) — When a matched file opens but is immediately EOF (empty file), the outer `while (c & 256)` correctly advances. This is fine, but note the first byte of the newly opened file is read on line 83 and, if that byte also signals EOF, the loop re-evaluates and advances again — correct, but only because open_path is assumed to have succeeded (see the discarded-return finding above). Flagging as a reminder that the empty-file and failed-open paths currently converge on the same untested code. Unverified against actual FGETB/FOPEN micro-behavior. → No change needed if the discarded-return finding is addressed; otherwise add a test for a glob set containing an empty file in the middle and at the end.
- _low_ · clarity · openarg, line 99 / global constants — The glob capacity 24 (line 105) and the gfiles stride 64 (line 81) are repeated as bare literals; gfiles[1536] equals 24*64 but that relationship is only enforced by a comment (line 35). A future change to any one value can silently desync them and reintroduce an out-of-bounds stride in nextc(). → If p8cc supports //#define constants (memory notes indicate it does), define GMAX 24 and GSTRIDE 64 and size gfiles as GMAX*GSTRIDE, using the names on lines 81 and 105 so the three stay consistent by construction.

**Docs:** Excellent file header: explains the no-linker concatenation convention, the openarg return codes, the glob-as-one-stream contract, the $FC00 read-buffer rationale, the FSDIRBUF/$FA paging that protects an open write stream, and even lists the BIOS entry points. Each function has an accurate purpose header, and the non-obvious FGETB "A | carry<<8" packing is explained where it matters (lines 74-75). The subset constraints are documented so future edits stay portable. Gaps/inaccuracies: (1) both open_path and openarg headers say the word is "space-skipped", but no code skips leading spaces — misleading. (2) The header claims path is the "absolute path of the current file", but for an absolute argument or an absolute glob entry it is used verbatim, which is fine but not strictly "built" as advertised on line 40. (3) No comment notes that nextc() discards open_path()'s failure result. Minor relative to the overall quality.

**Strengths:** Clean separation of concerns: open_path (build+open one file), nextc (byte stream with glob rollover), openarg (dispatch stdin/single/glob) each do one thing.; The glob-as-single-concatenated-stream design in nextc() is elegant and removes per-command glob logic — a real architectural win.; Disciplined adherence to the p8cc subset (no ++/--, decls at top, callees before callers) so commands still build on both compilers.; Outstanding documentation: the header explains the concatenation model, buffer placement ($FC00 vs stack/TPA), and the FSDIRBUF paging that keeps an open write stream's SBUF intact.; Glob capacity is internally consistent (glob_expand maxn 24 matches gfiles' 24-slot capacity), and the nextc stride math stays within gfiles bounds.; EOF handling via the FGETB carry-in-bit-8 trick is correctly masked (& 256 to detect, & 255 to extract) and clearly explained.

**Top improvements:** Add a bounds guard to the path[80] copy in open_path so long CWD+name / absolute paths fail cleanly instead of corrupting adjacent globals.; Check open_path()'s return in nextc()'s glob roll-forward and skip files that fail to reopen, rather than calling FGETB on an unopened stream.; Fix or remove the inaccurate '(space-skipped)' comments so the documentation matches actual behavior (no leading-space handling exists).; Replace the repeated 24 / 64 / 1536 literals with named constants so the glob-slot capacity and stride cannot desync.


### `os/commands/lib_streq.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Provides streq(p, q), returning 1 if two NUL-terminated strings are byte-for-byte equal (both reaching NUL together) and 0 otherwise; spliced into /BIN commands via //#use streq.

This is a genuinely well-written, correct small function. The loop-plus-final-compare idiom is the classic minimal strcmp-equality pattern and it is implemented correctly: the loop halts on the first mismatch or at p's NUL, and the single trailing comparison correctly distinguishes equal, prefix, and differing cases without ever reading past either string's terminator. Control flow fits the p8cc subset (no break/continue) cleanly, and the documentation is accurate and genuinely explains the non-obvious final-compare trick. There are no real bugs; only cosmetic and defensive-programming notes remain.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · safety · streq (line 8) — No NULL-pointer guard on p or q; passing NULL dereferences immediately. This is unverified as a real defect because it is consistent with the p8cc subset convention (callers pass valid buffers) and adding checks costs scarce function/local budget. → Leave as-is unless a caller can legitimately pass NULL; if so, document the precondition in the header (e.g. 'p and q must be non-NULL') rather than adding a runtime check.
- _low_ · efficiency · line 16 — The final `return p[i] == q[i]` re-reads both p[i] and q[i], which the loop condition already evaluated on its last iteration. This is a trivial, arguably clearer, non-issue — noted only for completeness. → No change recommended; the re-read is negligible and the current form is more readable than any micro-optimized alternative.
- _low_ · style · line 9 — `i` is a 16-bit int used purely as a non-negative index; a char/unsigned index would suffice for typical command-line strings, though int is the safe general choice. → Keep int unless local-variable pressure in a caller ever matters; not worth changing on its own.

**Docs:** Strong. File header states purpose, the //#use splice mechanism, the exact contract ("1 if equal, else 0"), and subset constraints. The inline comment on the loop is the standout: it correctly explains why the post-loop `p[i]==q[i]` decides equality (both NUL vs. one NUL vs. mid-string diff) — this is the one subtle part of the code and it is documented accurately, matching the code exactly. Nothing misleading. Only conceivable addition: a one-word note that the function assumes non-NULL pointers, but that is standard for the subset.

**Strengths:** Correct in every case: equal strings, common-prefix (one shorter) strings, and mid-string differences all resolve properly via the single post-loop comparison.; No buffer over-read: the loop terminates at p's NUL, and the final compare reads at most the index of a terminator in each string — never beyond.; Control flow respects the p8cc subset (no break/continue) and is minimal and easy to follow.; Documentation is accurate and actually explains the subtle final-compare logic rather than restating the obvious.; Equality-only comparisons make char signedness irrelevant, so it is robust for bytes >127.

**Top improvements:** Optionally document the non-NULL precondition for p and q in the header block.; No functional changes needed — the code is correct and idiomatic for the target as written.


## Commands — C

### `os/commands/awk.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A minimal awk: reads records from a file or stdin, splits each into fields (whitespace or -F separator), and applies a single optional /regex/ pattern plus a `print` action supporting $0/$N/$NF/NF/NR and string literals.

This is genuinely clean, defensively-written code for the target. Every fixed buffer (line[256], fstart/flen[40], re[80], act[120], prog[128], d[8]) is bounds-checked at each write, there are no buffer overruns or uninitialized reads I could find, and all control-flow loops make guaranteed forward progress so no infinite loops. Short-circuit `&&` protects the multi-char keyword/token look-aheads from reading past the NUL. The remaining issues are minor semantic deviations from real awk and slightly over-permissive parsing, none of which corrupt memory or crash. Documentation is a standout: accurate file and per-routine headers that explain the non-obvious subset workarounds.

**Docs:** Excellent for a homebrew utility. File header documents the supported grammar, the quote-stripping rationale, and explicit non-goals (BEGIN/END, printf). Every routine has a header, and the tricky spots are called out accurately: the 65535 EOF sentinel and 255-char truncation in readrec, the no-`%`-operator workaround in pnum, the offset+length no-copy field storage in split, and the 40-field cap. I verified these comments against the code and they match (e.g. line[256] really does hold 255 chars + NUL; d[8] really suffices for a 16-bit value's 5 digits). Only small gap: the whitespace-vs-separator empty-field/empty-line difference in split() is not documented, and the fact that an action lacking the `print` keyword still prints its items is undocumented.

**Strengths:** Rigorous bounds discipline: every write into a fixed array (line, fstart/flen, re, act, prog) is guarded, so there are no buffer overruns even on pathological input; readrec caps at i<255 and still NUL-terminates safely at index 255.; All parsing loops guarantee forward progress (every branch in run_action's item loop advances `a`, including the catch-all `else { a = a + 1; }`), so malformed programs cannot hang the interpreter.; Look-ahead into multi-char tokens (the "print" keyword, $NF, NR, NF) relies correctly on && short-circuit so it never reads past the NUL terminator.; Clean separation of concerns: readrec / split / pfield / pnum / run_action / parse_prog each do one thing, making the single-rule engine easy to follow.; Field storage as offset+length into line[] instead of copying is the right memory-frugal choice for the target, and pnum's manual digit extraction correctly compensates for the missing % operator.

**Top improvements:** In split()'s separator mode, an empty input line produces NF=1 (one empty field) whereas whitespace mode and real awk give NF=0; special-case line[0]==0 to yield n=0 for consistency (awk.c:74-85). Low impact but a real semantic deviation.; Harden the -F argument handling: `awk -F 'prog'` (no separator char given) sets sepc to the program's opening quote and consumes it, corrupting program parsing. Guard against the next token being the program (awk.c:206-212).; Tighten action parsing: the `print` keyword is matched without a trailing word boundary ("printx" is accepted) and an action with no `print` keyword still prints its items; document or restrict this so behavior is predictable (awk.c:130-132, 134-157).; Recognize NR/NF only as whole tokens; currently `print NFX` prints NF then the leftover chars because there's no word-boundary check after the two-letter match (awk.c:152-153).


### `os/commands/cat.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A Unix-style `cat`: prints one or more named/globbed files by building an absolute path and streaming via BIOS FOPEN/FGETB, or copies stdin to stdout as a filter when given no argument.

This is clean, idiomatic p8cc code with an unusually good header that accurately explains the CWD/FRESOLVE/RDBUF machinery. Control flow is clear and the three modes (named file, glob, stdin filter) are cleanly separated. The one real weakness is that the absolute path is assembled into a fixed 80-byte buffer with no bounds checking anywhere, so a long CWD plus a long filename (especially a 64-byte glob slot) can overrun `path[]`. Everything else is minor polish.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · catpath, lines 42-51 — The absolute path is assembled into fixed `char path[80]` with no bounds check. The CWD (from SYS_GETCWD) is copied, a '/' may be appended, then the argument is appended char-by-char — none of these guard against i reaching 80. A deep CWD plus a long filename overruns path[], corrupting adjacent globals. This is worst in the glob path: each gbuf slot is up to 64 bytes and catpath prepends the CWD on top of it, so CWD length + up to 63 filename chars can easily exceed 80. → Cap every write, e.g. guard each append with `if (i < 79)` and always NUL-terminate at `path[i]` with i<80; or size path[] to comfortably exceed max CWD (SYS_GETCWD's documented limit) + 64-byte slot + separator + NUL and assert that ceiling in a comment.
- _low_ · clarity · main line 80 vs catpath line 56 — Inconsistent masking: the stdin filter does `putchar(c)` while catpath does `putchar(c & 255)`. Harmless here (getchar yields a 0-255 byte or the 65535 sentinel that the loop already excludes), but the asymmetry invites confusion about whether putchar expects a masked byte. → Use `putchar(c & 255)` in both places, or add a one-line comment noting getchar already returns a clean byte so no mask is needed.
- _low_ · correctness · main line 71 — Leading-whitespace skip only strips spaces (32), not tabs; and the empty-arg test checks only 0 and CR (13). If argstr() can ever hand back a leading tab or other whitespace, the '-h', empty, and glob detection all misbehave. Unverified — depends on how argstr()/the shell tokenizes the tail. → If argstr() can emit tabs, extend the skip to also consume 9; otherwise leave a comment stating the tail is guaranteed space-delimited so the assumption is explicit.
- _low_ · correctness · main lines 84-92 — Glob detection scans arg for '*'/'?' up to the first space/CR, but glob_expand(arg, ...) is then passed the raw arg, which may still include a trailing CR or extra word. Whether the pattern is cleanly terminated depends on glob_expand's own parsing. Unverified — flagging so the arg/pattern boundary contract with lib_globx is confirmed. → Confirm glob_expand stops at space/CR itself; if not, NUL-terminate a copy of just the pattern word before passing it in.

**Docs:** Excellent file header: usage examples, the non-obvious CWD-vs-BIOS-cwd rationale, the RDBUF/$FC00 scratch placement, the carry-in-bit-8 bios() convention (lines 30-31), and exact BIOS/OS addresses. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (e.g. the `& 256` carry test, the 64-byte slot stride matching gbuf sizing). Only gap: no comment notes that `path[80]` is never bounds-checked, and the header does not state the assumed max path length, which is the code's real hazard.

**Strengths:** Exceptional, accurate file header that documents the non-obvious CWD vs BIOS-current-directory problem and the FRESOLVE/FOPEN/RDBUF solution; Clean separation of the three modes (named file, glob concatenation, stdin filter) with early returns; Correct handling of the bios() carry-in-bit-8 convention for both FOPEN not-found and FGETB EOF, documented at the call sites; gbuf sizing (1536 = 24 slots x 64) is internally consistent with the 24 passed to glob_expand and the 64-byte slot stride, and the comment states the relationship; Correct empty-file handling: FGETB is read once before the loop so a zero-length file prints nothing

**Top improvements:** Bounds-check every write into path[80] (or enlarge and document the ceiling) to close the buffer-overrun risk, especially on the glob path where a 64-byte slot is appended to the CWD; Make putchar masking consistent between the stdin filter and catpath; Nail down the pattern/arg termination contract with glob_expand and the whitespace assumptions from argstr(), documenting whichever guarantee holds


### `os/commands/cmp.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A /BIN command that compares two files byte-for-byte: it slurps file1 into an 8 KB buffer, streams file2 through the shared BIOS read buffer, and reports the offset/line of the first difference (or which file is a proper prefix), staying silent when the files are identical.

This is a well-constructed, carefully documented command. The tricky parts — the count-past-8192 over-size detection, the FGETB 256/EOF bit convention, and the prefix/EOF logic — are all correct, and the b1[] buffer is properly bounds-checked with no off-by-one. The main weaknesses are minor: an unchecked abspath write into the fixed path[80] buffer (a systemic p8x pattern rather than this file's invention), a dead parameter on openf, and a slightly wasteful ordering that reads all of file1 before noticing file2 is missing. Nothing here is a confirmed functional bug.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · main lines 75, 92 (abspath into path[]) — abspath(path, a) writes the CWD-prefixed path into the fixed 80-byte global path[] with no length bound. A long relative argument (CWD prefix + arg name > 79 chars) would overrun path[] and corrupt the adjacent globals (b1/n1). Whether this can actually happen depends on abspath's own internal clamping, which is not visible in this file. → Have abspath take/enforce a max length, or add a guard (e.g. reject/truncate when the combined length reaches 80). At minimum add a comment stating the assumed abspath contract. Marked unverified — confirm whether abspath already clamps to the destination size.
- _low_ · efficiency · main lines 79-91 — The file2-missing check (if *a==0||*a==13) is performed only AFTER file1 has been fully read and buffered. Running `cmp onefile` with no second argument needlessly slurps all of file1 before reporting the usage error. → Move the file2-presence check to right after the space-skip at line 77, before opening/reading file1.
- _low_ · clarity · openf (lines 29-33) — The parameter char *a is never used; openf operates entirely on the global path[]. Callers pass openf(path), which reads as if the argument matters. It is documented, but the dead parameter is still misleading. → Drop the parameter and call openf(), or actually use the argument (e.g. FRESOLVE the passed pointer) so the signature reflects behavior.
- _low_ · clarity · main lines 69 vs 91 — Two different usage strings: the help path prints the long 'usage: CMP file1 file2 report the first differing byte...' while the missing-file2 path prints a terse 'usage: CMP file1 file2'. Inconsistent user-facing output. → Emit one shared usage string from both sites (or a small usage() helper).
- _low_ · clarity · whole file (256, 8192, 255) — Magic numbers 256 (EOF/error bit), 8192 (buffer size), and 255 (byte mask) are repeated inline. 8192 in particular appears in the store guard, the over-size check, and the header text, so a resize means editing several spots. → Define constants (e.g. //#define BUFSZ 8192, EOFBIT 256) if the p8cc subset permits, or at least derive the guard from a single named size.
- _low_ · correctness · main lines 65, 77 (space-skip loops) — Whitespace skipping only handles ASCII space (32); a leading/separating TAB (9) between arguments would be treated as part of the filename. Likely harmless given how the shell tokenizes, but it is an unstated assumption. → If tabs can reach argstr(), also skip 9; otherwise add a comment noting only spaces are expected as separators.

**Docs:** Documentation is a genuine strength. There is a clear file header describing invocation, output forms, and the buffering strategy, plus an accurate routine header on every function. Non-obvious mechanics are explained correctly and match the code: the 256 EOF/error flag on FOPEN/FGETB, the "keep counting past 8192" trick, the v-(v/10)*10 modulo substitution, d[8] sizing rationale, and the line-counting/off+1 conventions. Comments were spot-checked against the code and none are misleading. Only gap: nothing documents that path[80]/abspath is trusted not to overflow.

**Strengths:** Correct and overflow-free 8 KB buffering: the 'count past 8192 but stop storing' pattern detects an over-size file1 while never writing outside b1[0..8191]; the n1>8192 boundary is exactly right (8192-byte file accepted and fully buffered).; EOF/prefix logic is fully correct, including the tricky empty-file cases: both-empty is silent, empty-file1-vs-nonempty triggers 'EOF on file1', and file2-shorter is caught by the post-loop off<n1 check.; Exceptionally accurate documentation — every routine has a header and the non-obvious ISA/compiler details (256 flag, modulo substitution, d[8] sizing) are explained and match the code.; Clean single-pass design: file1 read once into memory, file2 streamed once, no redundant passes; line counting is folded into the compare loop.; pnum's d[8] scratch and the off+1/line values are safely bounded (off < n1 <= 8192), so no digit-buffer overflow or signed-overflow risk in practice.

**Top improvements:** Check that file2 is present before reading/buffering file1, so a missing second argument fails fast instead of slurping file1.; Bound or document the abspath write into path[80] to remove the unverified overflow risk (ideally fix it once in the shared abspath helper).; Remove the unused openf parameter so the signature matches its global-based behavior.; Unify the two divergent usage strings and factor the repeated 8192/256 magic numbers into named constants.


### `os/commands/cp.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the shell `cp` command: copies a single file, a glob of files into a destination directory, or (with -r) a directory subtree, using BIOS read/write streams and one-level directory iteration with recursion.

A well-structured, clearly-written command that reads as production-quality for this target. Control flow is clean given the no-break/no-continue subset, helper decomposition (scopy/joinp/copy_file/isdir/copy_tree) is sensible, and the recursion-safety strategy (collect entries first, then recurse) is correct and well-reasoned. The main weaknesses are missing bounds checks on the fixed 80-byte path buffers: the SRC-word copy into patw[] and the joinp() concatenations can overrun on long words or deep trees, and the 12-byte name slots can be left unterminated for a maximal-length filename. None are likely on typical short 8.3-style paths, but they are genuine unguarded-array issues.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · main, lines 164-169 — The SRC-word copy loop `while (a[i] != 0 && a[i] != 13 && a[i] != 32) { ... patw[i] = a[i]; i = i + 1; }` has no upper bound on i, and patw is only 80 bytes. A source word of 80+ characters overruns patw[] (and the trailing `patw[i]=0` writes at index 80 for an exactly-80-char word), corrupting adjacent globals (gfiles/src/dst). → Bound the loop: `while (i < 79 && a[i] != 0 && a[i] != 13 && a[i] != 32)`. Same guard is warranted anywhere a raw arg word is copied into a fixed buffer.
- _medium_ · safety · joinp, lines 52-61 (called at 130-131, 195) — joinp() writes dir + '/' + name into out with no length check. out is jsrc/jdst[80]. During deep -r recursion sp accumulates one '/name' segment per level; once <sp>/<name> exceeds 80 bytes the write overruns jsrc/jdst, and the subsequent scopy(sp, sp0) into the child's 80-byte sp overruns too. Not reachable on shallow trees but unbounded by depth. → Pass a capacity to joinp() and stop/flag at the limit, or at minimum document and enforce a max total path length; consider skipping (with a message) entries whose joined path would exceed 79 chars.
- _medium_ · correctness · copy_tree, lines 117-121 — The name-trim copies de[] bytes until the first 0x20 pad or k==12, then NUL-fills only while k<12. If a directory entry name occupies all 12 bytes with no space pad, no NUL terminator is written in the slot, so joinp()'s `while (name[j]!=0)` reads past the 12-byte slot into the next entry (or past names[288] for the last slot). Depends on the on-disk max filename length; if names are guaranteed <=11 chars this cannot trigger. → Reserve a terminator: cap the copy at 11 (`while (k < 11 && ...)`), or use 13-byte slots (names[312], stride 13) so a full 12-char name always has a trailing 0. Marked medium because it hinges on the filesystem's max name length, which I could not verify from this file.
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 173-179 — For a single named source, `cp FILE DIR` (dst is an existing directory, non-recursive, non-glob) does not copy INTO the directory as Unix cp does; abspath(dst,a) is used as the literal target path and copy_file writes to that path directly. Behavior diverges from the documented 'Unix cp' framing and from the glob path, which does compute <dst>/<basename>. → If !g and !rec, test isdir(dst) and, when true, retarget to <dst>/<basename of src> via joinp() — mirroring the glob branch — so single-file copies into a directory behave consistently.
- _low_ · correctness · copy_file, lines 69-70 — The return value of FWOPEN is not checked. If the destination directory does not resolve or the volume is full, the code still proceeds to the FGETB/FPUTB loop and FCLOSE. Unverified whether FWOPEN signals failure via carry like FOPEN does; if it does, failures are silently ignored. → If FWOPEN reports failure (e.g. `& 256`), close the read stream and return a distinct error code so main() can print a message. Mark unverified pending the FWOPEN ABI contract.
- _low_ · clarity · copy_tree, lines 114 and 91-92 — Directories with more than 24 entries are silently truncated (the `n < 24` guard just stops collecting), and glob matches beyond 24 are silently dropped by glob_expand's maxn=24. No diagnostic is emitted, so a partial copy looks like success (return 0). → Emit a warning (e.g. 'cp: directory too large, some entries skipped') when the collection loop hits the 24 cap, and when glob_expand returns its maximum, so a truncated copy is visible to the user.
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 150, 159, 165, 171 — Whitespace handling only treats 0x20 (space) as a separator/skip; a tab (0x09) between args is not skipped and would be copied into patw or terminate parsing oddly. Minor, since the shell likely delimits on spaces. → If the shell can pass tabs, treat 0x09 like 0x20 in the skip/terminate conditions; otherwise document that only spaces separate args.

**Docs:** Exceptional for a command file. The header block explains the feature set, the single-file stream/buffer model, the cross-mount behavior, the -r collect-then-recurse strategy and WHY (global FNEXT cursor), and even lists the BIOS/OS entry addresses and subset constraints. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (verified: the &256 carry-bit checks, the space-pad-to-12 name trim, the recursion-safe local copies, the $E000 FSDIRBUF page rationale). Gaps: no routine header on main(); the header does not mention the silent 24-entry / >79-char truncation limits; nothing documents the assumption that filenames are < 12 chars (which the name-slot termination relies on).

**Strengths:** Outstanding header documentation: explains the model, the cross-mount mechanism, the recursion-safety rationale, and even BIOS entry addresses.; Correct and well-justified solution to the global FNEXT cursor problem: collect one level's entries into local arrays before recursing.; Recursion-safe by copying params sp0/dp0 into locals sp/dp on entry, exactly as needed given the shared global JSRC/JDST scratch.; Clean control flow within the no-break/no-continue subset; helpers are small and single-purpose.; Carry-bit / not-found conventions are checked consistently (& 256) and commented accurately.; Fixed arrays isd[24], names[288]=24*12, and gfiles[1536]=24*64 are internally consistent, and the collection loop does guard the 24-slot cap.

**Top improvements:** Bound the SRC-word copy into patw[] (i < 79) to prevent global-buffer overrun on long source words.; Add a length guard to joinp() (and the child sp copy) so deep -r recursion cannot overrun the 80-byte path buffers.; Reserve a NUL terminator in the 12-byte name slots (cap copy at 11 or widen the stride to 13) so a maximal-length filename cannot cause joinp to read past its slot.; Make single-file `cp FILE DIR` copy into the directory (compute <dst>/<basename>) to match the documented Unix cp behavior and the glob path.


### `os/commands/dep.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A /bin command that parses "DEP addr b b b ..." from the command tail and pokes each hex byte value into memory starting at a hex address; the write-counterpart to DUMP.

This is a clean, tight, and genuinely well-documented little program. The core logic is correct: the hex parser handles all three digit ranges, uses a safe out-of-range sentinel (99) that survives p8cc's unsigned comparison, and the two-phase parse (address then bytes) is easy to follow. Buffer safety is a non-issue because there are no fixed local buffers — it walks the argument string in place and pokes directly, so there is nothing to overrun. The weaknesses are all minor and mostly about silent handling of malformed or out-of-range input rather than any real bug.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · line 52 (address parse) and line 66 (byte parse) — Neither the address accumulator nor the per-byte accumulator has an upper-bound check. An over-long address token (e.g. "DEP 10000") silently wraps mod 65536 (writes at $0000), and a byte token wider than two hex digits (e.g. "DEP 8000 1FF") is silently truncated to its low 8 bits by the v & 255 mask.
