Both are documented as intentional wrap/truncate, so this is a design note, not a bug. → If tighter behavior is ever wanted, count digits and reject a byte token longer than 2 hex digits or an address longer than 4, printing a diagnostic. Otherwise consider adding one sentence to the header noting that wide byte tokens are truncated, matching the existing address-wrap note.
- _low_ · correctness · main, byte loop (lines 59-74) — A malformed byte token stops the whole command silently and returns 0 (success). E.g. "DEP 8000 A9 ZZ 60" pokes only $A9, then hits the non-hex 'ZZ', hits the any==0 branch on line 71, and returns 0 — $60 is never written and no error is reported. The user gets a partial write with a success exit code. → This is a deliberate 'non-hex tail -> stop' per the comment, but partial-write-plus-success can surprise. Consider printing something like "dep: bad byte" and returning 1 when a token is non-empty-but-unparseable, distinguishing it from a clean end-of-input (*a == 0/13).
- _low_ · clarity · line 40 and line 60 — Only ASCII space (32) is treated as a separator; a tab (9) is not skipped. If the shell ever passes a tab between tokens, parsing stops there. Unverified — depends on how the shell tokenizes/forwards the command tail; it may guarantee space-only separation. → If the shell can emit tabs, extend the skip loops to also consume 9. If it cannot, no change needed — worth a one-line comment stating that the tail is space-separated by contract.
- _low_ · clarity · line 75 — The final 'return 0;' is unreachable dead code. The while(1) loop on line 59 has no break/fallthrough exit — it can only leave the function via the return statements on lines 61 and 71 — so control never reaches line 75. → Remove line 75, or keep it if p8cc requires a trailing return for a non-void function to compile cleanly; if kept, a brief '/* unreachable; satisfies compiler */' comment would prevent a future reader from puzzling over it.

**Docs:** Excellent for a file this size. There is a real file header with usage examples and rationale (why it moved out of the kernel, the "writes anywhere" warning). Both routines have header comments, and main documents every local variable. Inline comments are accurate and match the code: the "32 = ' '" annotations, the unsigned-sentinel note on line 21, and "store low byte only" on line 72 all correctly describe what the code does. The only small gap: the header claims "prints a usage line for ... a missing address," but a missing/blank address after leading spaces prints the usage line (treated as empty tail), while a present-but-non-hex address (e.g. "DEP zz") prints "dep: bad address" instead — the two error paths are not distinguished in the header prose. Also nothing documents that byte tokens wider than 8 bits are silently truncated rather than rejected.

**Strengths:** Genuinely strong documentation: real file header with examples and rationale, per-routine headers, and a documented meaning for every local variable.; The hx() sentinel (99) is a thoughtful, correct choice for p8cc's unsigned comparison, and the inline comment explains exactly why -1 would be wrong.; No fixed-size local buffers at all — it parses the argument string in place, so there is no buffer-overrun surface to get wrong.; Clean two-phase structure (parse address, then loop over bytes) with a clear success/no-op/error contract that matches the documented return codes.; Handles the empty tail, bare-CR, and -h/-H cases explicitly and quietly succeeds on the common path (Unix-style).

**Top improvements:** Distinguish a genuinely malformed byte token from clean end-of-input: report an error and return non-zero instead of silently stopping with a partial write and success code (lines 59-71).; Reconcile the header's error-handling description with the two actual error paths (empty/blank address -> usage; non-hex address -> "dep: bad address"), and note the silent truncation of wide byte tokens.; Remove the unreachable return on line 75 (or annotate it as a compiler-satisfying stub).; Optionally bound-check token widths so out-of-range addresses/bytes are rejected rather than silently wrapped/truncated, if stricter input validation is desired.


### `os/commands/diff.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A small `diff` command: reads two files into fixed line grids, peels the common leading/trailing lines, and prints the differing middle block ("< " for file1-only, "> " for file2-only).

Clean, careful, well-bounded code that reads like it was written by someone who understands the target intimately. The load path is properly guarded against row and line overflow, the prefix/suffix anchoring is correct (including pure insert/delete cases where na != nb), and the documentation is genuinely excellent. The few weaknesses are minor: one unchecked bound in the resolved-path buffer (external to this file), a small ordering inefficiency, and documented-but-silent truncation limits. No confirmed correctness bug in the file's own logic.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · safety · main, lines 115/122 (abspath into path[80]) — abspath(path, a) writes the resolved absolute path into the fixed 80-byte global `path` with no length limit passed. A deeply-nested or long resolved path could overrun into the adjacent alines[] global. This depends on abspath's internal behavior, which I could not inspect, so it is UNVERIFIED — it may well cap at 79. → Confirm abspath bounds its write to the destination size, or pass/enforce a max length. If abspath already caps at the buffer size, add a one-line note to the path[80] declaration saying so.
- _low_ · efficiency · main, lines 118-121 — file1 is resolved, opened, and fully read into alines BEFORE the code checks that a second filename argument exists (line 121). If the user runs `DIFF onlyfile`, it does all the file1 I/O and then prints usage and returns 1 — wasted work, and it opens a file for a call that was malformed. → Move the `if (*a == 0 || *a == 13)` file2-presence check up to right after advancing past the first token (after line 117), before openf/loadlines of file1.
- _low_ · correctness · loadlines, line 57 — Lines longer than 79 chars are silently truncated, so two lines that differ only past column 79 compare equal in leq and the difference is not reported. This is documented as a limitation, not a hidden bug, but it means diff can report 'identical' for files that actually differ. → Acceptable for the target; consider printing a one-line stderr-style warning when truncation occurs (col hit 79 with more non-LF bytes pending) so the user knows output may be incomplete.
- _low_ · correctness · loadlines, line 54 (n < 96 cap) — Files longer than 96 lines are silently truncated at load. If two files are identical through line 96 but differ afterward, diff prints nothing; if they differ within the first 96 but the real change is later, output is misleading. Documented, but silent. → Same as above — a visible 'truncated at 96 lines' notice would prevent a false 'identical' result going unnoticed.
- _low_ · clarity · openf, line 33 — The `a` parameter is unused; the routine reads the global `path` instead. Documented, but a dead parameter is a mild smell and every caller passes `path` anyway (lines 118, 123), which reads as if it were used. → Either drop the parameter and call openf() with no args, or actually use `a` (bios(FRESOLVE, a, 0)) so the signature is honest. Given tight function/local limits, dropping it is the cleaner choice.

**Docs:** Outstanding for a homebrew command. A full file header states the algorithm, memory layout, buffer address, and explicitly names it a prefix/suffix-anchored diff (not LCS) so nobody expects minimal-edit output. Every routine has an accurate header; the flattened L*80+C grid, the EOF bit-8 convention, CR-dropping, truncation, and the 96-line cap are all documented and match the code. The na/nb globals and the sa/sb/p index semantics in main are spelled out. Inline comments are accurate, not redundant. Only gap: the file header does not warn that multiple separate changed regions collapse into one printed block (the per-routine text implies it, but a caller reading only the top comment could be surprised), and neither header notes the path[80] length assumption.

**Strengths:** Load path is fully bounds-checked: both the 79-char column cap (col < 79) and the 96-row cap (n < 96) are enforced on every write, including the unterminated-last-line flush at line 60 — no buffer overrun in this file's own logic.; Prefix/suffix anchoring is correct, including asymmetric line counts: the suffix loop guard (sa > p && sb > p) prevents overlapping the already-matched prefix, so pure insertions and deletions produce the right minimal block (verified by hand on an insert case).; EOF/byte handling is right: bit-8 masking to recover the raw byte, CR dropped for clean CRLF loads, empty-file yields 0 lines, and leq masks to 8 bits so high-bit chars don't sign-mismatch.; Documentation is thorough and accurate — every routine has a header that matches its code, and the algorithm's nature (anchored, not LCS) is stated up front so output is not mistaken for minimal-edit diff.; Sensible use of globals (na/nb, buffers) to stay within the target's tight per-function local budget, with clear comments explaining the flattened grid layout.

**Top improvements:** Verify abspath caps its write to path[80] (or enforce the bound here); it's the only unchecked buffer bound reachable from this file.; Validate that both filename arguments are present before opening/reading file1, to avoid wasted I/O on a malformed invocation.; Make silent truncation visible: emit a notice when a line exceeds 79 chars or a file exceeds 96 lines, so diff can't quietly report a truncated 'identical' result.; Remove the unused `a` parameter from openf (or actually use it) so the signature reflects reality.


### `os/commands/dir.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the P8X shell DIR command as a loadable /BIN program: lists a directory (CWD, a path, or a glob), optionally recursing (-R) and sorting by name or size (-S), by buffering each level's entries into fixed global arrays, sorting an index, and streaming the result to stdout.

This is a carefully engineered, well-documented command that shows real fluency with the p8cc subset — unsigned-comparison workarounds, a byte-wise 24-bit divmod for the size column, a char index-permutation array, and a clean collect/sort/show/walk decomposition with a globals-reuse-across-recursion scheme that is explicitly reasoned about. The core dirent iteration, sorting, and 24-bit size printing are correct. The main weaknesses are unbounded copies of user-supplied path/glob text into small fixed buffers (no length checks) and the silent truncation of directories past 64 entries, which is a real functional gap for a listing tool. Documentation is a genuine strength.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · main, lines 339 and 361 (gpat copy) — The glob pattern / file leaf is copied from the user's argument into gpat[16] with no bounds check: `while (ls < i) { gpat[j] = arg[ls]; j++; ls++; }` and the analogous file-mode loop. Although on-disk names are max 12 chars, gpat holds a user-typed pattern, which can be arbitrarily long (e.g. `DIR *ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRST`). Writing past gpat[15] overruns into the adjacent global (enam[]), corrupting entry names. → Guard both copies with `j < 15` (reserving one byte for the NUL), e.g. `while (ls < i && j < 15) { ... }`, then always `gpat[j] = 0;`. Optionally print a 'pattern too long' diagnostic when truncation occurs.
- _medium_ · correctness · collect, lines 207-217 (the `if (k < 64)` cap) — Entries beyond 64 in a single directory are silently dropped, and walk()'s sub[64] similarly caps recorded subdirectories at 64. Per project memory, P8X directories can span multiple extents / grow past one LBA, so a directory with >64 entries will list an incomplete result with no indication to the user — a real functional defect for a listing command, worse than an error. → At minimum print a truncation warning to stderr/stdout when k reaches 64 (e.g. `... (truncated at 64 entries)`), or make collect() run in chunks. If the 64 cap is a hard target constraint, document the user-visible limit in the -h usage text.
- _low_ · safety · main, lines 343/366 (dbuf) and abspath into abuf — The directory prefix is copied into dbuf[64] via `while (j <= slashpos) dbuf[j] = arg[j];` with no bound, and abspath() writes into abuf[80] — both driven by an unbounded user path. A path longer than the buffer overruns a stack local. Unverified whether abspath() itself bounds its output; the dbuf copy definitely does not. → Cap the dbuf copy at 63 chars and confirm abspath() enforces the abuf capacity (pass a max length if the helper supports one, or pre-check strlen).
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 327-335 (glob detection) — The glob flag g is set if '*'/'?' appears ANYWHERE in the path token, but the spec (and header) say only the LAST component is a pattern. For `DIR /A*B/FILE` this takes the glob branch with gpat="FILE" and tries to FOPENDIR the literal dir "/A*B/", which fails and reports 'not found' rather than treating the leaf as intended. Edge case; benign failure mode. → Compute the glob flag only over the leaf (scan from slashpos+1 to end) so a glob char in a parent component doesn't flip the mode.
- _low_ · efficiency · main, lines 387-388 (non-recursive print loop) — For the filter count, gmatch(gpat, nbuf) is called at line 387 and then show() at line 388 calls gmatch again on the same nbuf — the pattern match runs twice per entry. → Capture the match result once (e.g. `hit = (gpat[0]==0 || gmatch(gpat,nbuf)); if (hit) shown++;`) and have show() take a pre-computed 'passes filter' flag, or fold the shown count into show()'s return value.
- _low_ · clarity · walk, lines 259-289 (recursion) — walk() recurses with a 64-int local sub[] (~128 bytes/frame plus other locals) once per directory depth. Deep subtrees under -R can exhaust the tight P8X stack with no guard. Unverified how deep the target stack tolerates; flagging as a design limit, not a confirmed crash. → Document a max recursion depth in the header, or add a depth guard that stops descending and prints a notice past a fixed level.

**Docs:** Exceptional file header: usage, sort semantics, glob semantics, the buffer-reuse-across-recursion invariant, and a full BIOS/OS address map are all documented. Routine headers are present and accurate (dm10's big-to-little long division, before()'s tie-break rules, collect()'s preconditions all match the code). Inline comments correctly explain the non-obvious p8cc workarounds (unsigned '<', 24-bit divmod, char index array). Minor gaps: two inline comments are visibly truncated/dangling (eisd at line 66 and eidx at line 68 trail off mid-sentence); the -S doc claim that directories 'sort after all files' is slightly inaccurate since a 0-byte file ties a directory and then breaks by name rather than always preceding it. Nothing misleading enough to lower the grade materially.

**Strengths:** Outstanding documentation: the header explains sort/glob semantics, the globals-reuse-across-recursion invariant, and a full BIOS/OS address map; routine comments match the code.; Correct and clever handling of the 16-bit-int constraint: byte-wise 24-bit divmod (dm10/putsize24) for the size column and an explicit 24-bit magnitude compare in before().; Deliberate, correct workarounds for p8cc quirks — unsigned comparisons done via explicit byte compares (namecmp), a char index-permutation array, and countdown loops that avoid signed `>= 0` tests.; Clean separation of concerns: collect (snapshot+sort), show (print+filter), walk (recurse), with a well-reasoned scheme that lets each recursion level reuse the global buffers after copying child LBAs into a local frame array.; Streaming design (buffer-then-emit) keeps output redirectable/pipeable, and iteration runs on a dedicated FSDIRBUF page so the BIOS sector buffer stays free for the write stream.

**Top improvements:** Bounds-check the gpat[16] copies (glob pattern and file leaf) against user-supplied length to prevent overrunning into adjacent global buffers.; Signal (or eliminate) the silent 64-entry truncation so listings of large directories aren't quietly incomplete.; Cap the dbuf[64] directory-prefix copy and confirm abspath() respects abuf[80] to close the long-path stack overrun.; Detect the glob pattern from the last path component only, matching the documented semantics.


### `os/commands/disasm.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A loadable /BIN command that disassembles a half-open hex memory range [start,end): reads bytes via peek(), decodes each against the generated distab opcode table (dt_op/dt_sh/dt_mn), and prints "AAAA: bb bb .. MNEMONIC operand" lines, emitting "???" for unknown bytes.

This is clean, careful, well-documented code that reads like it was written by someone who understands both the ISA and the p8cc subset's limitations. Control flow is idiomatic for the no-break/no-decrement target, buffer handling is safe (no fixed arrays are indexed unchecked; all output is streamed byte-by-byte), and the operand-length/shape logic is coherent. The one real weakness is a latent sentinel collision (99 used as both the "not found" marker and a possible valid table index), which is dormant only because the generated table currently has 88 entries. A minor column-alignment discrepancy and a couple of unguarded parse edge cases round out the list.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · main, lines 92-98 (found sentinel) — The value 99 is overloaded as both the 'not found' marker and a real table index. found is set to i on a match; if a matching opcode ever sits at index 99, `if (found == 99)` misclassifies it as unknown and prints '???' plus advances only 1 byte, corrupting the rest of the disassembly. This is dormant only because lib_distab.c currently has dt_n = 88. The opcode is a full 8-bit byte and the table is machine-generated from genucode.OPC, so growth past 100 entries is plausible and would silently break DISASM — directly contradicting the header's promise that disasm 'never drifts from the ISA'. → Decouple the found flag from the index. Use a separate boolean, e.g. `found = 0; i = 0; while (i < dt_n) { if (dt_op[i]==op) { idx = i; found = 1; i = dt_n; } else { i = i+1; } }` and branch on `if (found == 0)`. This removes any dependency on dt_n staying below 99.
- _low_ · clarity · main, line 99 (unknown-byte branch) — The '???' column does not line up with the mnemonic column, despite the comment on lines 106-108 claiming the padding aligns them. Known instructions print 5 byte-slots x 3 chars = 15 columns then a space, so the mnemonic starts at column 16. The unknown branch prints ph2(op) (2 chars, no trailing space) + 12 spaces = column 14, so '???' starts at column 14 — two columns to the left of every mnemonic. → Emit the byte in the same 3-char slot and pad to the same width: e.g. print 'bb ' then the 4 empty slots (12 spaces) then the leading space, or simply change the literal to 14 spaces so 2 + 14 = 16 matches the mnemonic column. Then the alignment comment becomes true.
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 87-118 (range boundary) — An instruction that starts before end but extends past it is fully decoded and its raw bytes (including bytes at addresses >= end) are printed. The header advertises a strict half-open [start, end) range, but the last line can display and consume bytes beyond end. This is conventional disassembler behavior and usually harmless, but it mismatches the documented contract. → Either accept and document it (note that the final instruction may straddle end), or clamp: if addr + ln > end, fall back to printing the single opcode byte as data. Documenting is probably enough for this tool.
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 78-84 (hex parsing) — Hex address parsing accumulates with start = start*16 + d and has no digit-count or overflow guard. More than 4 hex digits silently overflows the 16-bit int and wraps, so e.g. 'disasm 10000 10010' parses start=0 without any diagnostic. There is also no check that start <= end; if start > end the loop simply prints nothing with no message. → Optionally cap input to 4 hex digits (or detect when the value exceeds 0xFFFF) and print 'disasm: address too large'. Consider a friendly note when start >= end instead of silent empty output. Low priority given the tool's audience.
- _low_ · correctness · w16 / main loop near 0xFFFF (unverified) — Reads via peek(addr + k) and w16(addr + 1) can compute addresses past 0xFFFF for an instruction near the top of the address space; behavior depends on how peek() treats a 17-bit argument (wrap vs truncate). Not confirmed to be a problem. → Unverified — if peek() masks to 16 bits this is a non-issue. If not, guard the top-of-memory case. Worth a one-line check against the peek() BIOS wrapper semantics.

**Docs:** Strong. A thorough, accurate file header explains the command, the half-open range, the distab coupling, the operand shapes and their byte lengths, and the p8cc constraints. Every routine has a purpose comment, and the non-obvious bits (unsigned-compare sentinel in hx, little-endian ph4 ordering, the i=dt_n early-break idiom, the padding column) are all called out. Two small inaccuracies: (1) the inline claim that the raw-byte padding makes mnemonics align is not quite true for the unknown-byte branch, which lands the "???" two columns early; (2) the comment "99 is out of the valid 0..dt_n-1 index range" is true today (dt_n=88) but is presented as an invariant when it is actually a fragile assumption about a generated table.

**Strengths:** Excellent, accurate file and per-routine documentation that explains the ISA coupling, operand shapes, byte lengths, and the p8cc-subset idioms actually used; Correct handling of the p8cc unsigned-comparison gotcha in hx() (sentinel 99 instead of -1), with the reasoning spelled out in a comment; Clean, safe streaming output: no fixed-size scratch buffers are indexed, so there is no buffer-overrun surface at all; Idiomatic no-break loop structure (i = dt_n to terminate early) that is clearly commented; Little-endian word reads (w16) and MSB-first hex printing (ph4) are correct and the ordering rationale is documented; Sensible factoring into small single-purpose helpers (ph2/ph4/hx/prs/ilen/w16/preg) that keeps main readable within the target's tight function/local budget

**Top improvements:** Replace the overloaded 99 index sentinel with a separate boolean found flag so DISASM cannot silently break if the generated opcode table grows past 99 entries (medium, latent correctness).; Fix the unknown-byte '???' column so it aligns with the mnemonic column (or correct the comment that claims it already does).; Add an overflow/too-many-digits guard to the hex address parser and a note (or clamp) for instructions that straddle the end boundary, to match the documented half-open range.


### `os/commands/dump.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: B**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A /bin hex-dump utility: parses a hex address from the command tail and prints memory 256 bytes at a time (16 rows of 16, hex + ASCII gutter), paging on any key and quitting on '.'.

This is genuinely well-written code for the target: tight, readable, correct on its main path, with unsigned-safe hex parsing, a zero-digit guard, safe usage/-h handling, and no buffer arrays to overrun. There are no confirmed crashes or memory-safety bugs. The remaining issues are all low-severity edge cases (silent address overflow, memory read done twice per byte, top-of-memory wrap) plus two documentation inaccuracies in the header. A strong file that would benefit from a couple of small robustness and comment fixes.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · line 60-65 (address parse loop) — The parse loop folds hex digits with addr = addr*16 + d and never limits the digit count. addr is a 16-bit int, so an argument of 5+ hex digits (e.g. DUMP 12345) silently overflows and dumps a wrapped/garbage address instead of reporting an error. The user gets no indication the address was truncated. → Either cap the run at 4 hex digits, or detect overflow (e.g. flag if a 5th digit is consumed) and print 'dump: bad address' / return 1, mirroring the existing any==0 error path.
- _low_ · efficiency · lines 76 and 83 (hex loop and ASCII loop) — Each byte is read via peek(addr+i) twice per row — once in the hex pass and again in the ASCII pass — so every displayed byte is fetched from memory twice. For plain RAM this is merely wasted work, but if any address in range is a read-sensitive location, the double read is a behavioral hazard, not just slower. UNVERIFIED: the P8X memory map (syscall ABI at $20xx) suggests I/O is via syscalls rather than read-side-effect MMIO, so this may be harmless in practice. → Read each row's 16 bytes once into a small char buf[16] (a fixed local array, no heap), then emit hex from the buffer in the first pass and ASCII in the second. Halves the peeks and guarantees the hex and ASCII columns show the same sampled value.
- _low_ · correctness · lines 76/83 (peek(addr+i)) and line 90 (addr = addr + 16) — addr is 16-bit; near the top of memory addr+i and addr+=16 wrap past $FFFF back to $0000, so a dump started high wraps around and, because the outer while(1) only exits on '.', can page forever. This is inherent to a 16-bit address space and low impact, but there is no top-of-memory guard. → Acceptable as-is for a debug tool; if desired, note the wrap behavior in the header comment, or stop paging when addr wraps below its start.

**Docs:** Documentation is above average: a real file header with a usage example, a format spec, an explicit rationale for why it left the kernel, and accurate inline comments explaining the non-obvious bits (the hx() 99 sentinel and why it exists for unsigned comparison, the MSB-first fold, the `any` guard). Two concrete defects keep it from an A: (1) the header example on line 3 reads "DUMP 7A00 ... from $6A00" — the input 7A00 and the stated result $6A00 disagree; one is a typo (likely meant DUMP 6A00, the TPA base). A header example that contradicts itself is actively misleading. (2) Line 71's comment `/* "AAAA: " */` attributes the ": " to ph4(), but ph4 prints only the four address digits; the colon and space are emitted separately on lines 72-73. Otherwise comments match the code well.

**Strengths:** Hex parsing is unsigned-safe by design: hx() returns a 99 sentinel instead of -1, and the comment explicitly explains this is because p8cc compares unsigned — a subtle target gotcha handled correctly and documented.; The zero-digit guard (any) correctly rejects a non-hex first char with an error and exit code 1, while a valid parse that simply stops at a non-hex terminator is handled gracefully.; No fixed-size arrays are indexed by untrusted length, so there is no buffer-overrun surface; the only table access HD[x&15] is masked into range.; Input edge cases are handled cleanly up front: empty arg, bare CR (13), and -h/-H, with the '-' followed by NUL case being safe because *(a+1) is simply not 'h'.; Control flow is clear and idiomatic for the break/continue-free subset: while(1) paging loop with return-on-'.', flag-guarded parse loop, no goto spaghetti.; printable-range test (b>=32 && b<127) exactly matches the documented $20..$7E intent.

**Top improvements:** Fix the header example on line 3 (7A00 vs $6A00 disagree) and the line 71 comment that misattributes ': ' to ph4().; Bound or overflow-check the hex address parse so a >4-digit argument errors instead of silently wrapping.; Read each row's 16 bytes once into a local char[16] buffer and drive both the hex and ASCII columns from it — halves the peeks and removes any double-read hazard on read-sensitive addresses.


### `os/commands/examine.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

An interactive /bin command that displays memory one byte at a time starting at a hex address, letting the user press Enter to advance, type two hex digits to write a byte via poke(), or '.' to quit. It is the interactive counterpart to DUMP/DEP and mirrors the ROM monitor's E command.

This is clean, well-structured, correct-for-purpose code with unusually good documentation. Control flow is easy to follow despite the no-break/no-continue constraint, there are no fixed arrays to overrun, and every variable is initialized before use. The one genuine gap is that the hex-address parser has no digit-count or overflow guard, so an over-long input silently wraps modulo 64K. A couple of console edge cases (a partial hex digit followed by Enter) produce cosmetic stray blank prompts. None are dangerous; the code is safe and readable.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · main, address parse loop lines 57-63 — The hex-address parser has no digit-count or overflow guard. addr is a 16-bit int, so an input like "1FFFF" silently wraps modulo 0x10000 (0x1FFFF -> 0xFFFF) and the command examines a different address than typed, with no diagnostic. Every 16-bit value is a legal address so this is not unsafe, but it is silently misleading input handling. → Cap the loop after 4 digits and reject longer input (count digits; if a 5th hex digit appears print "examine: address too large" and return 1), or at minimum document that only the low 16 bits are used.
- _low_ · correctness · main, second-digit path lines 83-85 — Typing one valid hex digit and then pressing Enter leaves a stray queued LF that is never swallowed on this path (only the Enter path at line 78 eats the queued LF). The LF surfaces on the next loop iteration as a bad digit, producing an extra blank prompt cycle. Cosmetic only, and the exact SYS_GETC echo/queue behavior cannot be fully confirmed from source — marking unverified. → If the second-digit read returns CR (13), consume the queued LF as the Enter path does, or treat CR after a first hex digit as an explicit cancel. Verify against real getchar echo behavior before changing.
- _low_ · clarity · main, second-digit path lines 82-90 — The second getchar() does not honor '.' (quit) or CR the way the top of the loop does; after a first hex digit, '.' is treated as an invalid low nibble rather than quitting. Minor inconsistency in the interactive contract, not a bug. → Document that the two-hex-digit write is atomic (a partial entry is abandoned, not a quit), or optionally handle '.'/CR on the second read for a more forgiving UI.
- _low_ · clarity · ph2/ph4 lines 18-19 and inline literals 15/16/255 — Nibble/byte mask literals (15, 16, 255) recur inline without a named constant. Most other literals (32, 13, 10, 99) are commented at first use, so readability is otherwise good. → Acceptable as-is given the tight target; a one-line comment on the mask arithmetic in ph2/ph4 would round it out. No functional change required.

**Docs:** Excellent for a file this size. The file header explains what the command does, shows an interactive-session example, and justifies why it lives in /bin. Each routine has a one-line purpose comment. The non-obvious parts are all called out accurately: the 99 sentinel exists because p8cc compares unsigned (lines 26-27, and hx(-1) does return 99 under unsigned compare); the getchar echo/queued-LF behavior is documented (lines 66-68) and the code matches it — the write path emits nl() because the typed digits were echoed inline, while the Enter path does not because getchar already echoed CRLF. The ABI note (nothing clobbers P1/P2 because no BIOS calls are made) is consistent with //#use abi being declared but unused. Only real doc gaps: nothing states the accepted address range or that over-long input wraps, and the header does not mention the '-h'/'--h' usage path.

**Strengths:** Documentation is genuinely first-rate for the size: file header with a worked interactive example, per-routine comments, and accurate explanations of the two non-obvious behaviors (unsigned-compare 99 sentinel and getchar CRLF/queued-LF handling).; No fixed-size buffers at all — parsing walks the argstr pointer directly and writes go through poke(), so there is no array to overrun; a good structural choice for this command.; All locals (d, c, hi, lo, any, addr) are initialized before use; the empty and '-h' argument cases and the no-digit case are all handled with distinct exit codes.; The write-path vs Enter-path newline handling is subtle but correct and matches the documented echo behavior; the no-break/no-continue loop stays readable via clear if/else nesting.; hx() returning an out-of-range sentinel (99) instead of -1 is the right call for an unsigned-compare compiler, and the parse loop's `while (d < 16)` cleanly doubles as the terminator test.

**Top improvements:** Add a digit-count/overflow guard to the address parser so over-long input is rejected (or explicitly documented as low-16-bits-only) rather than silently wrapping.; Handle or swallow the queued LF when a single hex digit is followed by Enter, to avoid the stray blank-prompt cycle.; Document the atomic two-digit write contract (that '.' and CR are inert once a first hex digit is entered) so the interactive behavior is unambiguous.


### `os/commands/find.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `find . -name PAT` for the P8X: recursively walks the CWD directory tree depth-first and prints the full path of every entry whose trimmed name matches a pattern (glob if it contains * or ?, otherwise a literal substring).

Clean, well-organized, and genuinely well-documented code that solves a real constraint (the single global FNEXT cursor) with a sensible two-phase record-then-descend design. The fixed buffers pat[], nm[], cn[], and clba[] are all correctly bounds-checked, and the root-path slash handling is right. The one real weakness is that cur[256] — the accumulated path — is grown during descent with no bounds check at all, so a sufficiently deep tree or long names can overrun it. Recursion frame size and the silent 24-subdir cap are secondary concerns, both partly acknowledged in the header.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · walk, lines 107-110 — The accumulated path cur[256] is grown on every descent (append '/' + child name) with NO bounds check on plen. Each level adds up to 13 chars ('/' + 12-char name), so a tree deeper than ~19 levels, or fewer levels with long names, writes past cur[255] and corrupts adjacent globals (nm/pat/isglob) or beyond. cur[plen]=0 at line 110 can also write at index 256 (one past the array) when plen==256. Nothing prevents this. → Guard the descent: before appending, check that plen + 1 + child_name_len < 256 (leaving room for the null); skip descending into that child (or stop) if it would overflow. Optionally emit a diagnostic so the truncation is visible rather than silent.
- _low_ · efficiency · walk, locals clba[24] + cn[384] — Each recursion frame carries ~432 bytes of locals (cn[384] + clba[24]*2 + several ints). Combined with recursion depth this consumes stack quickly on a tight target; deep trees could exhaust the stack before cur[256] even fills. Unverified — depends on the P8X stack size/placement, which I can't confirm from this file. The header does acknowledge 'path depth bounded by the stack.' → If stack space is tight, consider a single shared child-name/LBA scratch buffer indexed by depth, or convert to an explicit LBA stack, to keep per-frame size small. At minimum, cap recursion depth explicitly rather than relying on the stack to fault.
- _low_ · correctness · walk, line 88 (nsub < 24) — Subdirectories past the 24th in a single level are silently not descended into, so their entire subtree is omitted from results with no warning. Matching entries at the current level are still printed (the print is above the cap), so the truncation only affects descent. This is documented in the header as an intentional cap, but the user sees no indication that results are incomplete. → Print a short warning to the user (e.g. 'find: >24 subdirs in <path>, some skipped') when nsub hits the cap, so silent incompleteness is at least visible.
- _low_ · clarity · lines 105 and 144 (0xEA) — The scratch dir-buffer page number 0xEA is a magic constant repeated in two places (FSDIRBUF calls). If the scratch page ever moves, both must be updated in sync. → Define it once as a named constant via the //#define mechanism the ABI convention already supports (e.g. FIND_DIRPAGE), matching the project's 'named addresses, never raw hex' guidance.
- _low_ · style · rdname, line 62 / main, line 128 — rdname()'s return value k (name length) is computed and returned but never used by any caller, so the length work at line 62 is dead from the caller's perspective. Minor. (Not a bug — just a slightly misleading signature.) → Either make rdname void, or use its returned length at the call sites (line 91's descent copy and the overflow guard suggested above both need the length, so returning it and using it would be a clean fix).

**Docs:** Excellent, unusually thorough. The file header explains the algorithm, the CWD-only limitation, the exact reason for the record-then-descend structure (shared global FNEXT cursor), the per-level 24-child cap, and BIOS/OS addresses. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (e.g. the bit-8/256 EOF note on line 75, the descend rationale on lines 99-101). Two minor gaps: (a) the header's raw FNEXT addresses ($704A/$7070/$7047, line 18) are informational only — the code actually reads entries via de_read()/de[] snapshot, so those addresses are vestigial and could mislead a reader into thinking rdname reads them directly; (b) nothing documents the cur[256] depth limit as a hard, unchecked ceiling — the header vaguely says "path depth bounded by the stack," which understates the risk.

**Strengths:** All fixed buffers except cur[] are correctly bounds-checked: pat[] via the i<63 loop guard, nm[] fits the 12-char max name, and cn[]/clba[] are gated by nsub<24 with indices that stay within 384/24.; contains() is safe against reading past the haystack null — the inner loop stops at h's terminator because n[j]!=0 is required, so no over-read.; The record-then-descend design correctly works around the single global FNEXT cursor, and the reason is clearly documented (lines 99-101).; Root-path handling is correct: the `plen != 1 || cur[0] != '/'` guard avoids emitting '//file' at the filesystem root, both when printing and when descending.; Genuinely strong documentation — file header and inline comments are accurate and explain the non-obvious parts.; Single-pass, no wasted work; control flow is clear and readable within the no-break/no-continue subset.

**Top improvements:** Add a bounds check on plen in walk()'s descent (lines 107-110) so cur[256] cannot overrun on deep trees or long names — the single most important fix.; Surface the 24-subdir-per-level cap to the user with a warning instead of silently omitting subtrees.; Replace the magic 0xEA scratch-page constant with a named //#define per the project's named-address convention.; Consider stack-frame size / explicit depth limits given the ~432-byte-per-frame recursion on a tight target.


### `os/commands/grep.c`  
**Code: C  ·  Docs: B**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `grep`-style /BIN command: matches a basic regex (. * + ? ^ $) against a named file/glob or stdin, or with -r walks the CWD tree depth-first and greps every file's contents, printing matches as "path:line".

The control-flow structure is clean and the two-phase recursive design (gather paths, then grep, to avoid clobbering the global FNEXT cursor) is well thought out and clearly documented. However there is a genuine, exploitable buffer overrun: the per-line accumulator is guarded against index 255 while its backing buffer is only 176 bytes, so any input line longer than 175 characters writes past the end of `line[]`. A second unbounded-write path exists in the -r path collector (paths longer than the 80-byte slot stride overflow into adjacent slots / past `rfiles[]`), and the CWD path buffer `cur[]` is likewise never bounds-checked. These are the kind of latent corruptions that will silently trash adjacent globals on real hardware. Fixing the buffer-vs-guard mismatch is the priority.

**Findings:**

- _high_ · safety · grep_stream, line 61 (buffer decl line 27; also lines 50, 66) — line[] is 176 bytes but the fill guard is `if (n < 255)`, so n can grow to 255. line[n]=c writes indices 176..254 and the terminator line[n]=0 can write line[255] — up to ~80 bytes past the end of the 176-byte buffer. Any input line longer than 175 chars corrupts whatever global follows line[] (here cur[]/nm[]/rfiles[]). This is a real overrun, not theoretical: log files and source lines routinely exceed 175 chars. → Make the guard match the buffer: `if (n < 175)` (leaving room for the NUL at line[175]), or enlarge the buffer to `char line[256];`. Then fix the line-22 header comment to state the true cap.
- _medium_ · safety · collect, lines 119-127 (buffer decl line 30) — rfiles[] uses an 80-byte stride (base = nrf*80) but the code writes plen bytes of cur, a separator, then the name and a NUL with no check that this fits in 80 bytes. Absolute paths in a deep tree easily exceed 79 chars; the write runs into the next slot, and for the final slot (nrf=35, base=2800) past the end of rfiles[2880]. Silent corruption. → Bound the copy to the slot, e.g. track an offset and stop at base < nrf*80 + 79 before writing the NUL, or skip/emit-truncated when plen+namelen+2 > 80. At minimum add a length check and drop paths that don't fit rather than overflowing.
- _medium_ · safety · collect lines 149-152 and main lines 193-195 — cur[] is 176 bytes and is extended in place during recursion (cur[plen]='/' then each child-name char) with no bound on plen; SYS_GETCWD at line 193 also writes cur with no length cap. A deep directory tree or long CWD overflows cur[]. → Guard the descent (only extend while plen < 175, and stop recursing / skip the child otherwise) and confirm SYS_GETCWD cannot return more than 175 chars, else cap it.
- _low_ · correctness · grep_stream, line 51 — The main-loop match is guarded by `n > 0 && match(...)`, so empty lines are never tested. Real grep matches empty lines against patterns like `^$` or an empty regex. Minor behavioral divergence; note the final-line branch (line 67) omits this guard, so the two paths are inconsistent. → If empty-line matching is desired, drop the `n > 0` precondition and test match() on the empty string; otherwise document the intentional skip. Unverified whether the shared match() handles a zero-length target as expected — worth a quick check.
- _low_ · efficiency · collect (recursion) — decl lines 100-101 — Each collect frame holds clba[24] (48B) + cn[384] + other locals (~450B) and recursion depth equals directory nesting depth, which is unbounded. A deeply nested tree could exhaust the stack on this tight target. Unverified — depends on stack size and realistic tree depth. → Consider a max-depth guard, or note the assumed depth limit. Low priority if trees are known-shallow.
- _low_ · clarity · grep_stream, lines 52-57 vs 68-73 — The prefix-print + puts block is duplicated verbatim between the in-loop and final-line paths. → Factor into a small `emit(pfx)` helper if the function-count budget allows; otherwise the duplication is acceptable given the tight target — leave a note.
- _low_ · style · main, line 177 — Only lowercase `-r` is accepted for recursion; real grep also accepts `-R`, and the -h check on line 172 already handles both cases. Minor inconsistency. → Accept `*(a+1) == 'r' || *(a+1) == 'R'` for parity, or document that only -r is supported.

**Docs:** The file header is genuinely excellent: it documents the regex dialect, all four invocation modes, the $FC00 read buffer, the two-phase walk rationale, and the caps, plus the shared lib_regex dependency. Per-routine headers on grep_stream/rdname/collect are accurate and the phase A/B comments in collect are especially helpful. One materially MISLEADING comment: line 22 says "Lines are capped at 255 chars," which matches the in-code guard (`n < 255`) but NOT the actual buffer `line[176]` — the doc describes the bug, not the safe reality. The "36 x 80" comment on rfiles also implies an 80-char path cap that nothing enforces. Both should be reconciled with the real buffer sizes.

**Strengths:** Outstanding file-level header: regex dialect, all invocation modes, the $FC00 buffer, the two-phase walk rationale, and the shared lib_regex dependency are all spelled out.; The core design insight — that FNEXT is global BIOS cursor state, so subdirs must be stashed and recursed in a separate phase (with FSDIRBUF re-pointed after each child open) — is correct and clearly explained at both the file and routine level.; CRLF handling is correct: a \r\n pair prints once (the \n hits the n==0 skip) with no double-emit.; Regex-copy loop (lines 183-187) is correctly bounds-checked: `i < 63` stops before overflowing re[64], and re[i]=0 lands safely at index <=63.; The path-separator special-case for root (avoid "//name" when cur is exactly "/") is handled correctly in both the file-record and descent paths.; nsub < 24, nrf < 36, and the nm[16] copy from a 12-byte padded name are all correctly bounded.

**Top improvements:** Fix the line[] overrun: change the guard to `n < 175` (or enlarge line[] to 256) so the fill and NUL cannot exceed the buffer, and correct the line-22 comment to state the real cap.; Bound the rfiles[] path copy to the 80-byte slot stride (skip or truncate over-long paths) instead of writing unchecked into adjacent slots / past the buffer.; Add a length guard when extending cur[] during the recursive descent and verify SYS_GETCWD output cannot exceed the buffer.; Reconcile the two match/print paths in grep_stream (empty-line behavior and the duplicated emit block).


### `os/commands/head.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `head` command: prints the first N lines (default 10) of a named file or of stdin, parsing an optional `-N` count and a `-h` help flag from the raw argument string.

This is a clean, tight, single-pass implementation that reads well and is unusually well documented for its size. The core copy loop is correct and safe: it holds no local buffers, streams through the shared stdin library, and terminates properly on EOF or line budget. The main weaknesses are in argument parsing edge cases — an unrecognized `-` flag or a bare `-` silently prints nothing rather than erroring or falling back to stdin — plus an unguarded n*10 accumulation. None are memory-safety bugs; they are behavioral/robustness gaps.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · lines 28-34 (the `-` branch) — After seeing '-', n is reset to 0 and digits are parsed, but there is no check that at least one digit was consumed. An unrecognized flag like `head -x file` (or any `-` not followed by a digit and not `-h`/`-H`) falls into this branch, parses zero digits, leaves n=0, and then the copy loop `lines < n` is `0 < 0` immediately false — so the command silently prints nothing and exits 0. The user gets no output and no error. → Track whether any digit was parsed (e.g. a `got` flag inside the while). If none was consumed, either treat it as an error (`puts("head: bad option"); return 1;`) or fall through to the default n=10. At minimum document the behavior.
- _low_ · correctness · line 39 / lines 28-34 — A bare `-` argument (the conventional Unix spelling for 'read stdin') enters the `-` branch, parses no digits (n=0), advances past it, and leaves an empty filename. openarg("") selects stdin, but n=0 means nothing is printed. So `cmd | head -` prints nothing instead of the first 10 lines of stdin. → If this convention is intended to be supported, special-case a lone `-` to mean stdin with the default count; otherwise this is subsumed by the digit-check fix above.
- _low_ · correctness · line 32 (n = n * 10 + (*a - 48)) — n is a 16-bit signed int and the digit accumulation is unbounded. A caller passing a large count (e.g. `head -40000 file`) overflows into negative, after which `lines < n` is immediately false and nothing prints. Unverified as to whether p8cc int is signed or unsigned here, but either way large N wraps. → Cap n during parsing (e.g. stop accumulating or clamp once n exceeds a sane maximum like 30000). Low priority since such inputs are unlikely in practice.
- _low_ · clarity · line 24 vs line 28 — The `-h`/`-H` help check and the `-N` numeric check both begin with `*a == '-'`, evaluated as two independent ifs. This is correct because line 24 returns, but a reader must notice the early `return 0` to see the branches are mutually exclusive. Slightly easier to misread than an else-if. → Optional: make the numeric branch an explicit `else if` for symmetry, or add a one-word comment that the help branch returns.

**Docs:** Excellent. The file header states purpose, usage forms (file / pipe / redirect), the open mechanism, the line/EOF conventions, and the buffer address. Every magic number (32, 48, 57, 10, 65535) is decoded inline at first use, and each local is annotated. The comment on lines 44-46 accurately explains the subtle "final unterminated line still prints but is not counted" behavior, which matches the code. The `//#use stdin` line documents what it imports. The only minor gap: the header does not mention that `-N` with no following digits (or a bare `-`) prints nothing — the one behavior a reader would most want warned about.

**Strengths:** Single streaming pass with no local buffers — inherently free of buffer-overrun and off-by-one array bugs; delegates all path/FOPEN handling to the shared stdin lib.; The line-counting semantics are exactly right: counting only when the terminating LF is emitted means a final unterminated line prints without being counted, matching GNU head, and the comment says so.; `head -0` and files shorter than N are both handled correctly by the same `c != EOF && lines < n` guard.; Every magic number is decoded inline; locals are individually commented; the header covers all invocation forms. Documentation is a model for the rest of the codebase.; EOF handling via the explicit 65535 sentinel with an int-typed c is correct and clearly stated.

**Top improvements:** Guard the `-N` parse against consuming zero digits so unrecognized `-` flags and bare `-` don't silently print nothing — either error out or fall back to the default/stdin.; Clamp the n accumulation to avoid 16-bit overflow turning a large count into no output.; Document (or fix) the bare-`-`-means-stdin convention, since users coming from Unix head will expect it.; Minor: express the numeric flag branch as `else if` after the help branch for readability.


### `os/commands/man.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A /BIN command that prints an on-target manual page: it builds "/man/NAME" from its argument, resolves and opens that file via BIOS calls, and streams the bytes to stdout, printing "no manual entry for NAME" if the page is missing.

This is a clean, focused command that reads clearly and whose BIOS interaction matches its documentation. Control flow is simple and correct for the common cases (usage, missing page, streaming). The one real defect is that the name-copy loop into the fixed 80-byte `path` buffer has no bounds check, so a long argument can overrun a global buffer. Documentation is a genuine strength.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · safety · name-copy loop, lines 49-51 — The loop copies the argument into the fixed global path[80] starting at index 5 with no upper-bound check on i. An argument longer than 74 characters overruns the buffer and corrupts the adjacent global(s), and the NUL write at line 52 lands even further out. The argument comes from the command tail (argstr()), i.e. untrusted user input, so this is reachable. → Bound the loop: while (arg[j] != 0 && arg[j] != 13 && arg[j] != 32 && i < 79) { path[i]=arg[j]; i=i+1; j=j+1; } then path[i]=0. Optionally detect truncation and print an error.
- _low_ · docs · line 17 (//#use abi comment) — The comment advertises SYS_GETCWD (and RDBUF) as needed named addresses, but SYS_GETCWD is never referenced; man is intentionally CWD-independent. Slightly misleading. → Trim the example list to the symbols actually used (FRESOLVE, FOPEN, FGETB, RDBUF), or drop the enumerated names.
- _low_ · style · lines 34,36,49,63,68 — Character handling mixes numeric ASCII constants (32 space, 13 CR, 10 LF, 255 mask) with char literals ('-','h','/','m'), which is inconsistent and forces the reader to decode magic numbers. → If the p8cc subset supports them, use ' ', '\r', '\n' consistently; otherwise add a one-line comment mapping 32/13/10. Cosmetic only.
- _low_ · correctness · usage check, lines 36-37 — Help is only recognized as single-dash '-h'/'-H'; a leading '-' with any other following char (e.g. '--help', which starts '-','-') falls through and is treated as a page name, yielding a '/man/--help' lookup. Behavior is safe (reports no manual entry) but mildly surprising. Unverified whether it matters in practice. → Optionally also accept '--help', or treat any leading-'-' token as usage. Low priority.

**Docs:** Excellent for a 72-line file. The file header explains purpose, usage, the /man convention, CWD-independence, and lists the exact BIOS entry points with their calling convention. The main() header accurately explains the "& 256"/carry-in-bit-8 idiom and that puts() appends a newline. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (verified: prefix build, index-5 name slice reused in the not-found path, carry tests). The only doc gap is that nothing warns about the unchecked path length. Minor: the //#use abi comment lists SYS_GETCWD, which is never used.

**Strengths:** Header block is thorough and accurate: usage examples, the /man convention, why it is CWD-independent, and the exact BIOS entry points with P1/carry semantics.; The 'no manual entry' path cleverly and correctly reuses index 5 to print just the NAME slice of the already-built '/man/NAME' string, avoiding a second copy of the argument.; BIOS carry handling is consistent and correct: '& 256' tests bit-8 carry for both FOPEN (not found) and FGETB (EOF), and the inline comments match.; Leading-space skipping and the '-h'/'-H' usage check make the CLI behavior forgiving and predictable.; Simple single-pass streaming loop with no wasted work; well-suited to the tight target.

**Top improvements:** Add a bounds guard to the name-copy loop (lines 49-51) so it cannot write past path[79]: e.g. add '&& i < 79' to the while condition. This is the only correctness/safety issue in the file.; Decide truncation vs. error when the name is too long: silent truncation (with the bound above) is safe; erroring is friendlier. Either is fine, but the current unbounded copy is not.; Use consistent character constants (mix of numeric 32/13/10 and char literals) for readability, if the subset allows.; Optionally recognize '--help' as well as '-h'/'-H'.


### `os/commands/more.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A forward pager: copies a named file (or stdin) to the console, pausing every 23 lines with a "--More--" prompt whose key is read from the console (CONIN) rather than the possibly-redirected stdin.

This is clean, correct, well-reasoned code that handles the one genuinely tricky part of a pager on this platform — reading the paging key from the console while content streams from a redirected stdin — and documents that decision clearly. The paging arithmetic (23-line page, Enter-preloads-to-22 for single-line advance) is correct and the tricky off-by-one edge is explicitly explained. No buffers are indexed in this file (bounds live in the stdin library), so there is no overrun surface here. The only issues found are minor: whitespace/char-literal style inconsistencies and a couple of small doc gaps.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · docs · line 18 (//#use stdin comment) — The comment enumerates the stdin-lib symbols used (path[80], fromfile, nextc(), openarg()) but omits argstr(), which main() calls at line 46. A reader auditing dependencies would miss it. → Add argstr() to the //#use stdin comment list so the documented dependency set matches actual usage.
- _low_ · correctness · main, line 47 — Leading-whitespace skip only consumes spaces (32), not tabs. If the shell ever passes a tab-prefixed argument, openarg() receives a leading tab and the file lookup / -h detection fails. Unverified whether the shell can produce tab-led args on this target, so severity is low. → If tabs are reachable from the shell, extend the guard to also skip 9: while (*a == 32 || *a == 9) { a = a + 1; }. Otherwise note in a comment that only space-separation is expected.
- _low_ · style · lines 47, 62 — Character constants are written inconsistently: numeric literals for space (32) and newline (10) at lines 47 and 62, but char/named literals elsewhere ('q','Q','-','h','\r'). This makes the newline test read as a magic number. → Use ' ' and '\n' (or add a trailing comment) for consistency with the surrounding 'q'/'-'/'\r' style, e.g. while (*a == ' ') and if (c == '\n').
- _low_ · correctness · main, line 48 — The -h help check treats any argument beginning with '-h'/'-H' as a help request, so a file literally named starting with '-h' can never be paged, and openarg() is never given the chance to treat such a token as a filename. Very narrow edge case. → Optional: require an exact match (*(a+2) == 0) for the help flag, or accept the limitation and note it. Low priority given how unusual such filenames are.
- _low_ · correctness · main, lines 62-64 — Paging counts newlines, not displayed screen rows, so a line longer than the console width wraps to multiple physical rows but counts as one — a long-line file can scroll content off the top before the prompt appears. This is a known/acceptable limitation of a char-counting pager, not a defect, but it is undocumented. → Add one line to the header noting that paging counts logical lines, not wrapped screen rows, so readers understand the behavior on wide content.

**Docs:** Strong documentation. The file header explains usage, the per-key behavior, the 23-vs-24 row rationale, and the CONIN-vs-stdin design — the single most important thing a reader needs. Both routines have accurate header comments, and the non-obvious "lines = 22" preload is explained inline and matches the code. The "8 spaces" erase comment is verifiably correct ("--More--" is exactly 8 chars). Two small gaps: (1) the //#use stdin comment at line 18 lists path[80]/fromfile/nextc()/openarg() but omits argstr(), which main() actually calls; (2) the prompt() clobber note ("Clobbers A/B and P1/P2") is plausible but ISA-dependent and unverified here.

**Strengths:** Correctly solves the platform-specific hard part: paging key is read from CONIN ($0100), decoupled from a redirected stdin, so both `MORE file` and `cmd | MORE` pause properly — and this rationale is documented.; Paging arithmetic is correct: 23 lines fill a 24-row screen leaving the prompt row, and the Enter path preloads lines=22 so exactly one more line advances before re-prompting; the subtle off-by-one is explained inline.; Prompt-erase is exact: '--More--' is 8 characters and the erase string writes exactly 8 spaces between carriage returns.; EOF handling is clean and consistent with the 16-bit int model (65535 sentinel from nextc, commented).; No unchecked fixed-array indexing in this file; all buffer handling is delegated to the stdin library, keeping this command free of overrun surface.; Local count is small and every local is used, respecting the tight function/local limits of the target.

**Top improvements:** Add argstr() to the //#use stdin dependency comment (line 18) so documentation matches actual calls.; Make character tests stylistically consistent — use ' ' and '\n' instead of 32 and 10.; Document the logical-line-vs-wrapped-row paging limitation in the header.; Optionally skip leading tabs as well as spaces if the shell can pass them.


### `os/commands/mv.c`  
**Code: C  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the MV command: rename/move a single file, or glob-move every match into an existing destination directory. Since P8XFS has no rename primitive, it works by copy-then-delete via BIOS file streams.

Well-organized and unusually well-documented for a homebrew utility; the single-file path is careful and correctly guards the same-source-and-dest case. However, the glob-move path omits that same guard, which can silently destroy a file when the destination directory is the file's own directory. There is also no error check after FWOPEN, so a failed create still deletes the source. A couple of fixed buffers are filled without bounds checks. The correctness gaps involve data loss, so they matter despite the small size.

**Findings:**

- _high_ · correctness · main glob loop, lines 113-123 — The glob-move path never checks whether the computed destination jdst equals the source src. The single-file path guards this with streq (line 102) precisely to avoid deleting the only copy, but the glob path has no equivalent guard. If the destination directory is the same directory the matched files live in (e.g. `MV * .` or `MV *.TMP .` where dst resolves to CWD), jdst == src: move_one does FOPEN(src), FWOPEN(dst) truncates that same file, FGETB immediately hits EOF, an empty file is committed, then FDELETE removes it — the file is destroyed. → Inside the loop, after computing jdst, call streq(src, jdst) and skip (or report) that entry instead of calling move_one, mirroring the single-file guard on line 102.
- _medium_ · correctness · move_one, lines 54-64 — The return value of FWOPEN is not checked. If creating/truncating DST fails (destination directory does not exist, disk full, name invalid), move_one still runs the copy loop, FCLOSE, and then unconditionally FRESOLVE+FDELETE on SRC. A failed copy therefore still deletes the source — data loss. This is most reachable on the single-file path, where dst is an arbitrary user-supplied path whose parent directory is never validated. → Check FWOPEN's carry/flags return; on failure, do not fall through to FDELETE. Return a distinct nonzero code so main can print a 'mv: cannot create dest' message and leave SRC intact.
- _medium_ · safety · main, lines 89-93 — The SRC word is copied into patw[80] with no bound on i; the while loop copies until whitespace/CR/NUL. A source argument of 80+ non-space characters overruns patw and writes into the adjacent global (gfiles). The same unchecked pattern feeds abspath into src[80]/dst[80]. → Cap the copy loop with `i < 79` (reserve room for the NUL) and terminate/reject over-length input. Confirm abspath itself bounds its output to 80, or pass a size.
- _low_ · correctness · main single-file path, lines 100-105 — When SRC is a single name and DST is an existing directory, mv treats DST as a literal target filename rather than moving SRC into the directory (Unix mv would place it at DST/basename). Depending on FWOPEN behavior this either creates an oddly-named file or fails. Unverified — depends on how FWOPEN handles a path that resolves to a directory. → Optionally FOPENDIR-probe dst in the single-file path and, if it is a directory, build <dst>/<basename> as the glob path already does; at minimum document the limitation.
- _low_ · correctness · main glob loop, line 121 — move_one's return value is discarded in the glob loop, and main always returns 0 after globbing even if some moves found no source or (with the FWOPEN fix) failed to create the destination. The user gets no non-zero exit status and no diagnostic for a partially failed batch. → Accumulate failures (e.g. an error flag or count) and return nonzero / print a per-file message when move_one reports a problem.

**Docs:** Strong file header: states the command syntax, the copy-then-delete rationale, the glob-into-a-directory semantics, the exact BIOS/OS call addresses, and cross-references cp.c for the FRESOLVE-before-FWOPEN ordering. Routine headers on joinp and move_one are accurate and match the code (return-value semantics stated). Inline comments correctly explain the &256 carry-flag convention and why FRESOLVE is repeated before each op. Minor gap: the header advertises the MV-X-X refusal as a safety property but does not note that this protection is absent on the glob path, and the 64-byte stride / maxn=24 coupling between gfiles[1536] and glob_expand is documented only at the array, not at move_one's caller.

**Strengths:** Excellent, honest file header that explains the copy-then-delete design, its cost (lazy extent reclaim via PACK), and the exact BIOS/OS call addresses; Single-file path correctly refuses MV X X via streq, preventing self-destruction of the only copy; Inline comments accurately document the non-obvious carry-flag (&256) convention and the need to re-FRESOLVE before each FOPEN/FWOPEN/FDELETE; joinp correctly handles the trailing-slash edge (won't emit a double slash) and NUL-terminates; gfiles sizing (1536 = 24*64) is explicitly tied to the glob_expand maxn/stride, with a comment warning they must stay in sync; Control flow is clear and idiomatic for the no-break/no-continue subset; argument parsing (skip spaces, copy word, note glob metachar) is easy to follow

**Top improvements:** Add the streq(src, jdst) same-file guard inside the glob loop so a move into the file's own directory cannot delete it; Check FWOPEN's result in move_one and never FDELETE the source when the destination could not be created/written; Bound the SRC-word copy into patw[80] (i < 79) and verify abspath's output cannot overrun src/dst[80]; Propagate per-file failures out of the glob loop so main returns nonzero and reports which moves failed


### `os/commands/pwd.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A /BIN command that prints the current working directory by asking the OS for the CWD path string via the SYS_GETCWD syscall and echoing it with puts(); also handles a -h/-H help flag.

This is a clean, correct, well-documented little program. The fixed buffer is deliberately over-sized relative to the documented maximum path length, the argument parsing is safe against short-string over-reads thanks to short-circuit evaluation and NUL termination, and the control flow is simple and readable. The only substantive gaps are the absence of any error check on the syscall return and reliance on the OS never overrunning the buffer (a contract, not an enforced bound). Both are reasonable for this target, but worth noting. Documentation is a genuine strength.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · line 26 (bios SYS_GETCWD call) — The return value of bios(SYS_GETCWD, ...) is not checked. If the syscall can fail or return without populating buf, the subsequent puts(buf) prints whatever buf currently holds. Whether globals are zero-initialized on this target is unverified; if not, a failed/absent write could print garbage or run past the buffer until a stray NUL. → If SYS_GETCWD has a documented success/failure return, check it before puts(); otherwise, defensively set buf[0]=0 before the call so a no-write path prints an empty line rather than garbage. Marked low/unverified since in practice the syscall is expected to always fill buf.
- _low_ · safety · buf[52] / line 14, 26 — Buffer safety depends entirely on the OS honoring the 'up to 48 bytes + NUL' contract; the caller passes no length to SYS_GETCWD, so an OS-side path longer than 51 chars would overrun buf. This is a contract, not an enforced bound. → If the ABI supports it, pass the buffer size to SYS_GETCWD (e.g. bios(SYS_GETCWD, buf, sizeof-or-52)) so the OS can truncate. If the ABI has no length parameter, this is acceptable as-is but the invariant should be asserted/documented at the OS side too. Unverified against the actual SYS_GETCWD signature.
- _low_ · style · line 18 — Inconsistent character-literal style: the space test uses the numeric constant 32 while the flag tests on line 20 use character literals ('-', 'h', 'H'). The comment mitigates this, but it is a needless inconsistency. → Use ' ' instead of 32 for symmetry with the other char comparisons: while (*arg == ' ').

**Docs:** Strong file header: states purpose, the exact syscall and its number, the ABI contract (path incl. NUL copied into P1), the compile command, and on-target usage. Inline comments are accurate and match the code: the "skip leading spaces (32 = ' ')" note explains the magic number, the -h/-H comment matches the condition, and the "puts() appends a newline" and "Clobbers P1/P2" notes correctly document non-obvious ABI behavior. The buffer-sizing comment (48 bytes + NUL) justifies the 52. Only minor gap: nothing documents what happens if SYS_GETCWD fails, and the header doesn't note that a non-help argument is silently ignored (the inline comment does mention it).

**Strengths:** Excellent, accurate file header documenting the syscall number, ABI contract, compile line, and usage.; Argument parsing is overrun-safe: the -h/-H check reads *(arg+1) only after confirming *arg=='-', and short-circuit && plus NUL termination prevent reading past a bare '-' or empty arg.; Buffer is deliberately sized (52) larger than the documented max path (49), giving slack and showing intent.; Simple, single-pass, readable control flow that fits the p8cc subset cleanly (no reliance on break/continue).; Inline comments explain the non-obvious ABI details (P1/P2 clobber, puts newline) rather than restating the obvious.

**Top improvements:** Defensively initialize buf[0] = 0 before the SYS_GETCWD call (and/or check its return) so a failed or no-write syscall prints an empty line instead of potential garbage.; Pass the buffer capacity to SYS_GETCWD if the ABI allows, so buffer bounds are enforced by the OS rather than assumed by contract.; Use ' ' instead of the magic number 32 on line 18 for consistency with the other character comparisons.


### `os/commands/sed.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: B**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the `sed s/re/new/[g]` stream-substitution command: parses an s/// argument, then for each line from a named file or stdin, replaces the first (or all with `g`) regex matches of the LHS with the literal replacement and prints the result.

Clean, single-pass, well-bounded code that is genuinely careful about buffer limits and infinite-loop avoidance (the zero-length-match-as-no-match rule). Control flow is clear for a break/continue-free target and the header/inline comments are unusually thorough and mostly accurate. The main weaknesses are a doc/reality mismatch on the line-length cap and an unstated functional gap: because zero-length matches are dropped, pure-anchor insertions (`s/^/x/`, `s/$/x/`, `s//x/`) silently do nothing even though the header advertises `^`/`$` support. No memory-safety bugs found.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · correctness · re_at + substitution loop (lines 28-39, 97-109) — Zero-length regex matches are treated as no-match, so pure-anchor / empty LHS substitutions do nothing: s/^/x/ (rpat becomes empty after stripping ^), s/$/x/, and s//x/ all copy the line unchanged. This is deliberate loop-safety, but the header advertises ^ and $ as supported operators, so a user reasonably expects insertion-at-anchor to work. → Document the limitation explicitly in the header ("anchor-only or empty patterns never substitute"), or, if insertion is desired, special-case a zero-length anchored match to emit rep once at position 0 / end while still guaranteeing i advances to avoid an infinite loop.
- _low_ · docs · header comment, line 12 vs buffer decls lines 17-18 — Header states "Lines capped at 128 chars" but `line` and `out` are declared as 260-byte buffers, and the loop caps output at 255. The 128 number is not established anywhere in this file (it would come from the rdline lib) and contradicts the visible sizes, so it is at best unverified and at worst wrong. → Reconcile the comment with the actual rdline cap: either cite the real readline limit and why line[] is 260, or drop the specific 128 figure. If readline truly caps at 128, note that line[260] is oversized on purpose.
- _low_ · clarity · main, lines 78-82 — After the replacement, only 'g'/'G' is recognized as a flag; any other trailing token (e.g. s/a/b/2 or s/a/b/x) leaves `a` pointing at it and it is then consumed by openarg() as a filename, yielding a confusing "sed: not found" instead of a flag error. Also, a missing third '/' (s/a/b) is silently accepted rather than reported. → After parsing the optional flag, if *a is non-space and non-NUL, emit a clear "sed: bad flag" error; optionally require the closing '/' and report its absence, mirroring the existing "no second /" check.
- _low_ · clarity · main, line 67 (pattern parse) and analogous rep parse — An over-long LHS (>63 chars) is silently truncated at i<63; because `a` then does not point at '/', the code reports "sed: bad s/// (no second /)", which misattributes the real cause (pattern too long) and is unverified as intended behavior. → Optionally detect the i==63 / j==63 truncation case and emit a distinct "pattern too long" / "replacement too long" message so the error matches the actual failure.
- _low_ · safety · main, readline(line) at line 93 — Bounds-safety of the input read depends entirely on the rdline library respecting the 260-byte `line` buffer; this cannot be confirmed from this file. If readline does not honor a caller buffer size, a very long input line could overflow line[260]. Marked low and UNVERIFIED — no evidence of a bug here, just an external dependency worth confirming. → Confirm the rdline contract caps writes at the buffer size (and that the cap matches, not exceeds, 260). If readline takes no size argument and uses its own fixed internal cap, ensure line[] is >= that cap and note it in a comment.

**Docs:** Strong documentation for this codebase: a real file header explaining the command, syntax, semantics, and the zero-length-match rule, plus accurate per-routine headers on re_at and main and a helpful block comment over the substitution loop. Two issues keep it from an A: (1) the header claims "Lines capped at 128 chars" but `line` is 260 bytes — the 128 figure is unverified here (it depends on the rdline lib, not this file) and directly contradicts the visible buffer size, so it reads as misleading; (2) the docs tout `^`/`$` regex support but never warn that anchor-only or empty patterns produce no substitution because zero-length matches are skipped — a user-visible surprise that deserves an explicit note.

**Strengths:** Rigorous buffer bounds throughout: pattern/replacement copies gated by i<63 / j<63 with explicit NUL termination, and every write to out[] gated by n<255 so out[260] can never overflow (out[n]=0 lands at index 255 max).; The zero-length-match-as-no-match rule (re_at returns 0 when rend==lp) is a correct and well-reasoned guard that makes star/anchor patterns unable to loop forever, and it is explicitly explained.; Genuinely good documentation by codebase standards: real file header, per-routine headers, and a block comment over the rewrite loop that names each index variable and its role.; Clear single-pass substitution with correct first-vs-global semantics via the `done` flag, and no wasted passes over the line.; Sensible, defensive argument parsing (leading-space skip, -h/-H help, explicit s/ prefix check with safe short-circuit ordering) for a compiler subset without break/continue.

**Top improvements:** Either implement or clearly document the anchor/empty-pattern substitution gap (s/^/x/, s/$/x/, s//x/ currently do nothing despite ^/$ being advertised).; Fix the header's "Lines capped at 128 chars" claim to match the actual 260-byte buffers / rdline cap.; Add a distinct error for an unrecognized trailing flag so s/a/b/x doesn't get misreported as a missing file.


### `os/commands/sort.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `sort` command: slurps a named file or stdin line-by-line into a fixed flat buffer, selection-sorts the lines ascending by unsigned byte value, and prints them.

This is clean, careful, and genuinely correct code for the target. The buffer math is consistent (128 slots x 80 bytes = 10240) and every write into the flat buffer is bounds-checked, so I found no overrun and no off-by-one — the column cap, line cap, and final-line-without-LF handling are all right. The main weaknesses are maintainability nits (repeated magic numbers 80/79/128 with no shared symbol) and a couple of minor clarity/efficiency points. Documentation is a standout: thorough, accurate headers that match the code, including a non-obvious rationale for the boolean comparator.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · lines 12, 63, 73 (slot count 128) and 25/69/88 (80, 79) — The slot count (128), slot width (80), and column cap (79) are repeated as bare literals across the read loop, final-line block, sort, swap, and print. They are only linked by prose comments. A future buffer resize must edit several unrelated literals in lockstep or the code silently corrupts (e.g. bumping the array without bumping the 128 cap). → Given the memory note that p8cc supports //#define, introduce NSLOT (128), SLOTW (80), COLMAX (79) and use them everywhere, or at least keep the three literals adjacent with a single explanatory comment.
- _low_ · clarity · main, line 100 — The variable `col` is reused as the print-loop base index (col = i*80) after having meant 'current column within a line' during the read loop. Same name, two unrelated meanings, which mildly obscures the print loop. → Use a distinct name for the base offset (e.g. `base = i * 80`) in the print loop; `j` already serves as the intra-line index.
- _low_ · correctness · main, line 51 — Help detection matches any argument whose first two chars are '-h'/'-H' (so '-help', '-hxyz' also trigger usage), while any other leading-dash token (e.g. '-x') falls through to openarg and reports 'sort: not found', which is a slightly misleading message for an unknown flag. Not a bug, just imprecise arg handling. → Optionally verify the flag is exactly '-h'/'-H' (check *(arg+2) == 0 or a space), and/or emit 'sort: bad option' for other leading-dash args. Low priority for this toolset.
- _low_ · efficiency · lless, lines 25-26 — `x * 80` and `y * 80` are recomputed on every iteration of the compare loop. Minor, but this comparator is the hot path of an O(n^2) sort so the multiply runs often on a machine where multiply is not cheap. → Hoist the bases once before the loop: `bx = x * 80; by = y * 80;` then index `lines[bx + i]`. Unverified whether p8cc already strength-reduces this; if it does, disregard.
- _low_ · correctness · read loop / final-line block, lines 63-76 — Input beyond the 128-line cap (or beyond 79 columns) is silently discarded with no diagnostic on the output or a nonzero return. This is documented intended behavior, not a bug, but a user piping a >128-line stream gets a truncated result with no warning. → Consider emitting a one-line 'sort: input truncated' notice (to stderr-equivalent if available) when the cap is hit, so silent data loss is visible. Purely optional given the stated design.

**Docs:** Excellent. File header states the interface, the capacity limits (128 lines x 79 chars), truncation/drop behavior, and even the historical buffer size. Both routines have accurate header comments.
