The lless comment explaining WHY it returns a boolean instead of -1/0/1 (p8cc `<` is unsigned, so a negative sentinel tested `< 0` never fires) is exactly the kind of non-obvious reasoning worth recording, and it matches the code. Inline notes on the EOF sentinel (65535), CR-drop, and column cap are correct. Only gap: the capacity constants live only in prose, not tied to the literals in code, so a future buffer resize could silently desync the comments.

**Strengths:** Every write into the flat buffer is bounds-checked (col < 79 before store, nline < 128 in both the loop guard and the final-line block), so there is no buffer overrun and no off-by-one — the NUL always lands at index <= 79 within an 80-byte slot.; Correct handling of the tricky edge cases: empty lines are preserved, CR is dropped, a final line with no trailing LF is flushed, and over-length lines are truncated (not merged into the next line).; Selection sort is a sound choice here — it minimizes the expensive 80-byte slot swaps to at most n-1, and the comment explains the deliberate avoidance of an index array.; The lless comparator correctly masks bytes to 0..255 and returns a boolean specifically to sidestep p8cc's unsigned `<`, with a comment documenting exactly that hazard.; Control flow is clean and idiomatic for the no-break/no-continue subset; buffer arithmetic is consistent (128*80 = 10240) and the globals are zero-initialized so trailing slot bytes are benign.

**Top improvements:** Replace the repeated magic numbers (128, 80, 79) with //#define constants so a buffer resize can't desync the cap checks or the doc comments.; Rename the reused `col` in the print loop to a dedicated base-offset variable for readability.; Hoist `x*80`/`y*80` out of the lless compare loop (the sort's hot path) if p8cc doesn't already do it.; Optionally surface silent truncation (>128 lines or >79 cols) with a brief notice instead of dropping data invisibly.


### `os/commands/tail.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the `tail` command: prints the last N lines (default 10, clamped 1..40) of a named file or stdin, using a fixed 40-slot ring buffer of 256-byte line slots.

This is a clean, well-structured implementation with genuinely excellent documentation. The ring-buffer arithmetic and the oldest-line/base computation in the print phase are correct across the cases I traced (fewer-than-N, exactly-N, wrapped). Buffer indexing is safely bounds-checked and cannot overrun. The one real defect is a signed-16-bit overflow of `total` on inputs exceeding 32767 lines, which silently prints nothing. A few minor clarity nits (mixed literal styles) round out the review.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · line 64 / 75 (total counter) — `total` is a signed 16-bit int incremented once per line. On input with >32767 lines it overflows to negative. The print phase then computes count=total (negative) and `count > n` is false, so count stays negative and the `while (count > 0)` loop never runs — tail silently prints nothing instead of the last N lines. `base` is also left at 0 since `total > n` is false. → total only needs to distinguish total<=n from total>n. Stop counting once it exceeds n, e.g. `if (total <= n) total = total + 1;` (or cap at n+1). Then it can never overflow and the print-phase tests remain correct for arbitrarily large inputs.
- _low_ · clarity · line 45 vs 38 — Mixed constant styles: digit parsing uses decimal 48/57 (with a comment) while the -h check uses char literals '-','h','H'. Space is 32, LF 10, CR 13 all decimal. This is internally inconsistent and slightly harder to scan. → Prefer char literals throughout where the subset supports them: '0'/'9' for the digit range, ' ' for space, '\n'/'\r' for LF/CR — matching the '-'/'h' style already used. Purely cosmetic; behavior is unchanged.
- _low_ · correctness · line 45 (-N with no digits) — `TAIL -` or `TAIL -file` enters the -N branch, parses zero digits, leaves n=0 (clamped up to 1), then feeds the remainder to openarg. `-file` would attempt to open a file literally named 'file' (dash consumed). This is a minor argument-parsing quirk, not a crash, and unverified against how the shell/openarg treat such tokens. → Optional: if no digits were consumed after '-', treat the token as an error or as a filename rather than silently swallowing the dash. Low priority given realistic usage.
- _low_ · correctness · line 60 (c != 65535) — EOF is tested against 65535 while c/nextc are 16-bit ints; whether the literal 65535 compares equal to nextc()'s sentinel depends on p8cc's signed/unsigned handling of the constant. Unverified — this appears to be an established project idiom (matches the comment), so likely fine. → No change needed if this matches the convention used by other stdin-lib consumers (e.g. cat.c). Flagging only because I cannot confirm the compiler's 16-bit literal comparison from this file alone.

**Docs:** Outstanding for a homebrew command. File header states usage, behavior, defaults, and the 255-char/CR/LF handling. The buf declaration carries a precise slot-layout explanation (stride fixed at 256 regardless of N). main has a real routine header, and the print-phase comment correctly explains why `base=slot` is the oldest surviving line after a wrap. Inline comments are accurate and match the code (verified: line 63 wrap/overwrite, line 68 cap-leaving-room-for-NUL, line 82/83 count/base). Magic constants (32/48/57/10/13/65535) are each annotated at point of use. The only doc gap: nothing warns about the large-file line-count limit noted below.

**Strengths:** Ring-buffer indexing is fully bounds-checked: col is capped at <255 before store and the NUL lands at index <=255, so max byte index is 39*256+255 = 10239, exactly within buf[10240] — no overrun possible.; Print-phase base/count logic is correct across all traced cases (total<n, total==n where slot wraps exactly to 0, and total>n where base=slot is the oldest survivor).; Documentation is exemplary: accurate file header, a real routine header, and inline comments that explain the non-obvious ring wrap and oldest-line math rather than restating code.; Single-pass streaming with O(N) print and fixed memory — appropriate and efficient for the target.; Sensible input hygiene: leading-space skipping, -h/-H help, CR stripping, LF-terminated and final-unterminated-line handling all covered.

**Top improvements:** Fix the `total` 16-bit overflow by clamping the counter at n+1 (or only incrementing while total<=n) so very large inputs still print the last N lines.; Unify numeric constants to char literals ('0'/'9'/' '/'\n'/'\r') for consistency with the '-'/'h' style already present.; Optionally harden the -N branch so a dash with no following digits is not silently consumed.; Add one line to the header noting the correctness fix makes line count unbounded (once total is clamped), documenting the previously implicit limit.


### `os/commands/touch.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the TOUCH command: for each whitespace-separated name on the command tail, absolutizes it and creates a zero-byte file only if it does not already exist, leaving existing files completely untouched.

A small, clean, well-documented command that reads clearly and gets the core "create-only-if-missing" semantics right. The header and inline comments are unusually good and, as far as I can verify, accurate. The one substantive concern is that exists() opens the file with FOPEN on the "found" path but never FCLOSEs it, which is a plausible file-handle/stream leak for every already-existing argument. There is also a minor redundant FRESOLVE. Nothing here looks like a hard correctness bug in the create logic itself.

**Findings:**

- _medium_ · correctness · exists() (lines 29-33) — On the file-exists path, FOPEN succeeds but the function returns 1 without ever calling FCLOSE, so the just-opened read stream is left open. Every already-existing name passed to TOUCH leaks/keeps an open handle; touching many existing files (e.g. `TOUCH A B C D ...`) could exhaust stream slots or leave stale FS state for later commands. → Close the stream before returning success: `if (bios(FOPEN, RDBUF, 0) & 256) { return 0; } bios(FCLOSE, 0, 0); return 1;`. Verify FCLOSE's expected argument for a read stream against the other commands that FOPEN for reading.
- _low_ · efficiency · main() lines 62-64 vs exists() line 30 — For a missing file, FRESOLVE is executed twice: once inside exists() before the failing FOPEN, then again at line 63 before FWOPEN. The resolve state (DIRLBA+FNAME) from the exists() call is likely still valid after a failed FOPEN, making the second FRESOLVE redundant. → If FOPEN failure is confirmed not to disturb the FRESOLVE globals, drop line 63 and let FWOPEN reuse the resolved name. Keep it only if FOPEN is known to clobber the resolve state — in which case a one-line comment saying so would justify the re-resolve. Marked low; depends on BIOS behavior I cannot fully confirm.
- _low_ · safety · main() line 58, global path[80] — abspath(path, a) writes the absolutized name into the fixed 80-byte global with no visible length cap in this file; a pathological long path word could overflow path[]. Whether this is safe depends entirely on abspath capping its output. → Unverified here — confirm abspath bounds its write to the destination buffer (ideally taking a size, or documenting an 80-byte guarantee). If it does not, that is a shared-lib issue worth fixing there rather than in touch.c.
- _low_ · correctness · main() loop condition line 54 / abspath delimiter handling — The loop treats both 0 and 13 (CR) as terminators, but it relies on abspath to stop a word at CR as well as space/NUL. If abspath only delimits on space/NUL, a trailing CR could be pulled into the final filename. Unverified — depends on abspath's word-splitting. → Confirm abspath treats CR (13) as a word delimiter, matching the loop's own terminator set. If it does not, normalize or strip a trailing CR before use.

**Docs:** Excellent documentation. The file header explains purpose, the truncation-vs-create distinction, the deliberate no-globbing decision, usage examples, and even the BIOS/OS addresses with their carry-flag convention. Both routines have accurate function headers, and the tricky bits (the `& 256` carry test, the abspath consume-and-advance contract, why FRESOLVE precedes FWOPEN) are all commented and match the code. Only small gap: exists() does not document that a successful FOPEN is left open (no FCLOSE), which is precisely where a reader would want a note; and the header lists FWOPEN/FCLOSE addresses but does not mention the missing close in exists().

**Strengths:** Correct core semantics: existing files are genuinely left untouched (no truncation), which is the meaningful difference from a plain create and is explicitly called out.; Outstanding documentation — header explains rationale (including why no globbing), usage, and BIOS/OS addresses; inline comments accurately match the code.; Clean control flow within the p8cc no-break/no-continue subset: leading-space skip, usage/-h short-circuit, and per-word advance are all clear and correctly ordered.; Good use of named ABI symbols (FRESOLVE, FOPEN, FWOPEN, FCLOSE, RDBUF, SYS_GETCWD via abspath) instead of raw hex, per project convention.; Magic numbers (32 space, 13 CR, 256 carry bit) are consistently explained where used.

**Top improvements:** Add an FCLOSE in exists() after a successful FOPEN to avoid leaking the read stream for every existing filename.; Eliminate the redundant second FRESOLVE for the create path (or comment why it is required) after confirming FOPEN-failure does not clobber resolve state.; Verify abspath bounds its output to path[80] and treats CR as a word delimiter; add a brief note or guard if either is not guaranteed.


### `os/commands/tree.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

Implements the TREE command: a depth-first, indented listing of the directory tree rooted at the current working directory, analogous to Unix `tree`.

This is a clean, careful implementation that correctly works around the single global FNEXT cursor via a deliberate two-pass (record-then-descend) design, and its fixed sub[] buffer is properly bounds-checked before every write. Documentation is unusually strong — the header explains the WHY (global cursor forcing two passes) rather than restating the code, and per-routine comments are accurate. The main weaknesses are minor: a duplicated magic literal (24) that could desync, a couple of stale addresses in the header block, and unbounded recursion with a fat stack frame that has no depth guard. No confirmed correctness bugs.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · clarity · walk (lines 41 and 56) — The per-level cap 24 is a magic literal duplicated in three places: the array dimension sub[24], the bound check nsub < 24, and the header comment. If the array size is ever changed, the guard must be changed in lockstep or it silently over/under-runs (a raised array size with a stale guard just drops children; a lowered array size with a stale guard overruns sub[]). → Introduce a single named constant (e.g. //#define MAXSUB 24) used for both the declaration and the check so they cannot desync. If the subset can't express that here, at minimum add a comment on line 56 noting it must match the sub[] dimension.
- _low_ · docs · file header, lines 10-11 — The BIOS/OS address list names FOPENDIRAT=$0142 and SYS_CWDLBA=$2006, but the code opens directories via SYS_OPENCWD (line 82) and de_opendir (line 64), and aims the buffer with FSDIRBUF. The cited symbols don't appear in the file, making the header slightly misleading about which calls are actually used. → Reconcile the address block with the actual calls: document SYS_OPENCWD, FSDIRBUF, de_opendir/de_read/de_isdir/de_lba, or drop the raw-address list and defer to the //#use abi / //#use dirent equates that the code relies on.
- _low_ · safety · walk (recursion, lines 40-68) — Recursion depth is unbounded and each frame allocates a 24-int (48-byte) sub[] plus locals. A deeply nested directory tree consumes stack proportional to depth with a large per-frame cost, and there is no depth cap. On a tight target this risks stack exhaustion with no graceful failure. UNVERIFIED — I cannot confirm the P8X stack size or how p8cc lays out these frames, so this may be a non-issue in practice. → Consider a MAXDEPTH guard that stops descending (and optionally prints an indicator) beyond a safe level, sizing it against the known stack budget. At minimum, note the depth-vs-stack tradeoff in the walk header.
- _low_ · correctness · walk pass 2, line 64 — The return value of de_opendir(sub[i]) (and bios(FSDIRBUF,...)) is not checked. If a recorded child LBA is unopenable, the subsequent FNEXT loop iterates against whatever is currently in the $EA scratch page rather than failing cleanly. UNVERIFIED — depends on de_opendir/FNEXT behavior on a bad handle, which I can't confirm. → If de_opendir returns a status, check it and skip the child (and continue) on failure rather than walking a possibly-stale buffer.
- _low_ · style · putchar calls (lines 25, 52, 58, and 55/57) — Character constants are expressed inconsistently: space and newline are written as decimals 32 and 10, while '/' is written as a char literal. This mixes two conventions for the same kind of value within one small file and hurts readability (e.g. putchar(10) vs putchar('/')). → Pick one convention — prefer char literals '\n', ' ', '/' where the subset supports them — or add brief inline notes (as line 51 does for the indent). Purely cosmetic.
- _low_ · correctness · putname, lines 23-27 — putname drops every byte equal to ASCII 32, not just trailing padding. This is correct only under the invariant that dirent names never contain an interior space; a name with an embedded space would be printed with the space silently removed. The routine's own comment frames these as 'padding spaces', which is the assumed-but-unstated invariant. → If interior spaces are truly impossible in this filesystem, no change is needed but state that invariant explicitly. If they are possible, print the 12 chars and trim only trailing spaces (e.g. scan from the end to find the last non-space index, then emit up to it).

**Docs:** Excellent. Full file header with usage, an explicit rationale for the two-pass structure (the single global FNEXT cursor), and the per-level cap. Each routine has an accurate header stating preconditions, what globals it reads/clobbers, and side effects; the bit-8/256 end-of-dir comment matches the code, and the '.'/'..' skip is explained. The one blemish: the header address list (line 10-11) cites FOPENDIRAT=$0142 and SYS_CWDLBA=$2006, but the code actually calls SYS_OPENCWD and FSDIRBUF — the header names some symbols that never appear in the source, so it reads as slightly stale/mismatched relative to the //#use abi + de_* API the code truly uses.

**Strengths:** The two-pass design (record child LBAs in pass 1, re-open and descend in pass 2) correctly and cleanly sidesteps the single global FNEXT cursor — the central hazard of this task — and the header explains exactly why it must be done this way.; Every write to the fixed sub[] buffer is guarded by nsub < 24 before the store, so the fixed-size array cannot overrun; overflow degrades gracefully to dropped entries.; Documentation quality is high: a real file header with usage, accurate per-routine contracts (preconditions, globals read/clobbered, side effects), and correct explanation of the bit-8 end-of-dir test.; The -h/-H help parse is overrun-safe: because C short-circuits, *(a+1) is only read after confirming *a=='-', and the string's null terminator makes the second dereference well-defined even for a bare "-".; Control flow is clear and idiomatic for the no-break/no-continue subset — simple while loops with explicit index increments and a clean flag-based end-of-dir test.

**Top improvements:** Replace the duplicated literal 24 with a single named constant used for both the sub[] dimension and the nsub bound so the buffer size and its guard cannot drift apart.; Fix the header address block to match the calls the code actually makes (SYS_OPENCWD / FSDIRBUF / de_* API) rather than FOPENDIRAT / SYS_CWDLBA, which never appear.; Add a MAXDEPTH recursion guard (or at least document the stack cost of the 24-int frame per level) to bound worst-case stack use on deep trees.; Standardize putchar constants on one convention (char literals preferred) for readability.


### `os/commands/uniq.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `uniq` command that collapses adjacent duplicate lines from a named file or stdin, printing each line only when it differs from the preceding one.

This is a clean, correct, and unusually well-documented implementation for the target. The control flow is simple and right: the `first` flag correctly forces the initial line to print, buffers are generously sized relative to the documented rdline cap, and the argument/usage handling is careful about NUL terminators. I found no correctness bugs — only a minor doc/comment inaccuracy and a trivial redundant-copy micro-inefficiency. It reads like reference code for the subset.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · docs · lines 6-7, 21-22, 51-52 — Comments say the line is compared to the 'previously printed line', but the code updates `prev` on every iteration (lines 55-57), i.e. it compares to the previously *read* line. Behavior is identical for adjacent-dup collapse, so this is a wording mismatch, not a bug. → Reword to 'previous line read' / 'previous line' to match the algorithm exactly.
- _low_ · efficiency · main (lines 55-57) — `prev` is rebuilt from `cur` every iteration even when the line was a duplicate (cur already equals prev), doing a redundant byte copy on every repeated line. → Only copy cur->prev when the lines differ (the streq==0 branch). Since prev already equals cur on a duplicate, this saves the copy with no behavioral change. Minor; current code is perfectly correct.
- _low_ · correctness · main (line 53) — Correct handling of the first line relies on either short-circuit evaluation of `first || streq(...)` OR on `prev` being an empty string initially. p8cc's `||` short-circuiting is unverified here, but since `prev` is a file-scope global it is zero-initialized to "", so streq against it is safe on the first iteration regardless. Flagging only to note the dependency; no actual defect. → No change needed. Optionally add a one-line comment that `prev` starts empty via global zero-init, which is why the first-iteration streq (if evaluated) is safe and why the `first` flag is still required to emit an initial blank line.

**Docs:** Excellent. File header states purpose, usage, the SORT|UNIQ idiom, source of input, and the 128-char cap; the buffer globals, main's contract, every local variable, the space-skip, the -h path, and the openarg return-code contract are all explained. One inaccuracy: the header (lines 6-7) and main's comment (lines 21-22, 51-52) describe comparison against the "previously printed line," but the code actually compares against the previously *read* line (prev is updated every iteration, line 55-57, not only when printed). For adjacent-duplicate collapse the two descriptions yield identical behavior, so it is harmless, but the comment should say "previously read/emitted line" to match the code. Everything else is accurate.

**Strengths:** The `first` flag is genuinely necessary and correctly placed: it ensures an initial empty/blank line still prints, which a naive prev=="" comparison would wrongly suppress.; Buffer safety is sound — 260-byte buffers against a documented 128-char rdline cap give ample headroom for data+NUL, and the manual cur->prev copy is bounded by cur's NUL terminator.; Argument parsing is careful: skipping spaces then testing `*a=='-' && *(a+1)=='h'` never reads past the terminator because the NUL short-circuits naturally.; Documentation is thorough and mostly accurate, including the non-obvious openarg return-code contract and the streq 'nonzero means equal' inversion.; Magic numbers are either documented (32 = space, 128/260 caps) or expressed as char literals ('-', 'h').

**Top improvements:** Fix the 'previously printed line' comments to say 'previous line read' so the docs match the actual algorithm.; Optionally skip the cur->prev copy on the duplicate branch to avoid a redundant per-line copy (pure micro-optimization).; Consider a brief comment noting `prev` relies on global zero-init to an empty string, clarifying the first-iteration safety.


### `os/commands/vi.c`  
**Code: B  ·  Docs: B**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A minimal modal, vi-flavoured VT100 screen editor for the P8X: loads a file into a flat fixed-width line buffer, drives the serial console with raw ANSI escapes, and supports basic normal/insert editing, single-level undo, and literal substring search.

Genuinely solid, readable code that fits the target's constraints well: nlines and inschar are bounds-guarded, the redraw model is deliberately cheap for serial baud, and the op-based single-level undo is a clever memory-frugal design. However there are two real behavioral bugs in the search path (no scroll to off-screen matches; empty '/' destroys the saved pattern) plus a smaller undo edge case, and the code leans heavily on scattered magic numbers (80/110/78/22/23) that the p8cc //#define support could name.

**Findings:**

- _high_ · correctness · main, '/' and 'n' handlers (lines 425, 431); search()/findfrom() lines 347-350, 333-346 — search() sets cy/cx to a match anywhere in the buffer but neither the '/' nor the 'n' handler calls scroll() before redraw(). If the match is below top+22 (or above top after a wrap), redraw() still paints rows top..top+22 with the old top, the matched line is never shown, and placecur() computes gotoxy(cy-top+1, ...) with a row of 24+ — the cursor lands on the status line or off-screen. Searching to any off-screen match is broken. → After a successful search, call scroll() (which recenters top) before redraw(), exactly as the G/j/k and undo handlers already do: e.g. `if (search()) { scroll(); redraw(); }` in both the '/' and 'n' branches.
- _medium_ · correctness · getpat() line 363, used by '/' handler line 424 — On an empty '/' entry (k==0), getpat() unconditionally executes pat[k]=0 -> pat[0]=0, wiping any previously entered pattern. Because havepat stays 1 from a prior search, the '/' handler then calls search() with an empty pat; matchat() returns 1 immediately (pat[0]==0), so the cursor just jumps one char forward instead of repeating the last search. This directly contradicts the line 364 comment 'empty / repeats the last pattern.' → Only overwrite pat when k>0. Guard the terminator: `if (k > 0) { pat[k] = 0; havepat = 1; }` and leave the old pattern intact when the user enters an empty '/'. (Then empty '/' will genuinely repeat via the existing search().)
- _low_ · correctness · main 'dd' handler line 408 + delline() line 230 + undo() t==3 line 316 — When nlines==1, delline() clears line[0] but does NOT decrement nlines (stays 1). A 'dd' on that last remaining line sets uop=3 for reinsert; undoing it calls insline(uy=0), which shifts and bumps nlines 1->2. The buffer now has the restored line plus the leftover blank line — two lines where the original had one. → Make dd-undo aware of the clear-in-place case, e.g. when delline() only cleared a single line, use a restore-line undo (uop 1 with usave) instead of reinsert (uop 3); or have undo t==3 skip the insert when the delete didn't actually remove a slot.
- _low_ · correctness · docmd() lines 277-280 — Command matching only checks cmd[0] and cmd[1], so ':q!xyz', ':wqABC', ':wX' etc. are accepted as q!/wq. Harmless today but silently ignores garbage the user may have meant as something else. → Terminate each match, e.g. `cmd[1]=='!' && cmd[2]==0` for q!, and `cmd[1]=='q' && cmd[2]==0` for wq; report '?unknown command' otherwise.
- _low_ · correctness · clampx() line 176-182 vs 'l' movement line 410 — clampx allows cx up to llen (one past the last char), and j/k/G call clampx, so after a vertical move the cursor can rest one column past end. The 'l' key, by contrast, caps at llen-1. Inconsistent normal-mode cursor semantics; the one-past position also makes placecur draw the cursor one column beyond the text. → Decide on vi semantics (cursor on the last char in normal mode) and clamp uniformly, e.g. clampx to llen-1 when the line is non-empty; keep the one-past behavior only for insert/append entry points.
- _low_ · correctness · search() line 348 -> findfrom() line 338 — UNVERIFIED edge case: findfrom is called with sx=cx+1. If cx==llen (cursor one past end, reachable via the clampx inconsistency above), j starts past the line's NUL and the `while (line[b+j]!=0)` test reads slot bytes beyond the terminator, which may hold stale nonzero data from a previously longer line and cause a spurious scan. Not a memory-safety issue (stays within the 80-byte slot) but can mis-scan. → Clamp the search start: if cx+1 > llen(cy), begin the scan at the next line, or have findfrom treat any j at/after the NUL as end-of-line.
- _low_ · docs · header line 27 vs load() line 102 vs inschar() line 189 — The header states lines over 78 chars are truncated, but load() accepts up to 79 chars (col<79) while inschar() refuses to grow a line at n>=78. Three different effective limits; a 79-char loaded line also can never be edited by inschar. → Pick one line-length limit, apply it in both load() and inschar(), and make the header match.
- _low_ · clarity · whole file — Structural constants 80 (stride), 110 (max lines), 78 (max line len), 22/23 (text rows), 24 (status row), 38/40 (cmd/pat sizes) are hard-coded in many places. A future resize or buffer-size change means hunting every literal, and the 22-vs-23-vs-24 row relationships are easy to get subtly wrong. → Given p8cc's //#define support (per the project's abi convention), introduce COLS/STRIDE, MAXLINES, MAXLEN, TEXTROWS, STATUSROW, CMDMAX and use them throughout.
- _low_ · clarity · opendown() 218, splitline() 238, insline() 295; docmd() 256 & getpat() 352 — The 'shift lines below down by one' loop is copy-pasted in three routines, and the raw-line editor (prompt, backspace-erase, k<38 accumulate, Esc-cancel) is duplicated between docmd and getpat. Divergence risk on future edits. → Extract a shiftdown(from) helper for the line-insert loop and a readline(prompt, buf, max) helper shared by ':' and '/' entry.
- _low_ · clarity · undo() line 319 — undo() unconditionally sets dirty=1, so undoing the single change that made the file dirty leaves it flagged as modified. → Minor: this is hard to track precisely with single-level undo, but consider clearing dirty when undo returns the buffer to a just-saved state, or at least note the limitation.

**Docs:** Above-average for a homebrew project: a thorough file header explains the buffer model, the mode map, the undo scheme, and even the p8cc subset limits; most routines carry a one-line purpose comment and the tricky spots (FGETB EOF bit, trailing-line counting in load, the shift-down loops) are annotated accurately. Two inaccuracies drag it down: line 364's comment claims empty '/' "repeats the last pattern" but the code actually nulls pat (see findings), and the header (line 27) says lines are truncated at 78 chars while load() actually admits 79 (col<79) and inschar caps at 78 — the two limits disagree and neither matches the doc. No per-routine parameter/return docs, but for this size that's acceptable.

**Strengths:** Excellent, unusually complete file header documenting the buffer model, key map, undo scheme, BIOS call table, and the p8cc subset constraints the code must obey.; Fixed buffers are consistently bounds-guarded: nlines>=110 checks before every structural insert (opendown/splitline/insline/load), inschar caps line growth, and col<79 caps load — no obvious overrun.; The op-based single-level undo is a genuinely clever fit for a memory-tight target: it costs one 80-byte saved line instead of a whole second buffer, and correctly distinguishes restore/insert/delete cases.; matchat() relies on the line's NUL terminator to bound the compare, so substring matching stays within the slot naturally.; Deliberate, well-reasoned redraw strategy (cursor move = reposition, char edit = one row, structural change = full repaint) that is appropriate for real serial baud and documented as such.; Clean adherence to the p8cc subset: declarations at function top, no ++/--, no break/continue, helpers defined before use.

**Top improvements:** Call scroll() before redraw() in the '/' and 'n' search handlers so off-screen matches actually scroll into view and the cursor lands correctly.; Fix the empty-'/' repeat: don't overwrite pat when the entered pattern is empty, so a bare '/' repeats the last search as the comment promises.; Fix undo of 'dd' on the last remaining line so it doesn't leave a duplicate blank line.; Replace the scattered magic numbers (80/110/78/22/23/24/38) with //#define named constants and de-duplicate the shift-down and raw-line-editor code.


### `os/commands/wc.c`  
**Code: A  ·  Docs: A**  ·  C (P8CC SUBSET)

A `wc` clone: reads a file, glob (concatenated), or stdin via the shared lib_stdin helper and prints "lines words bytes" using three 24-bit little-endian counters.

This is a clean, careful implementation. The 24-bit counter machinery (inc24/dm10/nz24/put24) is correct, all fixed arrays are safely bounded, char sign-extension is consistently defended against with `& 255`, and the word/line scanning matches standard `wc` semantics. I found no correctness or buffer-safety defects — only minor efficiency and stylistic nits. The documentation is unusually strong and accurate.

**Findings:**

- _low_ · efficiency · dm10 (lines 41-42) — cur/10 is computed twice — once for the quotient stored in num24[i], once inside the remainder expression `cur - (cur/10)*10`. Minor redundant work in the hot per-print divmod loop. → Compute the quotient once: `q = cur / 10; num24[i] = q; rem = cur - q * 10;`. Same result, one fewer division per byte.
- _low_ · clarity · put24 (lines 78,81) and main (lines 117,119,120) — ASCII codes are used as bare magic numbers: 48 for '0', and 65535/10/32/9/13 in the scan loop. The file elsewhere uses char literals ('-','h','H'), so the style is inconsistent. The values are explained in comments, so this is purely readability. → Where the p8cc subset supports them, prefer `'0'`, `'\n'`, `'\t'`, `'\r'`, `' '`; otherwise keep the numeric codes but the inline comment on line 92 already documents them, which is adequate.
- _low_ · correctness · inc24 (line 66) — On full 24-bit overflow, `p[2] = (p[2] & 255) + 1` truncates 256 to 0 when stored in a char, wrapping the counter silently. This is the documented 16MB limit, not a real defect, and is unverified against exact p8cc char-store truncation behavior. → No action needed for the stated file-size ceiling; optionally note the silent wrap in the header for completeness.

**Docs:** Excellent. A thorough file header explains the invocation forms, the stdin-filter design, the 24-bit rationale (16-bit int limitation), and the EOF/console behavior. Every helper has an accurate one-line contract, and non-obvious points are justified in-line: the countdown loop (unsigned `>= 0` caveat), the `cur < 2560` bound in dm10, and "v is 1..256, never negative" in inc24 all match the code exactly. I found no misleading or stale comments. The only thing arguably missing is a note that a 24-bit overflow (files >16MB) wraps silently rather than saturating, but the header does state the 16MB ceiling.

**Strengths:** Consistent, disciplined `& 255` masking everywhere a char is read, correctly neutralizing possible signed-char extension in the p8cc subset.; All fixed buffers are provably in-bounds: max 24-bit value is 8 decimal digits into dg[10], and num24/counter arrays are always indexed 0..2.; dm10's intermediate `cur` is bounded (rem<=9, so cur<2560) well within 16-bit int range, so the long division cannot overflow — and this is called out in a comment.; Correct handling of the zero case in put24 (emits one '0' digit before the nz24 loop) and correct standard wc word/line semantics.; Control flow is clean and readable despite the no-break/no-continue constraint; counter initialization and the scan loop are straightforward.

**Top improvements:** Hoist the quotient in dm10 to avoid computing cur/10 twice per byte.; Replace bare ASCII magic numbers with char/escape literals where the compiler allows, for consistency with the char literals already used in the arg parsing.
